BP · BP-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 26 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: BP

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 46,175 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment is unusually favourable for BP's near-term earnings and cash, and BP is the European major with the most leverage to it, through refining capture, trading and financial gearing. That favour is cyclical, geopolitically driven and short in visibility; the underlying structural forces are flat demand, rising supplier costs, hostile UK and EU fiscal policy and an LNG supply wave, a mild net headwind. For the Consumer, the external picture means: a very strong FY2026 already largely in consensus; an FY2027 that depends on how long the dislocation lasts; and an FY2028 that should be modelled at a normal deck with no help from the environment. The skew of paths over 12 months is to the downside for prices and margins, even if not for the balance sheet, which the windfall is repairing now.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0248C-0540C-0745C-0952C-3555C-3938
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C-486th of 13

Layer 1 · External change forces

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

Judgement: competitive intensity in BP's core upstream is stable and benign because the whole industry is under capital discipline; it is rising in the two areas that drove the 2026 beat (LNG, from 2027, and refining, once Middle East capacity returns and new Asian capacity arrives), and rising in the contest for investors' capital, where BP is losing.

  • Upstream: the sector moved from production growth to free cash flow maximisation; reinvestment rates far below history cap industry growth at 0-5%. Majors guide "value over volume". This is a supportive, stable structure.
  • LNG: US and Qatari capacity additions (the US and Qatar projected at 57% of global capacity by 2031) intensify competition for uncontracted cargoes from 2027; BP's 23 mtpa long-term book plus 10 mtpa of short-term volumes is exposed at the margin.
  • Refining: currently tight, but new large refineries in developing markets and the return of Gulf capacity are the standard reversal mechanism; BP's own risk report rates a collapse in refined product margins as high plausibility.
  • Trading: volatility rewards scale players. BP and Shell monetised 2026 volatility; TotalEnergies suffered an $800m gas trading swing. Trading houses (Vitol, Trafigura) and the new Eni-Mercuria joint venture compete for the same optimisation margin.
  • Capital markets: Shell runs $3bn a quarter of buybacks at 18.7% gearing, TotalEnergies up to $1.5bn a quarter at 13.1%, Eni €3.4bn for 2026; BP's buyback is suspended. BP competes worst for the marginal investor.

For the model: no change to upstream unit margins from competition; assume refining and LNG trading margins compete back toward normal from 2027.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

F4010th of 13

Layer 1 · External change forces

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

Judgement: a clear, moderate headwind that is structural through 2028: oilfield service, rig and subsea costs are rising in highly concentrated supply markets, and the weaker dollar inflates BP's sterling and euro cost base; the effect is to absorb a large share of BP's gross cost savings and to push capital spending to the top of its frame.

  • Services: US well costs forecast up 2.5-7% in 2026; deepwater drillship day rates at $400,000-650,000 and expected near $525,000 or more by 2028; offshore support vessel rates up 10-20% at utilisation above 90%; subsea tree awards about 98% concentrated in three suppliers (TechnipFMC, SLB OneSubsea, Baker Hughes); component lead times up to two years.
  • Evidence of impact at BP: about $3bn of cumulative gross savings absorbed by inflation and activity choices against the 2023 baseline; 2026 capital expenditure guidance raised to $13.5-14.0bn from $13.0-13.5bn (BP attributes this to delayed Paleogene farm-downs rather than inflation).
  • Currency: a 10% sterling appreciation compresses underlying EBITDA by about $300m (North Sea and UK operating costs); forecasters see gradual euro and sterling appreciation against the dollar over 12-24 months.
  • Mitigants: Palantir-driven procurement (3,200 contractors exited, 1,200 more planned), standardised subsea equipment (56% lower subsea costs), sourcing from China, long-term alliances (TechnipFMC on Tiber, Subsea 7, Worley).

For the model: assume 3-5% annual cost inflation on the upstream and capital lines; do not let gross structural savings flow 1:1 into operating cost; keep capital expenditure at about $14bn a year.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

C+453rd of 13

Layer 1 · External change forces

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

Judgement: the macro force that matters is the oil and product price set by geopolitics and OPEC+; it acts on the whole industry and sector, but it bites hardest at the company level for BP, because BP has the highest leverage, the highest dividend breakeven and the largest refining and trading gearing among European peers; rates and currency are secondary but adverse.

  • Oil and products: Brent $1/bbl moves BP's pre-tax operating profit by $340m and the refining indicator margin $1/bbl by $450m. BP's dividend breakeven is $60-65/bbl (Redburn) against peers below $50 (TotalEnergies, Shell). Its equity therefore carries more of the price swing per unit of asset value.
  • Rates: the US 10-year near 5.10% with a December Fed hike priced, the Bank of England heading to 4.00%; 29% of BP's finance debt is floating at a weighted 5.00% rate, so higher-for-longer delays interest savings from deleveraging.
  • Currency: a weakening dollar inflates unhedged sterling and euro costs (C-05).
  • Growth: the UK budget office cut 2027 UK growth to 1.0%; European industrial weakness depresses petrochemical demand; stagflation keeps central banks tight.
  • Regime: the house view is risk-off, with Energy Overweight but Momentum Underweight and Fragility Double Underweight; complex, levered cases are the ones being sold.

For the model: run every forecast on at least three decks ($60, $70-75, $90 Brent with matching refining margins) and attach the balance sheet and distribution outcome to each.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–52

Layer 1 · External change forces

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

Judgement: four other dynamics are material: activist and merger pressure (positive optionality), sector consolidation in which BP is a plausible target, the risk-off market regime that punishes complex levered cases, and the long tail of legal liabilities.

  • Activism and control: Elliott Management holds about 5% (about $4bn), and has secured much of its agenda (two segments, strategy chief removed, Castrol sale, cost cuts). Its stated goal was $20bn of annual free cash flow by 2027.
  • Consolidation: Shell bought ARC Resources; Bernstein reported Shell's head of M&A resigned after Wael Sawan rejected a BP approach; Elliott built an £850m short in Shell as a hedge. BP's sub-scale position and uncompetitive distributions make it a plausible target, though a Shell-BP combination faces heavy antitrust remedies.
  • Market regime: risk-off and broadening; energy positioning at the 69th percentile; the Fitness for Fighting tilt rates fragility Double Underweight, and BP, with eight concurrent change items and 61 countries, is a fragile case.
  • Legal tail: Gulf of America payables and provisions of $5.88bn (Q2 2026) with about $1.1-1.6bn a year of pre-tax payments; climate litigation; the Kaskida lawsuit.
  • Geopolitical volumes: about 75% of BP's 400,000 boe/d of Middle East production exports outside the Strait of Hormuz, which limits physical volume loss while BP collects the price gain.

For the model: include the Gulf of America cash payments below operating cash flow; carry M&A as upside optionality only.

C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D557th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Judgement: entry threats are low in BP's upstream core (capital and access barriers are very high), moderate in LNG and trading (US developers, NOCs and trading houses), and moderate in retail and EV charging (convenience and utility players); none threatens the model before 2028.

  • Upstream: barriers include deepwater capital needs, proprietary seismic, government concessions; the active private operator base is shrinking.
  • Adjacent entrants: NOCs expanding (ADNOC's $150bn 2026-2030 capital plan, ADNOC Drilling); US LNG developers selling spot; trading houses and NOC-trader joint ventures (Eni-Mercuria) competing for optimisation margin.
  • Orthogonal entrants: EV charging operators, utilities and convenience chains in retail; AI-driven commodity trading.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F3812th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

Judgement: suppliers are a source of risk: they are concentrated, nearly fully utilised, gaining pricing power and quoting long lead times; BP mitigates through alliances, standardisation and digital procurement, but cannot stop inflation absorbing a large part of its savings or schedule risk on deepwater projects.

  • Evidence: subsea tree awards about 98% with three suppliers; Subsea7's backlog of $13.6bn and 24% margins; Baker Hughes' $37.1bn backlog; Transocean's Deepwater Atlas above $650,000 a day; no new floating rigs under construction, so deepwater rigs stay tight through 2028.
  • BP's mitigants: TechnipFMC's $600-800m Tiber contract modelled on Kaskida; 56% lower subsea cost from off-the-shelf equipment; Palantir procurement; rigs contracted early (Deepwater Mykonos for Brazil at $397,000 a day).
  • Support: suppliers' financial strength lowers counterparty risk on BP's major projects.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0152C-0360C-0450C-0860C-3252C-3748C-4345
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F5212th of 13

Layer 1 · External change forces

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility?

Judgement: demand for BP's products is not changing much structurally over the horizon; what has changed dramatically is the price of what BP sells, through a supply shock rather than a demand shock, and its visibility is short, well under two years, so it does not qualify as a visible cyclical swing.

  • Oil. BP's own outlook has global oil demand growing to about 2030 and peaking near 103 mb/d; emerging markets add 0.74 mb/d against OECD declines of 0.04 mb/d. This is slow, structural, low-single-digit change. The 2026 move is supply-driven: the Strait of Hormuz disruption took spot Brent from $81.13 (Q1 2026 average) to $103.85 (Q2) and to $111 on 22 October 2026; global oil inventories fell 507m barrels since February. Demand is the balancing force: brokers warn that Brent sustained above $125 would push economies toward recession, and high prices are expected to cut global diesel demand by about 2.5 mb/d in 2026.
  • Refined products. The real 2026 demand-side tailwind sits here. Middle distillate tightness, Middle East refinery outages and product-export disruption drove BP's refining indicator margin to $29.60/bbl in Q2 2026 against $11.90 a year earlier and BP's planning baseline of $10.80. Retail customers are absorbing high pump prices with fewer gallons per visit (ARKO), and European retail fuel demand is in structural decline of about 2-3% a year (Jefferies), slowed by weaker electric vehicle adoption (global electric vehicle sales up only 4% year to date against 10% expected).
  • Gas and LNG. Structural growth is real (global LNG demand about 6% a year to 2030, data centres adding power demand) but a supply wave of more than 150 mtpa of liquefaction from 2026 to 2028 turns LNG into a buyer's market; Bernstein expects spot LNG to average $9/mmbtu over 2026-28 against $12 in 2025.
  • Visibility. The windfall rests on a geopolitical disruption that could reverse in weeks (a reopened Strait and a peace would release stranded crude and LNG). Consensus itself has revenue falling 16% in FY2027.

For the model: treat volumes as structurally flat to slightly rising, and treat the 2026 price and margin environment as a temporary state with a normalisation path starting in 2027, timed to the geopolitical resolution, not to demand.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

C604th of 13

Layer 1 · External change forces

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

Judgement: the energy-transition disruption is being partly disproved on the timescale that matters for the model, which acts in BP's favour now because BP has just re-concentrated on hydrocarbons; digital and artificial intelligence (AI) tools are a modest, real tailwind to costs and reliability; the long-term disruption to retail fuel volumes is intact.

  • Disproved or delayed: green hydrogen costs ($2.50-6.80/kg) remain above the $2.00/kg parity threshold; renewable power returns of 6-7% sit below the 10-15%+ of oil and gas; electric vehicle adoption is slower than expected and European charger additions collapsed to 6,000 in Q3 2026 against a five-year quarterly average of 45,000; hybrid sales are surging. This validates the February 2025 reset (transition capex cut 75% to under $0.80bn a year; the 50 GW target retired) and removes a capital drain.
  • Strengthening and favourable: AI in subsurface work and maintenance (Palantir, Databricks, Nvidia; 90% cut in well-planning time in Azerbaijan; remote-operated platforms) supports the cost programme and could help recover plant reliability.
  • Still acting against BP: transport electrification erodes European retail fuel volumes over a decade; BP has the thinnest low-carbon optionality among European peers if policy or technology accelerates (stranded-asset and multiple-compression risk post-2030).

For the model: no disruption-driven volume losses to 2030 beyond a gentle European retail decline; credit some AI-assisted cost savings inside the guided programme, not on top of it.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

D509th of 13

Layer 1 · External change forces

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

Judgement: significant change is under way and it splits by geography: the United States has turned clearly favourable to BP's growth basins, the UK and the EU remain hostile, and the risk of new windfall taxes rises with every dollar of Brent above $100.

  • United States (favourable, BP's top growth country): Gulf lease sales resumed with BP a top winner; federal offshore royalty cut to 12.5% from 16.67%, raising net present values for Kaskida and Tiber. Against it: Earthjustice's April 2026 lawsuit to revoke approval of the $5.0bn Kaskida project (80,000 b/d).
  • United Kingdom (hostile): the Energy Profits Levy keeps a 78% headline marginal rate to March 2030 with the main investment allowance abolished, which triggered a $539m deferred tax charge and drove the decision to sell the whole North Sea business. The 28 October 2026 Budget made no change. A permanent successor (a conditional 35% rate above $90 Brent or 90p/therm) is drafted.
  • European Union (tightening): Emissions Trading System free allowances phasing out from 2026, methane rules, the Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism; these raise costs at BP's remaining European refineries.
  • Windfall-tax risk: at $111 Brent with stagflationary household pressure in the UK and Europe, a new levy is plausible; 2022-23 windfall taxes absorbed about 5% of European majors' free cash flow.
  • Host-government and sanctions: favourable recent actions (OFAC licence for Manakin-Cocuina, Egyptian concession awards, Calypso consolidation in Trinidad, BTC operatorship transfer in Azerbaijan); Hormuz-related shut-ins (Rumaila).

For the model: a falling group tax rate as the UK exits the mix (from the 35-40% guided underlying rate) is a modest positive; carry a scenario for a new European or UK windfall levy while Brent stays above $90.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F6010th of 13

Layer 1 · External change forces

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

Judgement: two sector drivers are accelerating BP's earnings more than any peer's, but they accelerate price and margin, not demand volumes: the supply-shock margin environment (refining and trading) and the sector-wide swing of capital and investor preference back to hydrocarbon cash returns.

  • Margin driver: BP captured 64% of global refining margin markers in the first half of 2026 against Shell's 55% and TotalEnergies' 35% (Jefferies), on the highest refining complexity among the majors and the largest US distillation capacity among European majors. Trading added about 4 percentage points to return on capital over six years and was "exceptional" in Q1 2026.
  • Sector re-rating driver: the sector digest names "the capital swing back to barrels" and "balance sheets at record lows" as big live stories; European majors trade at 9.3 times 2027 earnings against 14.2 for US majors. BP is the late, levered participant: as the last major to pivot, it gains most from the removal of the transition discount.
  • Volume driver: LNG and AI-driven power demand support gas, but BP's volume response is limited.

For the model: the sector drivers lift BP's realisations and capture rates in 2026-27; they do not lift volumes. Model capture as reverting toward the historical average when indicator margins normalise.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F5212th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement: customer demand supports the near-term thesis because product markets are tight and long-term contracts are holding, but the support is price-driven and fragile; signs of price resistance in retail and weakness in petrochemicals are already visible.

  • Supportive: marketing sales of 3,163 mb/d in Q2 2026 (up from 3,030 in Q1); Air bp's multi-year Airbus jet fuel and sustainable aviation fuel deal; 23 mtpa of long-term LNG offtake including Kogas (two contracts) and a 20-year China contract; take-or-pay style stability; US Gulf Coast industrial gas demand growing.
  • Fragile: retail customers buy fewer gallons per visit at high prices; convenience spending under pressure; petrochemical buyers delaying orders; European industrial gas demand hit by high energy costs; airline fare discounting at high fuel prices.
  • BP-specific: earnify loyalty (8m active US members) and co-located fast-charging hubs help retention, but BP is exiting 10% of company-owned sites by 2027.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

D4810th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

Judgement: regulators help BP in the United States and hurt it in the UK and the EU; on balance the position has improved because BP is moving capital to the helpful jurisdiction, but it is fragile to a US political swing after 2028, a European windfall tax and adverse litigation.

  • Helpful: US lease sales, royalty cut, permitting support; host governments awarding concessions (Egypt) and licences (OFAC for Manakin-Cocuina).
  • Hurtful: the UK's 78% marginal rate to 2030; EU carbon and methane rules; free-allowance phase-out; Canada's emissions cap.
  • Fragile: Kaskida lawsuit; US policy after the 2028 election; sanctions regimes around Shah Deniz partners (Lukoil, NICO) and Venezuela.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F4511th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

Judgement: the addressable market is enormous relative to BP and is not the constraint; BP's growth is constrained by resource access, capital and balance sheet, so the realistic mid-term growth rate is the company's own, about 0-2% a year in volumes, with price setting the revenue path.

  • Market: global oil demand about 103-106 mb/d peaking around 2030-35; gas at a record 4.3 tcm in 2025; LNG growing about 6% a year to 2030; refining and retail flat to declining in Europe.
  • BP: about 2-3% of global hydrocarbons; production guided to 2.3-2.5 mboe/d by 2030 from 2.31 in 2025, a compound rate of 0-2%.
  • Implication for the model: revenue growth over 2027-29 is the deck plus or minus perimeter changes; consensus revenue is flat from FY2027 to FY2029 ($199.7bn, $199.7bn, $200.0bn).
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0635C-1055
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

F3513th of 13

Layer 1 · External change forces

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

Judgement: BP's revenue and earnings cycle is at or very close to its peak, driven by price and margin; the turn is not yet visible in the data but is fully expected by consensus from 2027, and its timing depends on geopolitics, not on BP.

  • Revenue rose 48% year on year in Q2 2026 to $69.1bn; FY2026 consensus revenue of $237.9bn approaches the FY2022 peak of $241.4bn; consensus FY2026 EBIT of $38.5bn is about 93% of FY2022's $41.2bn.
  • Volumes are not cycling up: reported production was 2,201 kboe/d in Q2 2026, down 6% from Q1 on Gulf maintenance and about 100 kboe/d of Middle East disruption; underlying production is guided broadly flat for 2026-27.
  • Leading indicators still point up in the very near term: Brent $111 (22 October); a "very strong" Q3 2026 trading update (Jefferies, 13 October); FY2026 consensus EPS up 23% in one month.
  • The turn: consensus has revenue falling 16% and EPS 37% in FY2027, and EPS a further 22% in FY2028, the steepest fade in the peer set.

For the model: FY2026 is the peak year; model 2027 as the first normalisation year with a range of paths (fast reversal on peace, slow reversal on persistent disruption), and 2028 at or near the normalised deck.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F5510th of 13

Layer 1 · External change forces

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

Judgement: today the external tailwinds are strong and the headwinds moderate, but the tailwinds are cyclical and fragile while the headwinds are structural; over the next six months the external picture is clearly favourable, over 12-24 months the skew of paths turns negative as margins and prices normalise.

Sector themes, one by one (sector digest, 10 October 2026):

  • The capital swing back to barrels: a tailwind for BP's multiple (removes the transition discount); strong; already partly priced (BP up 39.5% over 12 months).
  • Balance sheets at record lows: a relative headwind for BP, whose balance sheet is the exception; turns into a tailwind only once BP's repair completes at end-2026.
  • Guiding low and beating: a tailwind; BP's $80 planning deck against $111 spot makes consensus a floor for 2026.
  • The 2027 gas wave: a headwind to BP's LNG trading and gas realisations from 2027.
  • Self-funded regional platforms: indirect (BP is a seller of mature basins into such platforms, which supports disposal prices).
  • Industry theme, "nobody is building": a headwind for BP as a buyer of rigs, vessels and subsea kit (C-05).

Value chain: demand supportive near term; suppliers adverse; regulators split (US positive, UK/EU negative); competitors benign upstream, intensifying in LNG and refining from 2027.

Overall: the strongest external force is a supply-shock price and margin environment that lifts BP more than peers; it is the least durable force. The most durable forces (cost inflation, hostile European fiscal regimes, LNG oversupply, refining capacity additions) are mild to moderate headwinds.

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

BP's internal change is the strongest positive force in the whole case: a complete leadership reset to an external operator, decisive portfolio shrinkage, a twice-raised cost target and an accelerated balance-sheet repair, all backed by an activist that wants the same things. The weakness is that the change is so large and so simultaneous that execution risk is high, and that its measurable successes so far are partly bought by the commodity windfall and by disposals that remove earnings. For the Consumer the key internal inputs are: the cost path (gross savings with leakage), the perimeter (remove Castrol, Gelsenkirchen, North Sea earnings), the financing path (lower net debt and hybrids), and the distribution reset (buyback restart in 2027, sized by the 15 December framework). The change forces lift normalised earnings modestly; they do not offset the normalisation of prices and margins.

Internal risks

Group judgement

Risks

F3411th of 13

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is the weakest part of the case after earnings quality. BP combines the sector's largest exposure to a reversal of the 2026 dislocation (refining capture, trading, financial gearing) with a price near its 52-week high and a forward multiple near the top of its five-year range; the most plausible sudden risk, a peace that reopens Hormuz, is medium-to-high probability over 18 months, not a tail. The downside to a fair trough is about one third in a normalisation and about one half in a downturn. For the Consumer this means: every forecast needs a downside deck with its balance-sheet and distribution consequences; the end-2026 balance sheet (net debt near $10bn on broker estimates) is the buffer that makes the downside survivable without a dividend cut, which it would not have been a year ago.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

F5711th of 13

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement: standards are improving clearly in capital and cost discipline and in governance intent, but operational execution standards have slipped in 2026 and the culture is under strain; the direction is up, the level is still mid-to-low among peers.

  • Improving: FY2025 organic capital expenditure of $13.6bn under its original guide; Q2 2026 capex 8% below consensus; structural cost savings of $3.54bn since 2023, ahead of the original plan; central corporate costs cut 8% in 2025; process safety Tier 1 and 2 events down 29% to 27 in 2025; recordable injury rate down 21%; downward bonus discretion for fatalities; candid disclosure of shortfalls.
  • Slipping: upstream plant reliability fell from a record 96.1% in 2025 to 92.4% in Q2 2026 (North Sea and Indonesia outages); refining availability fell to 94.7% (Whiting electrical event) from 96.3%; four workforce fatalities in 2025 in US retail; cost cuts not yet reaching the bottom line; a $5/bbl gap between realised and indicator refining margins.
  • Governance: a chair removed for conduct; board downsized and refreshed; independent committees functioning.

For the model: assume plant reliability recovers toward 95% by 2027 (a volume gain of roughly 2-4% in upstream if it does), but treat the recovery as a test, not a given; the August memo made two consecutive quarters above 95% a release condition.

What negative corporate change developments are present or plausible?

Judgement: the negatives are concentrated in execution capacity and in earnings quality: a stretched organisation running about eight simultaneous change items, slipping reliability, cost savings that leak, impairments that keep recurring, and a disposal programme that sells earnings.

  • Execution overload: new chief executive, segment restructure, three to four live disposal processes, the cost programme, deleveraging, hybrid reduction, an unannounced buyback restart, and Bumerangue appraisal, against a house guardrail of two concurrent change items.
  • Operations: reliability 92.4% and refining availability 94.7% in Q2 2026; O'Neill conceded "plants did not run as expected".
  • People: workforce cut by over 5% (more than 4,700 employees and 3,000 contractors); severe senior technical talent attrition; engagement down to 64%; four fatalities in 2025; an interim Downstream head.
  • Earnings quality: $1bn of low-carbon impairments in Q2 2026; Jefferies flags $3.0-4.5bn of post-tax impairments guided in Gas and Low Carbon Energy; underlying profit exceeded IFRS profit by $1.8bn in Q2 2026.
  • Cash: capital expenditure guidance raised; Gulf of America payments; working capital built $7.0bn in the first half; Castrol cash flow lost.
  • Strategy: flat volumes; reserve life of 7.1 years; Kaskida litigation; a possible fire-sale risk if markets weaken before the North Sea and Archaea sales close.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

–42

Layer 3 · Risks

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

Judgement: the differences split into two layers with opposite concentration. The difference between FY2026-27 and the last four to six years is concentrated almost entirely in two external prices, the refining margin and Brent, with trading a distant third: those three explain about 95% of the EBITDA change against FY2025 and about $12.6bn of the $13.8bn fall from FY2026 to FY2027. The difference between the FY2028-30 plateau and FY2024-25 is diffuse and mechanical: EBITDA is roughly unchanged, and the 25-40% higher EPS comes from five below-EBIT lines (depreciation, finance cost, tax rate, hybrid coupons, share count), none of which contributes more than about 40% of the uplift. Concentration in two prices makes the next 18 months unpredictable; diffusion across five mechanical lines makes the plateau predictable. The Consumer should model and monitor them as two different problems.

Layer one: the 18-month window, FY2026 and FY2027 against FY2023-25 (brokers' basis, $bn):

FY2024 FY2025 FY2026e FY2027e
EBITDA 37.8 36.6 56.3 42.5
Change against FY2025 +19.7 +5.9
Of which: refining margin (RIM about $31 and $17 against $12.8; $450m per $1) about +8.2 about +1.9
Of which: Brent ($93.5 and $80 against $69; $340m per $1) about +8.3 about +3.7
Of which: trading above normal +1.9 +1.0
Of which: perimeter, cost efficiency, volume, gas, other about +1.3 about -0.7

Three readings:

  • The concentration is in the refining margin as much as in Brent, and refining is the more reversible of the two. BP's $450m per dollar of RIM on a margin that moved from $11.90 (Q2 2025) to $46.70 (Q3 2026) gives downstream a larger swing than upstream, from a smaller asset base. Jefferies (14 September 2026) puts refining and trading at 28% of BP's FY2026 EBITDA against a historical average of 9%; refining capture of 64-70% is the highest among European majors. This is why the quarterly beats of 2026 all came from Customers and Products, and why FY2027 is the most disruption-sensitive year in the peer set. Eni's estimates did not move when refining markers changed; BP's FY2026 net income moved 16% (Jefferies).
  • The concentration is also in timing. Within the 18 months, the Q2-Q3 2027 period carries the steepest assumed fall (H1 2027 Brent $85 and RIM $21.5; H2 $75 and $13). The quarterly inputs show revenue growth turning from +6% (Q1 2027) to -25% (Q2 2027). One quarter's slippage in the reopening moves FY2027 EBITDA by about $2.5-3bn.
  • What is not concentrated in the 18-month window is small. Perimeter (Castrol about $1.0bn, Gelsenkirchen $0.3bn, North Sea $1.3bn from Q4 2027), net cost efficiency (+$0.4bn) and the oil-indexed LNG lag (+$0.3-0.5bn in H1 2027) together move EBITDA by about $1-2bn, less than a $4 move in Brent.

Layer two: the plateau, FY2028-30 against FY2024-25:

Bridge from FY2025 to FY2028 (second set) $bn Share of the net income uplift
EBITDA (deck +0.7, cost +0.8, growth and mix +0.9, perimeter -2.7, reliability -0.2) -0.5
Lower depreciation (held for sale, impairments, disposals) +1.7 about 40% of the pre-tax uplift
Lower net finance cost (net debt $22bn to about $6bn) +1.4 about 35%
Lower tax rate (about 40% to 37%) +0.5 about 20%
Lower hybrid coupons and minorities +0.25 about 10%
Net income: $7.49bn to $9.8bn +2.3
Share count about 6% lower on average adds about 7 points to EPS

Readings:

  • Each mechanical line is modest, controllable and already partly visible. Net debt is at $13.87bn; hybrids at $12.1bn with a published path to $9bn; the depreciation guidance of $17.0-17.5bn already reflects held-for-sale treatment; the 2026 tax guidance has been cut to 31-34%. No single line can wreck the plateau; a miss on one is a few cents a share.
  • But the plateau's EBITDA ($35-36bn) is a deck assumption as much as FY2027's. At $70 Brent and $11 RIM it is unchanged from FY2024-25 despite $2.6bn of perimeter leaving. If the normal is $65 and $10, EBITDA is about $3bn lower and EPS about $0.10 lower. So the plateau is diffuse in its improvement and concentrated in its level.
  • A concentration the earlier sets under-stated: the depreciation line. About 40% of the FY2028 net income uplift is lower depreciation. That follows from $4.0bn of post-tax impairments in Q3 2026, about $6bn in 2025 and the held-for-sale treatment of Castrol and the North Sea. It is real for EPS and for return on capital, and it is zero for cash. The Consumer should present the plateau EPS alongside free cash flow, where the uplift against FY2025 ($8.7bn to $9.6bn after leases, hybrids and minorities) is smaller and more honest.

Against peers. The sector's drivers are the same two prices, but BP's weight on refining and trading is the highest among the European majors, and its below-EBIT improvement is the largest because it started with the worst balance sheet. Shell and TotalEnergies have diffuse drivers in both layers (lower refining weight, buybacks already running, LNG and growth volumes); Equinor is concentrated on European gas; Repsol is the only peer more geared to refining. So BP's forecast is more concentrated than its peers' in the 18-month window and more diffuse than theirs in the plateau's sources of EPS growth, which is the mirror image of what a predictable case looks like.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

B+662nd of 13

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders or other key actors changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement: change at the top is total and recent, and its signal is a decisive break from a finance-led insider culture toward an external operator under activist and board pressure; it raises the quality of the leadership's intent and decisiveness, but it also signals instability and a thin bench that will not be fully settled before 2027.

  • Chief executive: Meg O'Neill took office on 1 April 2026, the first external chief executive in BP's 116-year history: chemical engineer, 23 years at ExxonMobil, four years leading Woodside. She replaced Murray Auchincloss (left December 2025 after under two years) and interim chief executive Carol Howle. Three chief executive transitions in about two and a half years (Looney September 2023, Auchincloss December 2025).
  • Chair: Albert Manifold removed on 26 May 2026 after eight months for unacceptable governance and conduct; Ian Tyler made interim chair, then permanent chair on 2 September 2026 (MT Newswires; one source still calls him interim). Helge Lund left after a 75.72% re-election vote in April 2025.
  • Board: cut from 13 to 10 at the April 2026 AGM; five non-executive directors left within 12 months.
  • Executives: two-segment structure from 1 July 2026, Gordon Birrell (39-year veteran) runs Upstream; Richard Harding is interim head of Downstream with an external search; departures include Emma Delaney (to chief executive of OMV), William Lin, Giulia Chierchia (as Elliott demanded) and Kerry Dryburgh. CFO Kate Thomson (permanent since February 2024) is the continuity.
  • Key actors: Elliott Management about 5%; BlackRock 7.37%; Norges Bank 3.99%.

Signal: an operator with no emotional attachment to legacy assets, backed by an activist and a refreshed board; but the senior team is half-built, and repeated churn has eroded morale (engagement 64%, pride 73%).

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

C645th of 13

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement: the strategy has changed completely since 2020 and again sharpened in 2026; the choices are clear and resourced at the level of capital (upstream first, low-carbon capped, simplify, deleverage), but the strategy is not yet complete because the new chief executive has not yet set out growth, distribution and portfolio shape; that comes on 15 December 2026.

  • Choices made: the February 2025 reset reversed the 2020 plan to cut production 40% by 2030; transition capital was cut 75% (now under $0.80bn a year to 2027) and all volumetric transition targets retired; about 75% of the $13-15bn capital frame goes to upstream, with upstream investment up 20% to about $10bn a year at target returns above 20%.
  • 2026 sharpening under O'Neill: five priorities (balance sheet, portfolio high-grading, capital discipline, operational excellence, accountability); "every dollar of capital has to compete"; "no asset is sacred"; the United States as the top country; LNG and integrated gas as conviction areas.
  • Resourcing: capital is allocated and protected; the cost programme is staffed with tools (Palantir, Databricks); but people resourcing is stretched (workforce cut over 5%; senior technical attrition).
  • Gaps: no answer yet to flat volumes (2.3-2.5 mboe/d by 2030 against Shell's 4% and TotalEnergies' 3% growth), and no stated buyback trigger.

For the model: base capital spending on $13.5-14bn a year with upstream about three quarters of it; treat the 15 December event as a possible step change in the distribution assumptions.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

C644th of 13

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement: guidance has been raised on the items BP controls through portfolio and balance-sheet actions (the net debt timeline, the cost target), held on the mid-term return and cash targets, and lowered on capital spending and tax; the net effect is positive, but part of each upgrade is mechanical (portfolio-driven) rather than operational.

  • Raised: net debt target of $14-18bn pulled forward a full year to end-2026; structural cost target raised from $4-5bn to $5.5-6.5bn and then to $6.5-7.5bn by end-2027 (the last $1bn from the Gelsenkirchen sale); total financial obligations target of $39-41bn by end-2026; hybrid stack to about $9bn by end-2027.
  • Held: return on average capital employed above 16% by 2027 (at $70 Brent, $4 Henry Hub, $10.30 refining margin); adjusted free cash flow growth above 20% a year over 2024-2027; dividend growth of at least 4% a year.
  • Lowered or loosened: 2026 capital expenditure raised to $13.5-14.0bn; 2026 divestment proceeds expectation lowered to $8-9bn (cumulative $15-16bn by end-2026 kept); underlying tax rate guidance moved to 35-40%; depreciation guided to $17.0-17.5bn.
  • Coming: the 15 December 2026 New York strategy event, the first under O'Neill, is expected to set the buyback framework and long-term growth plans.

For the model: the cost target is a gross figure measured against a 2023 baseline and includes perimeter effects; do not treat it as an earnings increment.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

A761st of 13

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement: this is BP's strongest internal change force and among the strongest in the sector: a $20bn divestment programme, about $11bn done or signed, whole businesses and a whole basin being exited, and the segment structure halved; it simplifies the case and repairs the balance sheet, but it also removes earnings, including stable ones.

  • Done or signed: Castrol 65% to Stonepeak (about $6bn net proceeds, enterprise value about $10.1bn, about 8.6 times EBITDA; close expected late 2026); Gelsenkirchen refinery completed 31 July 2026 (removes about $1bn of operating cost and site liabilities); Netherlands retail sold; Austrian retail agreed; US onshore wind sold; bp Ventures holdings sold and the arm closing; Bay du Nord exited ($478m write-off); 5% of company-owned retail sites exited in 2025, 10% by 2027.
  • In process: the entire UK North Sea business (100 kboe/d, five hubs, risked value about $2.6bn); Archaea Energy (bought for $4.1bn in 2022); a partner for Lightsource bp; Kaskida and Tiber farm-downs deliberately delayed to maximise value.
  • Structure: three segments collapsed into Upstream and Downstream from 1 July 2026 (external reporting from 1 January 2027).
  • Cost of the actions: BP expects no income or dividends from its retained 35% of Castrol in the short to medium term because Stonepeak holds preferred distributions; the refining margin sensitivity falls from $550m to $450m per $1/bbl; Morgan Stanley cut FY2027-30 EPS by about 3% for Gelsenkirchen alone.

For the model: remove Castrol's EBIT (roughly $1bn a year of EBITDA on the deal multiple) from late 2026, the North Sea's production and costs from its sale date, and Gelsenkirchen's margin; credit the lower interest and the lower operating cost.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C-606th of 13

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly?

Judgement: BP is entering few genuinely new markets, and those it enters are sensible because they are extensions of existing positions, infrastructure or partnerships (Gulf of America leases, Brazil, Trinidad gas, Egypt, Namibia through acreage next to Azule's discoveries); it is simultaneously exiting new low-carbon product markets it entered badly in 2020-2023.

  • Entries: 60% operated interest in three Namibian Walvis Basin blocks (April 2026), next to Azule Energy's Orange Basin discoveries; Calypso in Trinidad taken to 100% to feed BP's 45%-owned Atlantic LNG; Manakin-Cocuina cross-border gas under a US OFAC licence (20% of the Venezuelan side sold to Trinidad's NGC); new Egyptian concessions (100% North-East El Alamein, 25% West El Hammad); Gulf of America lease wins; Bumerangue appraisal in Brazil's Santos Basin; Azule's $5.1bn Greater PAJ project in Angola (first oil 2028, 95 kboe/d gross).
  • Exits from earlier entries: offshore wind into the 50:50 JERA Nex joint venture (Morgan project scrapped, $0.7bn impairment), US onshore wind, hydrogen projects cut from 30 to 5-7, Archaea biogas, EV charging narrowed to four markets.
  • Sensibleness: entries use existing infrastructure (lower cost, faster payback) and non-operated joint ventures that keep capital off BP's balance sheet. The risk is geographic: more concentration in deepwater US Gulf (litigation), the Middle East and North Africa.

For the model: no material revenue from new markets before 2028; Bumerangue and Namibia are post-2030 options with appraisal and capital calls in 2027-29.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F3213th of 13

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?

Judgement: BP is not raising prices in real terms by its own action; its realisations are rising because commodity and product markets are rising, which is nominal, external and reversible; the only businesses with genuine pricing levers (Castrol lubricants, convenience retail) are being sold or are small.

  • Realised liquids price rose to $85.14/bbl in Q2 2026 from $60.16 a year earlier; this is Brent, not pricing power. Realised refining margins ran up to $5/bbl below the indicator because of feedstock and freight costs, so BP gave back part of the market move.
  • Castrol is the one demonstrated pricing-power business (premium brand, pass-through of base-oil costs), but it lost 1-2% market share after unhedged price rises in 2011, management flagged a lower Q3 2026 Castrol result on lagged base-oil costs, and 65% of it is being sold.
  • Retail: higher fuel margins sequentially in Q2 2026 but visible consumer resistance (fewer gallons per visit); UK retail energy price caps; long-term industrial contracts cap escalation.
  • LNG: contracts are largely oil-indexed with lags (oil-linked gas often follows oil with about a nine-month lag), so price changes flow mechanically, not by negotiation.

For the model: price is an exogenous deck input; there is no company-driven real price contribution to revenue or margin.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

D508th of 13

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they?

Judgement: BP is not stepping up acquisitions; it is a net seller with small, infrastructure-led bolt-ons, which is the disciplined choice for its balance sheet; its record on larger acquisitions in the last five years is poor, and the larger M&A question for the case is whether BP becomes a target.

  • Recent bolt-ons: Calypso (70% from Woodside), Namibian blocks, Lightsource bp consolidated to 100% earlier, TravelCenters of America (2023; four fatalities in 2025 at TA and Thorntons).
  • Record of value destruction: Archaea Energy bought for $4.1bn in 2022, about 12 months behind schedule and written down; a $1.1bn US offshore wind write-off; about $6bn of low-carbon impairments in 2025 including about $2bn of goodwill on biogas and solar.
  • Discipline now: "value over speed" in disposals; Bay du Nord exit; Kaskida and Tiber farm-downs delayed for value; O'Neill's Woodside record includes a large merger, which adds optionality.
  • Target status: sub-scale, discounted and highly geared relative to peers; Shell reportedly rejected an approach; Elliott hedged with a Shell short.

For the model: no acquisitions in the base case; disposals continue to the $20bn target.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

C+624th of 13

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet, or stepping up shareholder returns, or is management increasingly mooting either?

Judgement: today BP is doing the opposite (buybacks suspended since February 2026, all surplus cash to debt and hybrids), but the conditions for a restart are being met at speed by the windfall, management is increasingly mooting it, and a resumption in 2027 is the most likely and the most important change in shareholder returns; its size is the open question.

  • Now: dividend of 8.66 cents per quarter (+4% year on year; about $5.3bn a year); no repurchases since the Q4 2025 results; the old 30-40% of operating cash flow distribution guidance retired; €2.5bn of hybrids redeemed in Q2 2026, $1.0bn more in Q3, £1.25bn due March 2027.
  • Conditions for a restart: net debt excluding leases of $22.25bn at Q2 2026 heading into or below the $14-18bn target by end-2026 (brokers $6.3-10.2bn); total financial obligations from $60.5bn (Q1) to $53.6bn (Q2), guided to $39-41bn by end-2026.
  • Mooting: Morgan Stanley expects buybacks to resume by 2027; the market expects distribution plans at the 15 December 2026 event; but O'Neill said $40bn of total liabilities "remains too high for optimal resilience in a low-price environment", which argues for a cautious restart.
  • Peer bar: Shell 40-50% of operating cash flow and $3bn a quarter; TotalEnergies above 40%; Eni 35-45% with a €3.4bn 2026 buyback.

For the model: base case a restart in early 2027 at a framework expressed as a share of operating cash flow; capacity at a normal deck after the dividend, leases, hybrid coupons and Gulf of America payments is modest (C-52), so a large 2027 buyback would partly spend the 2026 windfall.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–64

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

What other material positive corporate change forces are present or plausible?

Judgement: the main other positives are an upstream project pipeline that offsets decline to 2027, a long-dated resource option in Brazil, a structurally falling cost of capital stack, and a falling tax rate as the UK North Sea leaves the mix.

  • Projects: 10 major start-ups delivering about 250 kboe/d of peak production through 2027; seven start-ups in 2025, five ahead of schedule; Kaskida and Tiber-Guadalupe (about 600m barrels) on schedule for 2029-30; Tangguh UCC; Aker BP's Yggdrasil (BP is a shareholder of Aker BP) on track for 2027.
  • BPX Energy: 545 kboe/d in Q2 2026, up 16% year on year; record Haynesville wells (81 mmcf/d initial rate); short-cycle capital that can be cut or raised quickly.
  • Bumerangue: about 8 billion barrels of liquids in place; appraisal from late 2026 or early 2027; high CO2 content complicates development; a post-2030 option, not a model input before then.
  • Financing stack: hybrids to about $9bn, net debt to below target, lower lease and coupon drag.
  • Tax: exiting the 78% UK regime lowers the group rate; lower US royalties on new leases.
  • Digital: AI-led maintenance and procurement; Palantir platform.

For the model: use project start-ups to hold upstream volumes flat to slightly up to 2028 against base decline; give no value to Bumerangue in the 2027-30 earnings.

C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

B603rd of 13

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy or approach?

Judgement: the key actor, Elliott, has already obtained most of what it publicly asked for, and its remaining agenda (higher free cash flow, deeper cost cuts, more disposals, a return to buybacks) points the same way as management's; climate activists push in the opposite direction but have lost influence; there is no controlling shareholder.

  • Elliott (about 5%): asked for the strategy chief's removal (done), separate accountability for upstream and downstream (done, two segments), Castrol's sale (done), hundreds of head-office job cuts, about $5bn of additional structural cost cuts, and $20bn of annual free cash flow by 2027; it pressed the new chair to fix high costs and chronic underperformance.
  • Other holders: BlackRock 7.37%, Norges Bank 3.99%; no dual-class structure; one share, one vote.
  • Climate investors: Follow This, ACCR and pension funds challenged the pivot; Follow This's resolution was blocked in 2026; their influence on capital allocation is now small.
  • Signal: the activist channel is live and aligned with shareholder returns; a sale of Elliott's stake once the turnaround is "done" would be a sentiment risk.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C665th of 13

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

Judgement: the corporate change is very strong and very broad, among the largest agendas in the European sector, and it is well resourced in capital and in board and activist backing; it is under-resourced in people and management bandwidth, and its outputs so far are financial (debt, costs, disposals) rather than operational (reliability, volumes).

  • Strength: new external operator chief executive; a new chair; a halved segment structure; a $20bn disposal programme about 55% done; a cost target raised twice; the net debt target pulled forward a year.
  • Breadth: covers leadership, strategy, structure, portfolio, capital allocation, costs and incentives (the 2026 bonus is 65% financial, 20% operations, 15% safety; long-term pay tied to four financial targets through 2027).
  • Resourcing: capital and balance sheet now well supported by the windfall; people and execution capacity stretched (C-86).
  • Proof still missing: costs reaching earnings; reliability back above 95%; disposals closing at fair prices; buyback restart.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

C+624th of 13

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

Judgement: the staircase is unusually well populated for the next six months, centred on the balance-sheet milestone and the first buyback framework under the new chief executive; months 6 to 18 depend on the self-help programme converting into reported profit; months 18 to 36 depend on deepwater start-ups and Brazil. The near steps are well founded; the later ones are progressively less so.

Next 6 months (to about April 2027):

  • 30 October 2026, Q3 2026 results: net debt and working capital release after a "very strong" trading update. Novelty medium; well founded.
  • Late 2026: Castrol sale closing (about $6bn); net debt inside or below the $14-18bn target; total obligations at $39-41bn. Novelty medium (expected); well founded.
  • 15 December 2026, New York strategy event: the new chief executive's first frame, expected to set the distribution framework, the buyback restart and growth plans. Novelty high; moderately well founded (the conditions are met; size uncertain).
  • February 2027, FY2026 results: the first buyback tranche (if announced); first two-segment reporting. Novelty high; moderately well founded.
  • March 2027: £1.25bn hybrid call. Low novelty; well founded.
  • Late 2026 to early 2027: Bumerangue appraisal starts; UK North Sea and Archaea sale outcomes; a Lightsource partner. Medium novelty; outcomes uncertain.

Months 6 to 18 (about April 2027 to April 2028):

  • Quarterly buyback execution and a growing dividend (at least 4% a year). Medium novelty once started.
  • The cost programme's end-2027 deadline ($6.5-7.5bn, operating cost about $18bn) and the 2027 ROACE test (above 16%). Medium novelty; partly well founded.

  • Bumerangue appraisal results; Kaskida and Tiber farm-downs at "full value"; completion of the $20bn disposal plan. High novelty; uncertain.
  • Possible UK fiscal reform replacing the Energy Profits Levy. Low relevance after the exit.

Months 18 to 36 (about 2028 to late 2029):

  • Azule's Greater PAJ first oil (2028); Kaskida first oil (2029) and Tiber (about 2030); hybrids at $9bn; extended gearing toward about 21% (Redburn, 2028). Medium novelty; reasonably founded, exposed to litigation and supply chains.
  • A Bumerangue development decision. High novelty; speculative.

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

D4610th of 13

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

D4610th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structure is supportive on average and unusually supportive in 2026, but BP is a mid-ranked, sub-scale participant without market power, on the wrong side of the consolidation toward advantaged resource holders, and on the buying side of a supplier market that is gaining pricing power. Its genuine value-chain advantages are in the middle of the chain (refining complexity and integrated trading), which are precisely the parts of the industry most exposed to normalisation from 2027. The support is fragile because it depends on OPEC+ and on Middle East geopolitics. For the Consumer: no market-share gains in the model; upstream margins move with the deck; refining capture and trading should revert toward historical averages; supplier inflation of 3-5% a year eats into cost savings; and the structural position argues for a below-peer multiple.

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F5611th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

Judgement: BP's own trading organisation is its most valuable route to market and is helping strongly; third-party intermediaries (distributors, convenience partners, joint ventures) are a moderate help; the fragility is in trading earnings' dependence on volatility.

  • Supply, Trading and Shipping: connects upstream and downstream, re-routes cargoes in disruptions, and adds about 4 percentage points to group return on capital on average; trading has historically contributed up to one third of group earnings in volatile periods.
  • Partners: Jio-bp grew to 1,916 outlets in India; Lekkerland supplies Aral sites in Germany to 2028; McFall Fuel doubled volumes in New Zealand; sales of Netherlands and Austrian networks to local operators.
  • Fragility: calmer markets reduce trading income; dependence on regional distributors grows as BP sells company-owned sites.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F4511th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

Judgement: competitors' operating behaviour is disciplined and does not hurt BP's businesses; their capital-markets behaviour hurts BP badly, because stronger balance sheets let them pay far more to shareholders and to buy growth, which keeps BP's relative valuation and its position as a potential target fragile.

  • Shell: 18.7% gearing, $3bn a quarter of buybacks plus a $1.2bn catch-up, ARC Resources bought; TotalEnergies: 13.1% gearing, 3% growth, a $1.5bn quarterly buyback; Eni: 10% pro forma gearing, 4-5% growth, €3.4bn buyback; ExxonMobil returned $9.4bn to shareholders in Q2 2026 alone.
  • Operating competition: TotalEnergies' sub-$5/boe costs and Chevron's early $3bn cost delivery set the cost bar BP must match; Shell's 102% refinery utilisation in Q2 2026 against BP's 94.7% availability shows BP left margin on the table.
  • Fragility: BP's 2026 total cash return yield was estimated at 5.1% against Shell's 8.2% and TotalEnergies' 7.1% (Zeus, Redburn); the gap closes only if BP restarts buybacks.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F5010th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting, how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement: the disruption forces currently act most strongly on suppliers (concentration and inflation), on competitors in LNG and refining (capacity waves) and on regulators (a US-Europe divergence); on demand they act slowly; the combined effect leaves BP's position moderately fragile, with the main fragility in cost inflation and margin normalisation rather than in demand.

  • Demand: slower electric vehicle adoption extends oil's runway; AI data centres lift gas and power demand; high prices cause marginal demand destruction.
  • Competition: LNG supply wave; Asian and Middle East refining capacity; trading entrants.
  • Regulators: US pro-hydrocarbon; Europe pro-decarbonisation; windfall-tax temptation at high prices.
  • Suppliers: oilfield services pricing power from a depleted capacity base; two-year equipment lead times.
  • Geopolitics: the Strait of Hormuz is the dominant disruption force of 2026, raising prices while cutting some BP volumes (about 100 kboe/d in Q2 2026).

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

F4011th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement: BP is a mid-ranking global integrated major, about fifth among the Western majors by production and well behind the number one and two (ExxonMobil, Chevron); it is a leader only in niches: refining complexity and margin capture, US distillation capacity among European majors, and integrated trading.

  • Production: 2.31 mmboe/d in 2025 and 2.20 mmboe/d in Q2 2026, against Shell 2.70 mmboe/d pro forma (higher after ARC Resources' 370 kboe/d), TotalEnergies 2.4-2.53, Equinor about 2.2-2.3, Eni about 1.8; ExxonMobil produces above 1.8 mboe/d in the Permian alone and targets 5.4 mboe/d by 2030. BP's upstream oil output is roughly half ExxonMobil's.
  • Market value: about $137bn (FactSet, 25 October 2026) against ExxonMobil about $621bn and Shell about $241bn in the business-foundations report.
  • LNG: a 26.8 mtpa strategic portfolio against Shell, which handles nearly 20% of global LNG demand, and TotalEnergies about 10%; BP is a distant third or fourth among the majors.
  • Refining: highest complexity cover among majors; 64% capture in the first half of 2026 (Shell 55%, TotalEnergies 35%); capacity falls to about 1.2 mb/d in 2027 after Gelsenkirchen, against TotalEnergies' 1.76 mb/d European capacity.
  • Trading: one of the two largest trading organisations among the majors (with Shell).
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

F4512th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution?

Judgement: the upstream market is not concentrated among listed companies (the top five Western majors produce roughly a tenth of global oil and gas), but price is set by a concentrated cartel (OPEC+, roughly 40% of crude supply), which gives the industry an effective concentration far higher than share data suggest; LNG supply is concentrating fast; refining and retail are fragmented.

  • Upstream: approximate general-industry figures (not from the files): global oil and gas output is about 175-180 mboe/d; the five largest Western majors together produce roughly 15-16 mboe/d, under 10%; national oil companies (Saudi Aramco and others) dominate the top of the distribution. The top five majors control over 30% of global deepwater capital spending (business-foundations report), so deepwater is concentrated.
  • LNG: the US and Qatar are projected to hold 57% of global liquefaction capacity by 2031; Shell alone handles about 20% of demand.
  • Refining and retail: fragmented regional markets; margins set by marginal capacity, not by share.
  • Implication: BP has no market power of its own; it benefits from OPEC+ discipline when it holds and suffers when it breaks.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F4511th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

Judgement: shares among the majors are stable over a few years but drift with portfolio choices; the large share gains of the last decade went to US shale independents, national oil companies and US LNG developers, and BP has lost share by choice (the 2020 production-cut plan and disposals).

  • Non-OPEC+ supply grew 1.8 mb/d in 2025, led by Americas tight oil and deepwater; BP's own production has been broadly flat at about 2.3 mboe/d and is guided to 2.3-2.5 mboe/d by 2030 while Shell and TotalEnergies grow 3-4% a year.
  • LNG: new US developers (Venture Global, Cheniere) have taken material share since 2020; North American export capacity is projected to rise from 15 Bcf/d (2024) to 25 Bcf/d (2028).
  • Trading: trading houses and now NOC-backed ventures (Eni-Mercuria) compete.
  • Retail: convenience operators (Couche-Tard, Catom, Orlen) gain where BP exits.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

F3512th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones, and is this company among them?

Judgement: share is consolidating toward the largest and most advantaged resource holders (ExxonMobil in the Permian and Guyana, TotalEnergies' low-cost growth, Shell in LNG and now Canadian gas), and BP is not among them: it is a seller of assets and a plausible consolidation target.

  • Consolidation: about $200bn of announced deals in the 2023 cycle; Shell-ARC ($16.4-16.5bn enterprise value, 4% production growth to 2030); a shrinking private operator base.
  • Advantage: TotalEnergies' sub-$5/boe operating cost and 12.2-year reserve life; ExxonMobil's Permian scale; Shell's LNG portfolio; Eni's 4.4-year discovery-to-production cycle. BP's advantages (refining capture, trading) are not the ones that win resource consolidation.
  • BP's position: reserve life 7.1 years (lowest among majors, Jefferies); organic reserve replacement of 39% (three years) or 90% (2025) on differing definitions; sub-scale against US majors.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

D567th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

What is competitors' attitude to competition: margin and return on capital first, or volume first; benign or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: the listed majors are clearly margin-and-return first and benign; the swing actor, OPEC+, behaves as a price-supporter most of the time and as a volume-first aggressor when it chooses; refining entrants and LNG developers are volume-first; industry prices tend to rise, not fall, when volume falls, because supply is managed.

  • Majors: "value over volume" (BP), buybacks benchmarked against every project (Shell), all projects tested at $50/bbl (TotalEnergies), reinvestment far below historical averages.
  • OPEC+: unwound cuts in 2025 (Brent averaged $69); in 2014-16 and 2020 it fought for market share and prices collapsed.
  • New refining capacity in Asia and the Middle East and new LNG liquefaction are built for volume and will lower margins as they arrive.
  • When volume falls (the 2026 Hormuz shut-ins) prices rise sharply, which helps diversified producers like BP whose volumes sit mostly outside the disrupted area.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

F3811th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability, and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: the majors' attitude is stable and more disciplined than three years ago; the instability sits with OPEC+ and with geopolitics, both of which are situational, reactive and able to move prices by tens of dollars within months; a market-share war is a real, low-to-medium probability tail.

  • Changes in 18 months to three years: majors became more disciplined (BP and Shell cut transition spending; capital frames tightened); OPEC+ unwound cuts in 2025, pushing Brent to the high $60s, then war reversed it; Bernstein at one point dragged its 2026 Brent expectation to $59 on OPEC+ supply and tariffs.
  • Shark-attack test: OPEC+ has done it twice in a decade (2014, 2020); the trigger is lost market share to non-OPEC supply, which grew 1.8 mb/d in 2025 and is expected to peak by early 2027, which lowers the incentive.
  • For BP specifically, the highest-geared European major, a shark attack is the most damaging external event: at $60 Brent EPS falls toward $0.34-0.41 (brokers' cases).
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

F4810th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement: the structure is supportive today (capital discipline everywhere, little new upstream capacity, low inventories, OPEC+ managing supply, a geopolitical risk premium), but the support is fragile because it rests on two actors BP cannot influence (OPEC+ and the parties to the Middle East conflict) and because two capacity waves (LNG from 2026-28, refining) are already under construction.

  • Supportive: majors' discipline; "nobody is building" scarcity is real in upstream; Bernstein's marginal cost of non-OPEC oil of $69 (2025) rising to $77 (2026) anchors a long-term floor near the mid-$70s in its view, with a marginal cash cost floor near $39.
  • Fragile: a peace that reopens Hormuz; an OPEC+ market-share decision; the LNG wave; refining capacity additions; demand destruction at very high prices.
  • Net: supportive on average over the cycle, but the 2026 level of support is far above the average and is the part most likely to go.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

D568th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement: substitutes are a slow, structural threat to road fuels and refining, receding in the near term and not a factor in the 2026-2029 model; the threat to BP is now more about having cut its own exposure to the substitutes than about the substitutes themselves.

  • Road transport: electric vehicles growing slower than expected; hybrids surging; electric trucks 15-20% more expensive per mile than diesel; charging infrastructure stalled in Europe.
  • Power: renewables and batteries substitute gas in power generation, offset by AI-driven power demand; standalone battery economics constrained by grid queues.
  • Fuels: biofuels to about 6% of liquid transport fuels by 2030, supported by mandates; BP retains biofuels in Downstream.
  • Long-term: Carbon Tracker warns that prolonged low-carbon retrenchment exposes BP to demand substitution as transport electrifies.
