4a

Business quality

F4412th of 138 answers
Judgement44C-4145C-4245C-4345C-4448C-4530C-4642C-4755
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

F4412th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

BP's competitive advantage is modest and concentrated in the middle of the value chain: integrated trading and complex, high-capture refining. These are genuine and currently earning far above their average, but they are the most cyclical parts of the business and they shrink in calm markets. BP lacks the long-run advantages of the sector's winners: low unit cost, scale, volume growth and long reserve life. Mechanical forces favour BP's financial lines from 2027, but not its competitive position. For the Consumer this means: no structural margin premium over peers in the normalised years, refining capture and trading modelled back toward historical averages, and no basis for a peer-premium multiple.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

F4511th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold, how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement: BP has two real but mid-chain advantages (integrated trading, and complex, high-capture refining with US scale) and several moderate ones (deepwater Gulf of America technology, long host-government partnerships, a resource base it rates as long-lived); it lacks the advantages that matter most for long-run returns in this industry: low unit cost, scale and reserve life. Overall the moat is modest, and the parts that are widening are the parts most exposed to normalisation.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

D459th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors, and what does that scale confer?

Judgement: BP is large in absolute terms but sub-scale against the two US supermajors and Shell; its scale confers access to mega-projects, host governments and the physical flows that feed trading, but not cost leadership or the balance-sheet weight to buy growth.

  • Size: revenue $189.3bn (FY2025, FactSet); about 93,700 employees in 61 countries; production about 2.3 mmboe/d; about 30,000 tier-one suppliers and $142.5bn paid to suppliers in 2025; market value about $137bn and enterprise value about $154bn.
  • Relative: ExxonMobil's market value is over four times BP's; Shell's operating cash flow and capital budget (about $40bn of capex in the business-foundations report; $20-22bn of cash capex guided for 2027-28) are far larger; Chevron's after-tax earnings sensitivity to $1 Brent (about $600m) is nearly double BP's ($340m pre-tax).
  • What scale confers: deepwater operatorship, a seat at host-government tables, 26.8 mtpa of LNG portfolio, a top-two trading book among majors, and procurement leverage.
  • What it does not: a self-funded growth programme like ExxonMobil's Permian (above 1.8 mboe/d growing about 9% a year) or Shell's ARC purchase.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F4812th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior?

Judgement: BP sells largely undifferentiated commodities, so surplus customer utility is at parity; it is demonstrably not inferior (reliable supply, global trading reach, long-term contracts, decent retail loyalty), but it is not leading anywhere except in supply reliability through trading.

  • Not inferior: long-term LNG buyers (Kogas twice, a 20-year China contract) and Airbus (jet fuel and sustainable aviation fuel) chose BP; loyalty-app customers are over five times more valuable than non-members; electric vehicle customers at co-located hubs spend 20% more in-store.
  • Not leading: unbranded retail customers have no switching costs; B2B power and fuel buyers are price-sensitive; Castrol, the most differentiated offer, is being sold.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

F3013th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility?

Judgement: BP has no demonstrable pricing power in the core; realisations follow benchmarks with lags, and realised refining margins can run $5/bbl below the indicator; the trading desk's ability to earn above benchmark in volatile markets is the closest thing to pricing power and it is cyclical.

  • Evidence of price-taking: realised liquids $85.14/bbl in Q2 2026 against Brent $103.85; US gas realisations of $1.72/mcf against European $17.25; one- to two-month realisation lags in upstream and about nine months for oil-indexed gas.
  • Evidence of capture: 64% refining capture in H1 2026 (top of peers); trading "exceptional" in Q1 2026 and slightly higher in Q2.
  • Castrol: genuine price pass-through and premium, but 65% sold; it lost 1-2% share after the 2011 price rises.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F4210th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

Judgement: it does not, and in a commodity business it does not need to; scale savings are retained for the balance sheet and shareholders. This neither helps nor hurts the case materially.

  • There is no stated scale-sharing strategy; pricing follows market benchmarks; convenience retail uses loyalty discounts (for example a 50 cents per gallon introductory card discount) as marketing rather than as structural sharing.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

D557th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

Judgement: the mechanical forces now act in BP's favour on the financial lines (interest, hybrid coupons, eventually share count and tax rate) and against it on the asset base (depletion with the lowest reserve life among majors); on balance they lift EPS modestly each year from 2027, but they do not improve competitive advantage.

  • Positive mechanics: net debt falling from $22.25bn (Q2 2026) toward about $10bn removes interest; consensus already models net finance costs (EBIT less pre-tax profit) falling from about $4.1bn in FY2026 to $2.7bn in FY2027 and $2.1bn in FY2028; hybrids fall from about $13bn to about $9bn by end-2027; buybacks, once restarted, shrink the share count (BP cut basic shares from 20.2bn in 2019 to 15.4bn in 2025); the UK exit lowers the tax rate; the cost run-rate rolls through.
  • Negative mechanics: base decline on mature fields and the 7.1-year reserve life require continuous new projects just to stay flat; supplier inflation compounds; Gulf of America payments continue.
  • Against peers: Shell, TotalEnergies and Eni already enjoy the financial mechanics (low gearing, buybacks); BP is catching up, not pulling ahead.

For the model: the clearest gravitational gains are below EBIT; test whether consensus's $2bn fall in net finance costs is achievable, since part of BP's finance cost is the unwinding of discount on provisions, which does not fall with net debt.

Management quality

F4612th of 1312 answers
Judgement46C-5552C-5655C-5764C-57b45C-5835C-5935C-6056C-6146C-6240C-6342C-6438
Group judgement

Company - Management

F4612th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is in transition from weak to promising. The new chief executive is an operator whose first seven months show decisiveness, candour and a willingness to sell anything; the chair is new, the board smaller, incentives tied to cash and returns, and the CFO has delivered the deleveraging. Against that: the senior team is incomplete, the culture is strained, the business is under-invested, the five-year record is one of reversals and capital destruction, and alignment through insider ownership is minimal. For the Consumer, management quality supports believing the financial targets that management controls (capex, net debt, disposals, a share of the cost programme) more than the operating targets it does not yet control (reliability, the bottom-line impact of costs, project timing). Weight the plan's financial lines at face value and haircut its operating lines.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D528th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Management

How big is the company's ambition or vision, particularly in relation to peers?

Judgement: the ambition is pragmatic and returns-led rather than big: to make BP "fit to grow" and to compete "in its actual weight class"; it is the right ambition for BP's balance sheet but smaller than TotalEnergies' (growth plus a power business) or Eni's (growth plus satellites), and it does not yet contain a growth vision.

  • Evidence: O'Neill rejected the "supermajor" label; "every part of the company needs to earn its place, generating cash"; the portfolio has "too much stuff"; production 2.3-2.5 mboe/d by 2030 (flat to +2% a year) against Shell's 4% and TotalEnergies' 3%.
  • Upside to ambition: the 15 December 2026 strategy event may add a growth leg (deepwater, gas, Brazil).
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F559th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Management

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities: widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

Judgement: the priorities are right and some are bold (exit the whole UK North Sea, sell 65% of Castrol, close the venture arm), but the strategy is a repair strategy, not a moat-widening or growth strategy; it capitalises well on high prices (accelerated deleveraging) and is fragile in low prices (dividend breakeven $60-65).

  • Right: deleverage first; simplify; capital to the highest-return barrels (US Gulf, BPX, LNG); value over speed in disposals; capex discipline.
  • Bold: a full basin exit; a chief executive who has "no emotional connection to legacy assets" (Jefferies); delaying Paleogene farm-downs to capture value despite raising capex.
  • Missing: a plan to fix the reserve-life gap other than Bumerangue; a stated distribution framework; a growth answer to peers.
  • Range of environments: in a high-price world the strategy turns windfall into balance-sheet repair and then buybacks; in a $60 world it relies on capex flexibility (down to $13bn) and disposals.

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture?

  • Tests to come: reliability back above 95%; disposal prices; the 15 December framework.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

F4211th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is: what does the evidence show, and why?

Judgement: the formal structure is shareholder-friendly and the current board's actions are prudent, but the evidence over five years shows a company that put management's strategic preferences and then activist demands ahead of its own foresight, with governance failures at the very top; prudence is being rebuilt, not inherited.

Evidence for shareholders first:

  • One share, one vote; nine of eleven non-executive directors independent; an unqualified audit with no restatements; a 2026 audit re-tender.
  • Pay: the former chief executive's single figure of £5.3m (below the US median of $18.5m); a downward bonus adjustment of four points for fatalities; clawback for three years; post-employment shareholding requirements; the 2023-25 plan vested at only 23.3%, so pay followed poor returns.
  • Prudence now: buybacks suspended to repair the balance sheet before a downturn rather than after; hybrids repaid without replacement.

Evidence against:

  • Governance failures: a chief executive resigned over undisclosed relationships (2023); a chair removed for conduct after eight months (2026); a 75.72% vote for the earlier chair (2025).
  • Capital destruction under board oversight: the 2020 transition strategy, Archaea, offshore wind.
  • Alignment: insider ownership about 0.03% of the free float; no open-market purchases by executive directors in the last 12 months (only plan purchases and dividend reinvestment; one senior officer sold $618,701 of shares in March 2026); the CEO shareholding requirement is 5 times salary against ExxonMobil's chief executive's 111 times.
  • Why: the change was forced by Elliott and by share-price underperformance rather than initiated by the board.

F3810th of 13

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: historically no: BP repurchased heavily (basic shares fell from 20.2bn in 2019 to 15.4bn in 2025) but partly with borrowed or disposal money and without regard to the cycle, and it misallocated growth capital to low-return transition assets; the current team allocates more rationally but is a forced deleverager, not yet a dynamic repurchaser.

  • Now: every project competes against cash; capex disciplined; farm-downs delayed to capture value; low-carbon capped at under $0.80bn a year.

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

F4313th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

BP's economic strength is weak-to-middling on a normalised basis and spectacular at today's prices. The key numbers for the Consumer: normalised net income of about $7.5-9.5bn by 2028 at BP's planning deck (consensus $10.1bn sits above it); ROACE of 12-14% in normal years against a 16% target that needs $70-plus prices to be safe; an earnings base flattered by recurring "one-off" items and by cash costs outside underlying profit; distributable cash after leases, hybrid coupons and Gulf of America payments that roughly covers the dividend at $70; and very high price leverage on a fixed cost base. The improvable lines are financial (interest, hybrids, share count, tax) and a few operating ones (reliability, cost). The base supports a dividend and a modest buyback at normal prices, not a compounding equity.

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

History and consensus on the brokers' basis (FactSet):

  • FY2029 rests on few brokers (6-10); treat as indicative.
  • Broker dispersion is extreme: FY2027 EPS ranges $0.54 (Intesa Sanpaolo) to $1.29 (Barclays); FY2028 $0.21 (GERDES) to $0.93 (Barclays). The street has no agreed normalised earnings level; the dispersion is mainly the price deck.

My normalised bridge, FY2025 to FY2028 at BP's planning deck ($70 Brent, $10.80 refining margin), post-tax, approximate:

  • FY2025 underlying profit $7.49bn at about $69 Brent and a refining margin of about $12.80: deck adjustment about -$0.35bn (lower refining margin outweighs slightly higher Brent): about $7.15bn.
  • Net structural cost savings reaching profit: gross incremental savings of about $3.7-4.7bn to end-2027, of which about $1bn is Gelsenkirchen (perimeter), less 40-60% leakage to inflation on the rest: +$0.6bn to +$1.3bn.
  • Castrol's lost earnings (no income from the retained 35%): about -$0.6bn to -$0.7bn.
  • Gelsenkirchen margin lost net of its $1bn cost: about -$0.1bn to -$0.3bn.
  • UK North Sea sale (100 kboe/d at a 78% marginal tax rate): about -$0.2bn to -$0.4bn.
  • Upstream start-ups net of decline, BPX growth: +$0.3bn to +$0.6bn.
  • Lower net debt and hybrid stack: +$0.5bn to +$0.6bn.
  • Lower group tax rate as the UK leaves the mix: +$0.2bn to +$0.3bn.
  • Result: about $7.5bn to $9.5bn (centre about $8.5bn), or about $0.49 to $0.62 a share on 15.4bn shares before any buyback.

A cross-check from BP's own target: return on average capital employed above 16% by 2027 at $70 Brent. ROACE of 13.9% in 2025 implies average capital employed of roughly $70bn; after about $15-20bn of disposals, capital employed of about $55-60bn at 16% implies roughly $9-9.6bn of profit before interest, or roughly $7-8bn after interest. BP's own target therefore sits at the lower half of my range, and consensus FY2028 ($10.1bn) sits above both, which means consensus embeds a deck above $70 or margins above planning. This arithmetic is rough and the capital-employed figure should be verified.

Durability: the price component is not durable; the self-help and financing component (about +$1.5bn to +$2.5bn over three years) is moderately durable; the perimeter component is a permanent loss of earnings in exchange for debt reduction.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

D4210th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

Judgement: BP has more high-return opportunities than balance-sheet room (Gulf of America deepwater, BPX's short-cycle shale, Brazil, Trinidad and Egyptian gas, LNG), but capital is capped at $13-15bn a year and much of the best resource (Bumerangue) pays only in the 2030s; the realistic reinvestment profile sustains volumes rather than compounding them, which justifies a below-peer, ex-growth multiple.

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

D489th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

Judgement: headline cash conversion looks high because depreciation ($17-18bn a year) far exceeds sustaining needs above capex, but after the claims that rank ahead of ordinary shareholders (leases, hybrid coupons, Gulf of America payments) the cash left for distributions at a normal deck only roughly covers the dividend; working capital is large, price-driven and counter-cyclical.

  • FY2025 at about $69 Brent: operating cash flow $24.5bn; operating cash flow before working capital $22.7bn; organic capex $13.6bn; adjusted free cash flow $9.1bn. Then: lease payments above $2.2bn and hybrid coupons together about $4bn a year (Morgan Stanley); Gulf of America payments about $1.1-1.6bn a year pre-tax (they sit in BP's working-capital line, so "adjusted" free cash flow excludes them). Cash left for shareholders: roughly $4bn, against a dividend of about $5.1bn. That arithmetic is why buybacks were suspended.
  • Redburn puts the dividend breakeven at $60-65 Brent, which implies a surplus of about $1-2bn at $70; the change-forces report quotes a breakeven of $83 including the former buyback structure.
  • 2026: FactSet consensus free cash flow of $24.7bn, plus $8-9bn of disposal proceeds, funds the drop in net debt toward about $10bn.
  • Normalised (2028, $70 deck): after Castrol's and the North Sea's lost cash and with lower interest and hybrid coupons, distributable organic cash after the dividend is about break-even to $2bn a year. Buyback capacity in 2027-28 therefore comes from: the elevated deck while it lasts; the headroom between the end-2026 net debt (about $10bn on broker estimates) and the $14-18bn target range; and the remaining $4-5bn of the $20bn disposal plan, less the £1.25bn hybrid call in March 2027.
  • Working capital: built $6.0bn in Q1 2026 ($4.1bn seasonal inventory and higher transit volumes, $1.1bn payables timing, $0.8bn Gulf of America) and $7.0bn in the first half; $2-3bn guided to release in the second half; it releases when prices fall, which cushions a downturn, and it builds when prices rise, which slows deleveraging in a spike.

For the model: model distributable free cash flow explicitly as operating cash flow before working capital, less capex, leases, hybrid coupons and Gulf of America payments; treat working capital as a function of the price path.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

F3011th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

Judgement: the earnings base is not clean. The gap between underlying and reported profit has been very large for three years, the "one-off" impairments recur, several real cash costs sit outside the underlying figure, and the tax rate is high; underlying profit overstates economic earnings over the cycle.

  • Gap: reported net income of $55m in FY2025 against underlying $7.49bn; $391m in FY2024 against $8.9bn; underlying exceeded IFRS profit by $1.8bn in Q2 2026.
  • Recurring "adjusting items": about $6bn of low-carbon impairments in 2025 (about $2bn goodwill), $1bn in Q2 2026, a guided $3.0-4.5bn post-tax in Gas and Low Carbon Energy (Jefferies), a $478m Bay du Nord write-off, a $539m UK deferred tax charge.
  • Capitalised costs: $4.4bn of exploration and appraisal assets capitalised in 2024, flagged by Deloitte as a key audit matter.
  • Cash outside underlying profit: Gulf of America payments; lease principal; hybrid coupons.
  • Tax: underlying effective rate guided at 35-40% (from about 40%); the UK's 78% regime; consensus implies about 45% leakage between pre-tax profit and adjusted net income in FY2027-28 (tax plus minorities and hybrids), against about 38% in FY2026.
  • Currency: net exchange losses of $295m (2025), $541m (2024).

Data traps for the Consumer (from the FactSet block and the reports):

  1. EPS comes on four bases (reported, FactSet adjusted, the Viewforth convention, brokers' adjusted); compute consensus growth only from the brokers'-basis actuals (FY2025 EPS $0.480; EBITDA $36.6bn).
  2. FactSet operating EBITDA ($31.3bn in FY2025) is 17% below the brokers' basis ($36.6bn); revenue appears as $189.3bn (FactSet), $155.8bn and $148.8bn (the last is Customers and Products only) in different reports.
  3. FactSet debt includes leases (total debt $72.7bn and net debt $35.5bn at Q2 2026); BP's headline net debt excludes leases ($22.25bn); gearing appears as 22.6% (ex leases), 32.5-33% (with leases) and 45-47.5% (extended, with hybrids).
  4. The FactSet "share buybacks" line for 2026 year to date (-$3.4bn) almost certainly includes the €2.5bn ($2.855bn) hybrid redemption, because BP's hybrids are equity instruments; it is not evidence of buybacks.
  5. Share counts differ: 15,376.7m basic (FactSet, FY2025), 15.7bn issued ordinary shares (annual report), 16,025.8m diluted (FactSet capital-structure snapshot).
  6. The hybrid stack is given as $13.3bn, about $13.0bn and $16bn at various dates; the $9bn end-2027 target is consistent across sources.
  7. Reserve replacement appears as 39% (three-year organic), 41%, and 90% (2025); reserve life as 7.1 years (proved) and 23 years (resource).
  8. From 1 January 2027 BP reports two segments (Upstream, including gas and power trading; Downstream, including oil trading and Castrol's retained stake); segment history will need re-basing.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

F4811th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement: financial leverage is still the highest in the peer set but falling fast; it amplifies EPS and distribution risk materially at low prices and is comfortable at today's prices; by end-2026 it should be acceptable on BP's own target, though "total financial obligations" of about $40bn remain high, as the chief executive herself says.

  • Stack at Q2 2026: net debt excluding leases $22.25bn (gearing 22.6%); with leases $35.6bn; hybrids about $13bn; total financial obligations (net debt, leases, hybrids, Gulf of America) $53.6bn, guided to $39-41bn by end-2026. Extended gearing was 47.5% in late 2025 (peer average about 18%); Redburn expects 31.1% at end-2026 after Castrol and 21.4% by 2028.
  • Cost: net interest expense rose from $1.9bn (2022) to $4.0bn (2025); finance debt carries a weighted 5.0% rate over about eight years, 29% floating.
  • Amplification: in a normal year, net finance costs of about $4bn equal about 30% of EBIT; in FY2026 about 10%. Each $10bn of net debt repaid saves about $0.5bn pre-tax.
  • Comfort: Fitch rates BP A+ (stable) and expects net leverage of 0.7 times in 2026 from 1.3 times; FactSet net debt to EBITDA (with leases) 0.85 times at Q2 2026. Discomfort: the dividend breakeven of $60-65 Brent; at $60 brokers see underlying EPS near $0.34-0.41 and extended gearing back above 50% in a downcycle (Redburn).

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

5a

Pricing power

3 answers
C-1732C-4530P-0430
C-17To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?F3213th of 13full answer under 2b Internal corporate change forces

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

F3011th of 11

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: price is the dominant contributor to every line, and BP sets none of it. From FY2026 to FY2028, price and margin normalisation take revenue down about 24% and EBITDA down about $19bn, with the EBITDA margin falling from 23.3% to 19.6%. BP's ability to earn above benchmarks (refining capture, trading, integration) is real but cyclical. It adds most when markets are dislocated, which is exactly when the deck is already high, so it amplifies the deck rather than offsetting it. Four contract mechanics shape the timing and should be modelled explicitly: upstream price lags, production-sharing entitlement, the roughly nine-month lag on oil-indexed LNG, and windfall levies.

Price's contribution to revenue and margin (base case):

  • Revenue: +27.5% in FY2026, almost all price, with Brent +35% and products more. -16.3% in FY2027: about -12% price, about -4% perimeter (Castrol about $7-8bn a year, plus the rest of Gelsenkirchen). -9.0% in FY2028 (price). -2.0% in FY2029. +1.0% in FY2030 (volume). Consensus has FY2028 revenue flat on FY2027 ($199.7bn in both years), which is inconsistent with any falling deck. It rests on 17 brokers with very different decks, and it does not matter for EPS.
  • Margin: EBITDA margin of 23.3% in FY2026, 21.0% in FY2027 and 19.6% in FY2028-30, against 19.3-20.8% in FY2023-25. Price and margin explain about 95% of the change. The rest is costs, perimeter and volume.

Evidence that BP is a price-taker, and where it captures more:

  • Upstream realisations track benchmarks with discounts. Liquids realisations were $85.14/bbl in Q2 2026 against Brent at $103.85 (82%), and $77.20 in Q3 against $96.99 (80%). By region in Q3: US $67.31 (heavy crude, Gulf lag), Europe $102.74, rest of world $88.04. Gas averaged $6.57/mcf, with the US at $2.41 against Henry Hub at $2.96 and Europe at $16.89.
  • Refining capture is high and cyclical. BP captured 64% of indicator margins in H1 2026, against Shell's 55% and TotalEnergies' 35% (Jefferies, Sep-26). The reasons are high complexity, Whiting's heavy Canadian crude and Rotterdam's link to the trading book. Capture is cyclical: realised margins ran more than $5/bbl below the indicator in early 2026 (feedstock and freight). The sale of Gelsenkirchen cut capacity 17%. Q4 maintenance cuts throughput by 200 kb/d. Rotterdam had an unplanned outage in October 2026. US independents capture more (Marathon 95-112%, Valero about 82%). I model capture falling from the H1 2026 level to about 55% in 2027 and 50% in 2028.
  • Trading: BP claims about 4 points of return on capital uplift on average over 2020-25, of which at least 2 points are structural. Jefferies earlier put the historical figure at 2 points. Trading was "exceptional" in 2026, and Q4 2026 is guided weaker on lower volatility. Calm years, 2023-24, produced eight quarters of results below consensus. I model trading at about $1.7bn above normal in 2026, $0.8bn above normal in 2027, and normal from 2028.
  • Retail: volumes fell 2% in the US and 8% in Europe in Q3 2026 at high pump prices. Customers EBIT was about $1.14bn in Q3 against $1.77bn in Q2 (Jefferies estimate), on weaker fuel margins and Castrol's lagged base-oil costs. Retail margins compress when prices rise quickly and recover when they fall. In 2027 this is a small counter-cyclical support, worth perhaps $0.2-0.4bn.

The four mechanics that set the timing:

  1. Upstream price lags (about one month in the Gulf of America, two months in the UAE) were worth $1.8-2.1bn in Q2 2026. They work in reverse when prices fall, so the quarter with the steepest Brent fall in 2027 (Q2 or Q3 in my deck) carries a negative lag effect of perhaps $0.3-0.6bn.
  2. Production-sharing entitlement (Angola, Azerbaijan, Egypt, Oman) gives more barrels at lower prices. That is a small automatic damper on the downside: at Equinor, the PSA volume effect was 52 kboe/d at high prices in Q3 2026. Rumaila in Iraq is a fee-per-barrel service contract, so the price upside goes to Iraq.
  3. Oil-indexed LNG with a lag of about nine months carries 2026's high oil prices into contract prices through H1 2027. That supports gas realisations while spot prices fall, a lagged tailwind of perhaps $0.3-0.5bn in H1 2027 that I include in my FY2027 EBITDA. From 2027 the LNG supply wave (net additions peaking at about 38 mt in 2028, Morgan Stanley) compresses margins on BP's roughly 10 mtpa of short-term and spot volumes. Buyers will seek price reviews on the roughly 23 mtpa long-term book. BP discloses no slopes or reopener terms.
  4. Windfall levies cap capture at high prices. The UK's 78% Energy Profits Levy runs to March 2030, BP is exiting the regime, and the October 2026 Budget made no change. The EU solidarity contribution cost BP $505m in 2022 and $146m in 2023. At Brent above $100 a new European levy is a live risk for refining and marketing, about 5% of free cash flow on the 2022-23 precedent. It is not in my base case.

One-off upside: BP's damages claim against Venture Global (Calcasieu Pass) is $3.7bn to over $6bn, with the hearing expected late 2026 to 2027. Edison settled for cargoes rather than cash. I do not model it. If won in cash, it would be a lump sum to buybacks or debt.

For the model: treat price as an exogenous deck. Use realisations of about 80-85% of Brent for liquids, with capture falling toward 50% and trading toward normal by 2028. Flag in the commentary that BP's above-benchmark earning power adds to the deck rather than offsetting it. There is no company-driven real price contribution to revenue or margin.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7535P-0138
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

F3513th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

Judgement: very material, but it is price leverage on a largely fixed cost base rather than classic volume operating leverage; a plausible range of decks over the next 18 months moves annual pre-tax operating profit by more than $20bn, which makes BP's earnings cone the widest of the five peers in relative terms.

  • Rules of thumb (2026, after Gelsenkirchen): Brent $1/bbl = $340m pre-tax (about 80% Oil Production and Operations, 20% Gas and Low Carbon); refining indicator margin $1/bbl = $450m; Henry Hub $0.10/mmbtu = $40m. A $10 Brent move is about 26% of FY2025 adjusted EBIT ($12.9bn); a $5 refining margin move about 17%.
  • Cost base: about $22bn of operating cost (2025, guided to about $18bn by 2027) and $17-17.5bn of depreciation are largely fixed against price.
  • Range: FactSet adjusted EBIT ranged from -$8.3bn (2020) to +$41.2bn (2022); consensus EBIT is $38.5bn, $26.3bn and $20.6bn for FY2026-28.
  • 18-month cone: from today's environment (Brent about $111, refining margin above $25) to BP's planning deck ($70, $10.80) is roughly $14bn of Brent effect plus about $7-8bn of refining effect, before trading normalisation.
  • Volume leverage: upstream volume of 1% (about 23 kboe/d) is worth roughly $0.2-0.25bn pre-tax at normal prices; plant reliability recovering from 92.4% to 96% is roughly 4% of upstream volume against the Q2 2026 run-rate (not against FY2025, when reliability was a record 96.1%).
  • Dampers: production-sharing contracts raise entitlement volumes when prices fall; realisation lags; trading can be counter-cyclical.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

F3810th of 11

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

Judgement: BP's operating leverage comes from price and margin acting on a cost base that is fixed in the short run and rises with inflation. It does not come from volume. Over the forecast it works mainly downward. In my base case EBITDA falls from about $56.3bn in FY2026 to $42.5bn in FY2027 and $36.1bn in FY2028, then holds at about $35-36bn to FY2030. That is back to the FY2024-25 level of $36.6-37.8bn, despite about $2.6bn of EBITDA leaving with disposals. Refining, not Brent, is the largest single source of the FY2026-27 fall. The cost base offers almost no cushion, because the guided fall in absolute operating cost is mostly perimeter.

What the cost base is, and how fixed it is:

  • BP now splits production and manufacturing expense into two parts. Activity-linked variable costs (transport, environmental compliance, marketing) were $5.97bn in Q3 2026, about 48% of the total. The underlying fixed operating expenditure was $5.26bn in the quarter, about $21bn a year. Depreciation adds $17.0-17.5bn a year (guided). EBITDA swings therefore pass almost one for one into EBIT.
  • The fixed base does not fall in real terms. Q3 2026 underlying operating expenditure was $5.26bn against $5.49bn a year earlier. About $0.17bn of that $0.22bn fall is Gelsenkirchen, which left on 31 July. Excluding perimeter, operating cost was flat year on year, even with $4.0bn of gross structural savings. Upstream unit cost rose 6% to $6.54/boe.
  • The guided fall in operating cost, from about $21.9bn (2025) to about $18bn (2027), is mostly perimeter. Morgan Stanley (22-Sep-26) puts $2.5bn of it on Castrol ($1.5bn) and Gelsenkirchen ($1.0bn). The North Sea sale would take a further $0.8bn or so of operating cost on my estimate (100-117 kboe/d at a high unit cost). The net efficiency gain after inflation is therefore only about $0.6-1.4bn, depending on whether the $18bn target already assumes the North Sea exit. Costs that leave with sold businesses take their revenue with them, so they are not operating leverage.

How the leverage will act on the line items (my base deck: Brent $93.5 in 2026, $80 in 2027, $72 in 2028 and $70 from 2029; RIM about $31, $17, $12, $11; Henry Hub about $3.3-3.75):

$bn (brokers' basis) FY2023 FY2024 FY2025 FY2026e FY2027e FY2028e FY2029e FY2030e
Revenue 210.1 189.2 189.3 241.4 202.0 183.8 180.1 181.9
EBITDA 43.7 37.8 36.6 56.3 42.5 36.1 35.3 35.7
EBITDA margin 20.8% 20.0% 19.3% 23.3% 21.0% 19.6% 19.6% 19.6%
Depreciation about 18.3 17.3 17.0 16.6 16.8 17.2
EBIT about 18.3 39.0 25.5 19.5 18.5 18.5

The FY2026-to-FY2027 fall in EBITDA ($13.8bn) breaks down as follows:

  • Refining margin: about -$6.3bn (RIM down about $14 at $450m per dollar).
  • Brent: about -$4.6bn ($13.5 lower at $340m per dollar).
  • Trading back toward normal: about -$1.7bn.
  • Castrol and Gelsenkirchen for a full year: about -$0.8bn.
  • Partly offset by early cost delivery and the North Sea's last nine months.

FY2027 to FY2028 loses a further $6.4bn: Brent -$2.7bn, refining -$2.3bn, the North Sea -$1.3bn, trading -$0.6bn, plus $0.4bn from costs.

Why this matters, and what is non-obvious:

  • Refining is now the main swing factor, and its shape is asymmetric. RIM fell to $6.70 in 2020 and reached $46.70 in Q3 2026. It has a floor (complex refiners keep running at $8-10) but no ceiling in a product shock. At $450m per dollar, the $35 swing in RIM between the 2026 average and a normal year is worth more than a $40 swing in Brent. Because of this convexity, BP's quarterly beats came from Customers and Products in every quarter of 2026. The same convexity makes BP's FY2027 the most disruption-sensitive year in the European peer set.
  • The margin percentage is a poor guide. Revenue is about 85% Downstream pass-through: Customers and Products third-party sales were $148.8bn of $189.3bn in 2025. The denominator therefore moves with price. EBITDA margin rose only from 19.3% to 23.3% while EBITDA rose 54%. The model should be driven in dollars from the deck, with the margin percentage derived from it. My margin inputs follow that rule.
  • Disposals lower the leverage, but only a little. The refining sensitivity fell from $550m to $450m per dollar. The North Sea sale removes high-cost barrels taxed at 78%, which carry a large pre-tax but small post-tax sensitivity. After the exit, BP's after-tax leverage to Brent is about the same as before, while its pre-tax leverage is slightly lower.
  • Volume leverage is small and goes the wrong way in the near term. Reported production is about 2,220 kboe/d in 2026 (2025: 2,312). Iraq lost about 100 kboe/d, but at service-contract economics worth little per barrel. Q4 guidance was lowered, and Redburn trimmed 2026 production by 2.5% (30-Oct-26). Each 1% of upstream volume (about 22 kboe/d) is worth only about $0.2-0.25bn of pre-tax profit at $70.
  • Against peers: Deutsche Bank estimates that European integrated sector EPS moves about 14.9% per $10 of Brent, with BP and TotalEnergies the most sensitive. The consensus EBIT margin fade from FY2026 to FY2028 is 6 points at BP, against 3 at Shell and Eni (peer table). Shell's refinery utilisation of about 100%, against BP's 94.7-96.5% availability, shows BP gets less fixed-cost dilution from throughput.

The cone and its skew. On my sensitivities, FY2027 EBITDA ranges from about $34bn (a peace in early 2027: Brent $65, RIM $11, normal trading) to about $53bn (disruption into 2027: Brent $95, RIM $28). The comparable FY2028 range is about $31-41bn. The cone is wider downward than upward from my base. My base already takes RIM to $17 in 2027, but a fast reopening that releases stranded Gulf crude and products, with Gulf refineries restarting, would compress both Brent and RIM within one or two quarters. The upside needs the disruption to outlast restocking demand.

For the model: drive EBITDA in dollars from the deck using BP's rules of thumb ($340m per $1 Brent, $450m per $1 RIM, $40m per $0.10 Henry Hub). Remove perimeter EBITDA explicitly: Castrol about $1.0bn from November 2026, Gelsenkirchen about $0.3bn from August 2026, the North Sea about $1.3bn from Q4 2027 (my timing assumption). Do not assume fixed costs flex down in a downturn. The only precedent of real flex is 2020, when BP cut cash costs 12% and capex 28% in a crisis.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7648P-0268
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?F4811th of 13full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

B+682nd of 11

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: within about 15 months BP moves from the most levered European major to one of the least levered. Net debt goes from $22.2bn (end-2025) to about $6bn at end-2026, before leases and hybrids. Financial leverage therefore stops amplifying the downside, and it becomes a source of per-share growth. The lower interest and hybrid cost is mechanical and worth about $1.2bn after tax, roughly $0.08 a share, by FY2028. The larger lever is the use of the new balance-sheet capacity for buybacks: on my base case that cuts the share count about 16% by end-2030. The balance sheet now protects the dividend at $60 Brent. It does not protect EPS, which stays a price-and-margin outcome.

The stack and its path (my estimates; BP definitions, net debt excluding leases):

$bn, year-end 2025 Q3 2026 2026e 2027e 2028e 2029e 2030e
Net debt (excluding leases) 22.2 13.9 6.0 6.3 6.6 7.9 9.5
Hybrids about 13-16 (sources differ) 12.1 12.1 9.0 9.0 9.0 9.0
Lease liabilities 14.6 about 14 about 14 about 14 about 14 about 14
Gulf of America payables 5.9 about 4.9 0 0 0 0
Net finance cost (P&L, excluding hybrids) 3.98 3.6 2.6 2.55 2.65 2.8
Hybrid coupons plus other minorities (post-tax) about 1.1 1.16 0.95 0.87 0.87 0.87

My end-2026 net debt of about $6bn is probably conservative. BP's new target for total financial obligations ($33-35bn: net debt plus leases, hybrids and the Gulf of America payable) implies end-2026 net debt of only about $3-4bn, unless a further hybrid is redeemed in Q4.

How financial leverage now shapes EPS:

  • Net finance costs fall about $1.4bn, but they have a floor. About $1.65bn of BP's net finance cost does not fall with net debt: lease interest ($0.70bn in 2025) and the unwinding of discount on provisions ($1.15bn in 2025, falling to about $0.95bn once the Gulf of America payable is settled and the North Sea decommissioning provisions leave). The debt-related part was about $2.1bn in 2025 on about $23bn of net debt. That rate is high because BP's gross finance debt (about $58bn at 5.0%, 29% floating) costs more than its roughly $35bn of gross cash earns. With net debt near $6bn, the debt-related part falls to about $0.9bn. That gives total net finance cost of about $2.6bn in FY2027 and about $2.55bn in FY2028.
  • I now disagree with the first set on finance costs. It expected a fall of only $0.5-1bn and called consensus's fall to $2.1bn in FY2028 "the line most likely to be too optimistic". That was right when net debt was $22bn and Castrol had not closed. On today's facts, consensus's FY2027 figure ($2.7bn, being EBIT less pre-tax profit) is about right. Its FY2028 figure ($2.1bn) is about $0.4bn too low, unless BP holds net cash, which conflicts with consensus's own assumption of a falling share count.
  • Hybrid coupons are deducted before profit attributable to shareholders. They were $568m in the first nine months of 2026. Taking the stack from about $13bn to $9bn saves about $0.25bn a year. Redemptions are equity outflows, not debt repayment: they raise net debt ($3.9bn in 2026, about $3.1bn in 2027).
  • Combined mechanical EPS lift: about $0.66bn post-tax from interest plus $0.24bn from hybrids by FY2028, against FY2026, and about $1.2bn against FY2025. That is about $0.06-0.08 a share, and it is the most predictable part of the forecast.

The bigger lever: capacity for buybacks (base case, detailed in P-08):

  • BP's old $14-18bn target range sits $8-12bn above my end-2026 net debt. Organic cash after the dividend is about $5bn a year at my base deck from 2028 (P-08). Remaining disposals are about $4bn in 2027 (North Sea, Archaea, a Lightsource partner, retail sites).
  • At $8 a share, each $1bn of buybacks retires about 125m shares, 0.8% of the count. That adds about 0.8% to EPS, against about 0.3% from using the same $1bn to cut net debt (pre-tax saving at 3.5-5%, taxed). At the current price, buybacks are worth about two to three times more to EPS than further deleveraging. This is the reinforcing half of the deleveraging-to-buyback loop (P-05).
  • My base case is $6.0bn in 2027, $5.5bn in 2028 and $4.5bn a year in 2029-30. The share count falls from 15.56bn to about 13.07bn by end-2030, which adds about 16% to FY2030 EPS. Morgan Stanley assumes only $0.75bn a quarter from Q3 2027 (5-Nov-26). Consensus implies average shares of 15.1bn (FY2027) and 15.0bn (FY2028), against my 15.27bn and 14.54bn.

How financial leverage now shapes the balance sheet and its risk:

  • Downside resilience is transformed. At $60 Brent and a $9 RIM in 2027-28, my model shows free cash flow after hybrids and leases of about $6.5-7bn a year, against a dividend of about $5.4bn. A larger working capital release in that case helps 2027. With buybacks cut to about $1-2bn, net debt would stay at about $6-9bn to end-2028, far inside the old $14-18bn target. The $60-65 dividend breakeven the first set quoted (Redburn) was a 2025 figure. With about $1.4bn less interest, no Gulf of America payments after 2027 (they were $1.1-1.6bn a year) and lower hybrid coupons, the dividend breakeven from 2028 is about $53-56 Brent on my arithmetic, assuming a RIM of about $9-10.
  • Credit: Fitch has BP at A+ (stable, May 2026). Morgan Stanley sees a possible net cash position in H1 2027. The credit feedback loop is now benign.
  • What remains: leases (about $14bn), hybrids ($9bn) and decommissioning provisions. These are about $23bn of claims ahead of equity that do not fall with deleveraging, against about $34bn of total financial obligations targeted for end-2026.

Two-sided risks:

  • A smaller buyback: if the 15 December 2026 framework is cautious (O'Neill has said total liabilities near $40bn were "too high for optimal resilience in a low-price environment"), the share count falls more slowly. Each $2bn a year less in buybacks costs about 1.6% of EPS a year, compounding. Finance cost improves slightly in that case.
  • A larger buyback: Shell-like 40-50% of operating cash flow, around $9-12bn in 2027. That is possible at $80 Brent without breaching $10bn of net debt, and it would cut the share count 3-4% more by 2028.

For the model: net financial items of about $3.6bn (FY2026), $2.6bn (FY2027), $2.55bn (FY2028), $2.65bn (FY2029) and $2.8bn (FY2030), excluding hybrids. Model hybrid coupons and minorities separately (about $0.87-1.16bn after tax). Model buybacks as the residual of free cash flow after the dividend, with a net debt ceiling of about $10bn.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7755P-0345
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C-556th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

Judgement: about $4bn of the $22bn operating cost base is reducible by 2027 as guided, but a large part of that is perimeter (sold businesses take their costs with them); central and procurement costs are the genuinely cuttable lines; field, refinery maintenance and safety costs are not cuttable without hurting reliability, which has already slipped.

Approximate split of the roughly $22bn of operating cost (my estimate, from unit costs and disclosures):

  • Upstream production costs (about $6/boe on about 2.3 mmboe/d, so roughly $5bn): reducible at the margin through standardisation, automation (onshore-controlled platforms) and the North Sea exit; field opex faces service inflation.
  • Refining, terminals, retail and lubricants (the largest block): Gelsenkirchen ($1bn) and Castrol leave with their businesses; site exits (10% of company-owned retail by 2027); the remaining refinery maintenance is not cuttable (Whiting outage, availability 94.7%).
  • Corporate and central functions (a few billion): cut 8% in 2025; offshoring to low-cost centres ($230m a year); total resource management ($200m of labour savings; 3,200 contractors exited); halving marketing agency spend; workforce down more than 5%.
  • Evidence of limits: about $3bn of savings absorbed by inflation and activity; management says cost cuts are not moving fast enough to reach the bottom line.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

D457th of 11

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

Judgement: little reaches the line items, and late. BP's $6.5-7.5bn end-2027 structural cost target is a gross, baseline-relative number. About $2.5bn of it is perimeter, and about $3bn of gross savings has already been absorbed by inflation and activity choices. The Q3 2026 data show that net efficiency is reaching operating cost at close to zero today. I expect a net EBITDA gain against FY2025 of about $0.4bn in FY2027 and $0.8-0.9bn in FY2028-30 (about $0.5bn after tax, roughly $0.035 a share). Almost all of it falls in H2 2027 and 2028. Further reductions are possible, but more cuts risk the reliability that recovered only in Q3.

The arithmetic of the programme:

  • Gross: $2.8bn by end-2025, $3.54bn by mid-2026, $4.0bn by Q3 2026, against a 2023 baseline of $22.6bn underlying operating expenditure. The 2025 figure was $21.9bn. About half of the first $2.8bn came from supply chain and third-party contracts; the rest was split evenly between organisational streamlining and portfolio changes, about $0.7bn of the latter.
  • Mechanical part of the raised target: Castrol $1.5bn and Gelsenkirchen $1.0bn, together $2.5bn (Morgan Stanley, 22-Sep-26). That leaves $4.0-5.0bn of genuine target, of which about $3.3bn is delivered. The rest, about $0.7-1.7bn gross, is due by end-2027.
  • Offsets so far: $3.0bn against the 2023 baseline, made up of inflation $1.5bn (2.5% a year), acquired assets $0.8bn (including the bpx energy step-up) and activity choices $0.7bn (CFO Kate Thomson, 2-Nov-26).
  • Evidence on flow-through: in Q3 2026, operating cost excluding perimeter was flat year on year (P-01). Upstream unit cost rose 6%. BP's refining cash-breakeven programme reached about 80% of its $3/bbl reduction by end-2025, through maintenance and supply-chain savings rather than disposals, so genuine savings do exist downstream. Price-adjusted free cash flow rose about 55% in 2025 and 49% in H1 2026, but much of that is capex discipline and lower finance cost, not operating cost.

What reaches the line items, and when (my estimate):

  • The remaining genuine gross savings are about $1.2bn (centre of $0.7-1.7bn) to end-2027. Some further $0.3-0.5bn could follow in 2028-29 from digital procurement (Palantir; 3,200 contractors already exited and 1,200 more planned) and the move to two segments.
  • Inflation runs about $0.5bn a year on the remaining base. Activity choices continue at bpx energy, though they buy volume and margin.
  • Net gain to EBITDA against FY2025: about +$0.4bn (FY2027), +$0.8bn (FY2028), +$0.9bn (FY2029), then flat as inflation catches up. After tax that is about $0.25bn, $0.5bn and $0.55bn. My base case includes these amounts.
  • Timing: H1 2027 shows little, because restructuring is still running and the reporting change on 1 January 2027 creates noise. The test is FY2027 absolute operating cost against the $18bn target, judged after removing perimeter.

Why less reaches profit than the headline suggests:

  • The perimeter savings leave with their EBITDA. Castrol's roughly $1.0bn of EBITDA goes with its $1.5bn of cost, and BP gets no income from its retained 35% in the short to medium term.
  • Supplier inflation is structural through 2028. Rig day rates run $375-525k, subsea margins have widened by 960 basis points since 2021, and 2027 is the largest offshore final-investment-decision year since 2013 (Redburn, 30-Oct-26). The same inflation hits capex: physical activity bought per dollar of the $13-15bn frame falls about 3-5% a year. The offsetting point is that a subsea construction vessel orderbook of 20% of the fleet (DNB Carnegie on Solstad, 5-Nov-26) may soften offshore construction costs in 2027-28.
  • Some savings are reinvested, for example in bpx energy activity. That is sensible, but it means operating cost does not fall.

Further scope, and its limits:

  • Precedents: Shell delivered $5.1bn by end-2025 and $5.8bn by mid-2026, against $5-7bn, with 57% from non-portfolio actions. Chevron delivered $3bn six months early and cut its business units from 14 to 5. ExxonMobil reports $16.9bn of cumulative savings since 2019. On Shell's ratio, BP's genuine savings could reach about $5.5bn rather than $4.0-5.0bn, a possible upgrade of about $1bn gross at the 15 December event. I do not model it.
  • Limits: process safety Tier 1 and 2 events were 18 in Q2 2026 against 5 a year earlier. Engagement is 64-66%. Q2 reliability fell to 92.4% before recovering to 95.3% in Q3. The Q3 recovery weakens the case that cost cuts have damaged capability, and on that point I am now less concerned than the first set. But BP's 2025 record reliability (96.1%) was delivered with more people, so another deep cut would reopen the risk. In 2013-15 BP removed 20% of its upstream workforce (about 10,000 people); unit costs fell, but flexibility was lost on the recovery.
  • Capex flex is the real cost lever in a downturn: bpx energy is short-cycle, and the frame can drop to $13bn. Operating cost is not.

For the model: gross programme progress is not an input. Use net EBITDA gains of +$0.4bn (FY2027) and +$0.8-0.9bn (FY2028-30) against FY2025, and remove perimeter EBITDA separately. Keep capex at $13.75bn (FY2026) and $14.0-14.5bn thereafter, because of service inflation.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4755P-0552
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?D557th of 13full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C524th of 11

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: two sets of loops run in opposite directions, and they act on different lines. Balancing loops in the commodity, refining and LNG markets dominate revenue, EBITDA and EBIT. They take about $20bn off EBITDA from FY2026 to FY2028. Reinforcing loops in the balance sheet and distributions dominate below EBIT and per share: deleveraging feeds buybacks and lower financing costs, and lower leverage improves the credit and activist positions. The working-capital buffer and the deleveraging-to-buyback loop fully cushion the dividend and the balance sheet against normalisation. They cushion EPS only partly: they offset about 10-15 points of a roughly 54% fall in EPS from FY2026 to FY2028. Disposals and depletion form a balancing loop on the earnings base. It is visible in 2027-30 as a permanent loss of about $2.6bn of EBITDA. Its larger effect, the volume gap from a 7.1-7.3 year reserve life, arrives after 2030, so it acts on the multiple rather than on the forecast.

The loops ranked by their effect on the line items, 2027-30:

  1. Commodity price (balancing; dominates revenue and EBITDA). High prices lead to demand destruction (diesel demand down about 2.5 mb/d in 2026; BP retail volumes -2% in the US and -8% in Europe in Q3), a supply response, windfall-tax pressure and, on a reopening of Hormuz, the release of stranded Gulf crude. Two forces work against the loop and set its speed. Global inventories drew 507m barrels from February 2026, and restocking demand will slow the fall. OPEC+ is likely to defend prices near $50-60 because of its fiscal breakevens (Bernstein, Jan-26). My base takes Brent from $93.5 to $80 and then $72. The loop is most likely to run fast in H2 2027.
  2. Refining capacity (balancing; the largest single EBITDA swing). Record margins bring Gulf refinery restarts, new Asian and Middle East capacity and demand loss. Morgan Stanley (5-Nov-26) expects peer refining indicators back to about $12 by late 2028. My RIM path ($31, $17, $12, $11) follows that. The loop is already starting: maintenance and restarts in Q4 2026, and product demand falling in Europe.
  3. LNG supply wave (balancing, delayed). Oil-indexed lags support H1 2027. From H2 2027 the supply wave compresses spot and optimisation margins. It is in my trading and gas assumptions, worth about -$0.3-0.6bn of EBITDA in 2028-29 against 2026-27.
  4. Deleveraging to buybacks (reinforcing; dominates EPS below EBIT). The windfall plus disposals lowered net debt ($22.2bn to about $6bn). That cut interest and hybrid coupons by about $1.2bn after tax by 2028, raised free cash flow and led to buybacks of about $5-6bn a year (base). The share count falls about 16% by 2030, and EPS and the per-share dividend capacity rise, which lowers the cost of equity (Morgan Stanley puts it at 8.5% now and sees the deleveraging lowering it). The loop runs at full strength at Brent of about $70 or more. It slows below $65 and stalls below about $58, where free cash flow only covers the dividend. Shell's precedent ran 19 quarters at $3bn or more and cut its share count 19% in three years. BP's 2020-23 precedent (net debt $40.4bn to $20.9bn) ended in buybacks funded partly by debt, transition capital and a weaker deck, ending in the February 2026 suspension. What distinguishes the loop now is that the starting debt is much lower and capital is disciplined.
  5. Working capital (balancing, counter-cyclical; cash only). It built $6.5bn in nine months of 2026, of which $2.8bn is permanent, and the Q3 price rise curtailed the guided release. About $3.7bn is price-driven and reversible. I model releases of about $3.0bn in 2027 and $1.2bn in 2028. In a $65 peace case the release would be about $5bn in 2027, cushioning the cash fall by about a quarter. The precedents: BP released $2.2bn in 2014 after a $5bn build in 2013, and Shell had positive swings of $7.5bn in Q4 2014 and Q1 2020. This is a one-off cushion, not a recurring source of cash. Counted against it in my model is BP's own working-capital line carrying the Gulf of America settlement ($4.9bn in 2027).
  6. Shrink to delever (balancing on the earnings base). Disposals remove EBITDA permanently: Castrol about $1.0bn, the North Sea about $1.3bn, Gelsenkirchen about $0.3bn, and Aker BP equity income about $0.1bn. The proceeds, of about $11-12bn, save only about $0.5bn a year pre-tax in interest. Each dollar of proceeds therefore removes about four times its interest saving in EBITDA, and about 1.5-2 times after tax, because the North Sea is taxed at 78%. Disposals are EPS-dilutive by about $0.03-0.04 a share by 2028, before buybacks. They are cash-positive and de-risking, and they shift the earnings mix away from steady earners (Castrol) toward cyclical ones (trading, refining). That mix shift widens the cone a little. The depletion half of the loop (organic reserve replacement of 39% over three years, 90% in 2025, about 76% price-adjusted; 7.1-7.3 years of reserve life) is held off to 2030 by start-ups: Kaskida in 2029 (35% complete) and Tiber in 2030 (10% complete). It does not reduce 2027-30 volumes much in my base case (2,150-2,290 kboe/d). The Bumerangue appraisal (early 2027) decides whether it bites after 2030, so it is a multiple question, not a forecast question.
  7. Service-cost inflation (balancing on costs and capex). Industry capital discipline keeps rig and vessel capacity scarce, which drives inflation, which absorbs BP's savings and buys less activity with the same capex. It is in my net cost gains (P-03) and in a capex of $14.0-14.5bn. It persists through 2028. A possible softening in subsea vessels from 2027-28 is the counter.
  8. Smaller loops. The UK tax exit lowers the tax rate by about 2 points (reinforcing, once). Credit is benign (A+; reinforcing). Elliott (about 5%) presses for distributions and costs (reinforcing, with an exit risk). Organisation: reliability recovered in Q3, so the negative loop of cuts, morale, outages and lower volume is weaker than the first set feared, but it is not closed (process safety events 18 in Q2).

Do the reinforcing loops cushion normalisation? From FY2026 to FY2028 in my base case, EBITDA falls 36%, net income 57% and EPS 54%. The reinforcing loops add about 4 points to FY2028 EPS from interest and hybrids, and about 7 points from the lower share count. Working capital adds about $4bn of cash over two years. My answer is therefore:

  • They do not cushion EPS. FY2028 EPS is about 46% of FY2026's.
  • They fully cushion the dividend and the balance sheet. Net debt stays below about $10bn even at $60.
  • They make FY2028-30 EPS of about $0.66-0.67 roughly 25-40% higher than FY2024-25 ($0.48-0.54) at a similar deck. That improvement comes from below-EBIT lines and share count, not from EBITDA.

For the model: drive revenue and EBITDA from the deck, with the balancing loops in the deck path and the RIM path. Drive EPS from the reinforcing loops: finance costs, hybrids and buybacks sized as the residual cash. Treat working capital as a price-linked release, net of the Gulf of America settlement. Treat disposals as a permanent EBITDA loss with a partial interest offset.

Guidance conservatism

Group judgement

Guidance

C-555th of 13

Layer 1 · Guidance

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

BP's guidance is specific on inputs, conservative in the near term and ambitious in the medium term. Because BP plans at $70-80 Brent while spot is above $100, FY2026 consensus is a floor and the end-2026 balance-sheet targets will very likely be beaten. The medium-term targets (ROACE above 16%, an $18bn cost base, adjusted free cash flow growth above 20% a year) are only safe at prices above $70 and depend on cost savings that have leaked before. For the Consumer: use BP's input guidance directly (capex, depreciation, tax, working capital, disposals, hybrid calls); treat the 2027 targets as stretch at a normal deck; and expect the 15 December event to replace the distribution framework, which is the largest guidance unknown.

C-68

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

D567th of 13

Layer 1 · Guidance

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement: BP does not guide profit; it guides inputs and price-adjusted targets. On the inputs, guidance and consensus agree for FY2026; on the balance sheet, guidance is far more conservative than the sell-side (the $14-18bn net debt target against broker estimates of $6.3-10.2bn); for FY2027 BP's price-adjusted targets cannot be compared with consensus, which uses a higher deck. The reason is a deliberate habit of setting achievable targets at planning prices far below spot.

FY2026:

  • Capex: guided $13.5-14.0bn; consensus $13.65bn (in line, lower half).
  • Depreciation: guided $17.0-17.5bn; consensus $17.3bn (in line).
  • Underlying tax rate: guided 35-40%; consensus implies about 38% between pre-tax profit and adjusted net income (in line).
  • Net debt excluding leases at end-2026: guided $14-18bn; Morgan Stanley $6.3bn, Redburn and Jefferies about $10bn (consensus well below the guided range; guidance is conservative).
  • Total financial obligations: guided $39-41bn by end-2026.
  • Planning deck: Brent $80 for 2026 against spot of $111 and a Q2 average near $104, which makes FY2026 consensus a floor rather than a ceiling.
  • Production: underlying flat on 2025; reported slightly lower.

FY2027:

  • Targets: ROACE above 16% at $70 Brent; adjusted free cash flow growth above 20% a year over 2024-27 on a price-adjusted basis; operating costs about $18bn; capex inside $13-15bn (consensus $13.8bn).
  • Comparison: Visible Alpha had 2027 ROACE at 10.4% in April 2026, below the target; since then FY2027 consensus EPS has risen 35% in three months on a higher deck, not on a belief in the target.
C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

F5012th of 13

Layer 1 · Guidance

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

Judgement: near-term guidance is conservative because it is set at planning prices far below spot; the mid-term targets are ambitious against BP's own five-year record (ROACE of 11-15%, flat volumes) and only become conservative if prices stay well above $70; adjusted for today's unusually strong input forces, the net reading is balanced, with the near term likely to beat and the mid-term targets at risk in a normal world.

  • Conservative: an $80 planning deck against $111 spot; a net debt target the windfall will beat; a working capital release of only $2-3bn after a $7bn build.
  • Ambitious: ROACE above 16% at $70 against 13.9% in 2025 and 15.4% average in 2023-25 at higher prices; operating cost down to $18bn while about $3bn of savings has already leaked to inflation; adjusted free cash flow growth above 20% a year, which needs about $22bn of price-adjusted free cash flow in 2027 if the FY2024 figure of $13.1bn is the base (BP's own base definition should be checked: the first half of 2026 annualised at $70 is about $20.7bn).
  • Input forces: far stronger now than over 2023-25, which makes any guidance set at $70-80 look easy for 2026-27 and leaves 2028 exposed.

That is a rational shape, but it leaves FY2027 dependent on the dislocation lasting and FY2028 slightly too high for a $70 world, mainly through optimistic finance-cost savings and retained margins. To what extent is informal or qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

Judgement: informal commentary is upbeat on operations and the portfolio and cautious on the balance sheet: it points to beating the formal net debt target and to further disposals, but warns that BP's liabilities are still too high for a low-price world, which signals a measured, not aggressive, buyback restart; that is slightly below the more bullish sell-side expectations of a strong 2027 buyback.

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

Judgement: four things are material: mid-quarter trading statements that pre-release much of the result, a heavy reliance on price-adjusted and company-defined metrics, a reporting change to two segments from 2027 that will break comparability, and a one-off strategy event on 15 December 2026 that resets the frame.

  • Implication: the bar for FY2026-27 has been raised to prices near today's; the FY2028 bar sits near normalised levels, as it should.
6c

Case simplicity

Group judgement

Risks

–46

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The predictability of BP's forecasts over the next 18 months is low where it matters most for earnings and high where it matters most for survival. The answers to the earlier sets are congruent on the shape of the case (a FY2026 peak at about $1.45 of EPS, a fade to about $0.65 by FY2028), on its engine (two external prices with a trading overlay) and on its cushion (a balance sheet repaired a year early). They are less congruent on how much the plan asks of management, and I have resolved that in the cautious direction: a smaller 2027 buyback, no credit beyond the model's for net cost savings, and a live reliability risk. The expected change rests mostly on demonstrated strengths, which makes it plausible, but its per-share leg rests on a buyback framework BP has not yet shown it can hold through a downturn. The drivers of the next 18 months are concentrated in the refining margin and Brent, which BP discloses well and controls not at all; the drivers of the plateau are diffuse and mechanical, which BP controls and discloses well. The cone of EPS outcomes is about plus or minus 45-50%, roughly balanced in probability because our base sits below the forward curve, but skewed down in speed and in consequence, and BP's beta of 1.18 to a sector trading near the top of its own range means the share price will follow the tide with a company-specific drag that has recently been negative. For the Consumer: build the model from the deck inwards; treat the balance-sheet lines as near-certain and the operating lines as scenario inputs; weight the model's FY2026 EPS at about $1.45 and its FY2028-30 EPS at $0.63-0.65; and recognise that the question of whether the forecasts are met is, over 18 months, mostly a question about the Strait of Hormuz, not about BP.

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

–58

Layer 3 · Risks

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

Judgement: the expected change, as our model states it, rests about four fifths on strengths BP has demonstrated in the last two years and one fifth on strengths it has not. The demonstrated strengths are balance-sheet execution, disposal execution, capital discipline, refining complexity and trading in volatile markets. The undemonstrated ones are converting gross cost savings into lower absolute cost, sustaining a buyback through a price downturn, delivering deepwater projects on schedule in today's supply chain, and earning above consensus in calm markets. Because our forecast asks little of the undemonstrated strengths (net cost efficiency of $0.4-0.9bn a year; no volume growth before 2029; buybacks sized to cash), the thesis is plausible. Its vulnerability is different: it relies on an acquired strength, a buyback framework that holds at $60-65 Brent, for the per-share growth that distinguishes it from a plain commodity bet, and BP has suspended buybacks twice in six years.
