Kaskida mid-2029, Tiber 2030 Partly Argos expansion delivered (2023); Kaskida 35% and Tiber 10% complete (June 2026); hull engineering on track in Singapore (EXMAR, Mar 2026); Earthjustice lawsuit against Kaskida (Apr 2026); two-year lead times on equipment Low inside 18 months; drives FY2029-30 volumes and the multiple
Buybacks of $4.5-6bn a year from 2027 sustained through normalisation No Buybacks funded partly by debt and disposals in 2022-25 were suspended in February 2026; the 2020 dividend halved; Jefferies (May 2025) expected BP to be the first major to suspend buybacks under $65 Brent. Shell ran 19 quarters at $3bn or more High: the share count supplies about 7 points of FY2028 EPS and 16 points of FY2030 EPS against a flat count
ROACE above 16% in 2027 at $70 Yes, but on a smaller base Trailing price-adjusted ROACE 17.7% at Q3 2026; 13.9% in 2025; Visible Alpha had 10.4% for 2027 (April 2026) Low for the forecast, high for the narrative

What this adds up to. The thesis as modelled leans on what BP has shown it can do: shrink, sell, repay and run a complex refining and trading system when markets are dislocated. It leans lightly on the things BP has not shown: net cost-out, calm-market earnings and project schedules. That is why the forecast is plausible. The place where it leans on an unproven strength is the one that matters most for the per-share story: a buyback that continues when Brent is $65. Here the evidence is two-sided. For: the starting point is unprecedented for BP (net debt near $6bn against $22bn a year earlier), the dividend breakeven falls to about $53-56 Brent from 2028 on the second set's arithmetic, and the board has already shown it prefers to suspend early rather than borrow to buy back. Against: the framework does not exist yet; the chief executive has signalled caution; and BP's distribution record is the worst of the European majors over six years.

Two things about the plausibility that the precedents sharpen:

  • Shell is the right template, but BP is starting later in the cycle. Shell's turnaround under Wael Sawan (2023-26) delivered $5.8bn of savings by mid-2026, 57% from non-portfolio actions, and 19 quarters of $3bn-plus buybacks from a balance sheet that was already at about 19% gearing. BP in late 2026 has the balance sheet Shell had, but its cost delivery is perimeter-heavy, and it is restarting distributions at the top of a margin cycle rather than in the middle of one. The precedent says the buyback can compound; it also says Shell's compounding was helped by starting while margins were normal, so the market never had to test the framework against a falling deck in its first year. BP's will be tested in its first year.
  • Chevron and ExxonMobil show what "structural savings" mean. Chevron hit $3bn six months early by collapsing 14 upstream units into 5; ExxonMobil reports $16.9bn of cumulative savings while its absolute cash operating cost rose. BP's $6.5-7.5bn target includes $2.5bn of perimeter; its segment collapse (three to two) is the Chevron move, made in July 2026; the proof will be FY2027 absolute operating cost against about $18bn after removing perimeter. The precedents say the headline will be met and the net effect will be smaller than the headline. Our model already assumes that.
D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?–42full answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

–35

Layer 3 · Risks

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

Judgement: the cone is wide, about plus or minus 45-50% around base EPS for FY2027 and FY2028, and it is skewed in two different ways that the Consumer must keep apart. In probability it is roughly balanced around our base, because our base deck ($80 and $72 Brent; $17 and $12 RIM) already sits below the forward curve and below the most constructive brokers. In speed and in consequence it is skewed down: the bear path needs one political event and re-prices the deck within days (Brent fell 16% in three days to 4 November), while the bull path needs a disruption to outlast restocking and refinery maintenance, and reveals itself only quarter by quarter. Beneath the earnings cone sits a much narrower cone for the balance sheet, the dividend and the per-share mechanics, which is skewed favourably. The case is therefore of low predictability on operating profit, high predictability on the balance sheet, and medium predictability on EPS, where the two meet.

The cone, with the evidence behind each edge (FY2027 and FY2028 EPS; model basis, so about 2-3 cents below the second set's figures):

Path What it needs Deck FY2027 / FY2028 EPS FY2027 / FY2028 Probability over 18 months (my estimate)
Bear: reopening and a settlement in H1 2027 A US-Iran agreement; Aramco back to 12 mb/d capacity within three weeks (Aramco, 3 Nov 2026); a 2 mb/d surplus in 2027 (Deutsche Bank, 29 Oct); Gulf refineries restart; OPEC+ defends share rather than price Brent $65 / $62; RIM $11 / $9; trading normal about $0.50 / $0.43 30%
Base: gradual reopening through 2027, restocking slows the fall The Strait reopens in stages; the 507-509m barrel inventory draw is rebuilt over up to two years; refinery maintenance of 3.4-6.4 mb/d in Q2 2027 holds product cracks into mid-2027 $80 / $72; RIM $17 / $12 $0.87 / $0.65 45%
Bull: disruption into late 2027 The Strait stays impaired beyond mid-2027 (Iranian position on 8 Nov; tanker fires in early Nov); Middle East refineries stay damaged; the forward curve ($88-92 Brent, $28 RIM for 2027) proves right $95 / $82; RIM $28 / $16; trading +$1.5bn about $1.33 / $0.90 25%

Why the probability skew is roughly balanced while the consequence skew is negative:

  • Our base is below the market on both prices. Morgan Stanley (5 November, after the Brent fall) still has 2027 Brent at $86.20 and BP's RIM at $27.7; the curve is higher. If the market is right, FY2027 EPS is nearer $1.10-1.20 than $0.87. That is the upside half of the cone, and it is not a tail: it is the forward curve.
  • The downside arrives faster than the upside. The 16% three-day fall in Brent, the comparable moves of 1990-91, Singapore margins falling from $30 to $9 in six months, and diesel cracks falling from $34 to $21 in two months in early 2023 (Jefferies) all show that dislocation premia unwind in weeks. Restocking and maintenance slow the physical normalisation, but the equity market prices the forward deck, not the physical balance. For the share price, the cone's downside is immediate and its upside is gradual.
  • The downside is amplified by three BP-specific gearings that the sector does not share equally: the highest refining weight among European majors (28% of FY2026 EBITDA from refining and trading against 9% historically), the largest trading book (earnings surprises above 10% in two thirds of quarters), and upstream realisation lags that turn negative in the quarter of the steepest price fall (worth perhaps -$0.3-0.6bn in Q2 or Q3 2027).
  • Tail events outside the deck widen the cone a little further, mostly downward: a Whiting-type outage ($0.5bn in a quarter in 2024), a Rotterdam repeat (October 2026), a trading loss (TotalEnergies' $0.8bn gas-trading swing in Q2 2026), a European windfall levy ($505m in 2022; 5-10% of free cash flow in 2022-23 per Morgan Stanley), a weather hit in the Gulf (20 kboe/d in Q4 2026). The upside tails are the Venture Global award ($3.7bn to above $6bn claimed; liability found; damages hearing May 2027, award unlikely before late 2027; Venture Global argues a $765m cap) and a Shell-sized buyback framework.

The narrow cone underneath. Whatever the deck, within 18 months: net debt stays below about $10bn in every path (about $7bn in the bear path with buybacks cut to $2.5bn a year); hybrids fall to $9bn on published call dates; finance cost falls to about $2.6-2.8bn; the dividend grows 4% and is covered down to about $53-56 Brent from 2028; the Gulf of America payable shrinks. These lines have a cone of perhaps plus or minus 10%. The working-capital release (about $3bn in 2027 in the base, about $5bn in the bear path) and production-sharing entitlement volumes cushion the cash fall in the bear path by about a quarter. This is the part of the case that is predictable, and it is the part the sector precedents (C-84) reward least in the share price, because "a step change in volume is the easiest thing to extrapolate" and BP has none.

Where the two cones meet: EPS. The share count and the finance line add roughly 10-15 points to FY2028 EPS against a flat count and flat interest; the deck can subtract or add 35%. So EPS is more predictable than EBIT but far less than the balance sheet. The forecast is predictable in shape (a fade), unpredictable in slope, and predictable again in the floor (a covered dividend and a net debt that does not rise).

What would narrow the cone over the next six months, in order of importance: the Strait's status and the shape of the Brent curve (daily); the Q4 2026 RIM and BP's Q4 trading statement (mid-January 2027); the 15 December framework (buyback size and trigger); the Q2 2027 refinery maintenance outcome; and the Bumerangue flow test (early 2027), which affects the multiple more than the 18-month forecasts.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

–56

Layer 3 · Risks

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

Judgement: unusually helpful on the external transmission and on the balance sheet, and poor on exactly the three internal lines our thesis depends on. BP publishes its rules of thumb, quarterly production and throughput ranges, the working-capital outlook, the hybrid path, the total-financial-obligations target and a mid-quarter trading statement, so the Consumer can track the deck's effect on earnings and the deleveraging almost in real time. BP does not disclose trading profit, realised refining capture by region, the bridge from gross cost savings to operating cost, the Castrol preference terms, the Gulf of America settlement schedule or the buyback trigger, and from 1 January 2027 it re-bases its segments, which breaks the comparison exactly when the fade begins. Monitoring the next six months is therefore easy for the things that will go right and hard for the things that could go wrong.

What can be monitored, and from what:

Driver of the thesis Disclosure Frequency and next date Usefulness
Deck transmission (Brent, RIM, Henry Hub) Rules of thumb ($340m, $450m, $40m per unit), updated for Gelsenkirchen; realisation data by region each quarter Quarterly; trading statement about 13-14 January 2027; Q4 results about 10 February 2027 High. The trading statement pre-releases margins, realisations and the direction of trading, so the result-day surprise is small
Net debt, total financial obligations, hybrids Hard year-end targets ($33-35bn total obligations; hybrids to about $9bn by end-2027 with named call dates); quarterly net debt Quarterly High. The best-disclosed line and the most predictable
Capex, depreciation, tax rate Ranges reconfirmed each quarter ($13.5-14.0bn; $17.0-17.5bn; 31-34%) Quarterly High for 2026; 2027 ranges due on 15 December
Production and throughput Quarterly ranges (Q4 2026: 2,090-2,230 kboe/d; maintenance cutting throughput by about 200 kb/d) Quarterly High for volumes; the ranges are wide (about 7%)
Working capital Guided release ($0.5bn in Q4 2026); split of the nine-month build into seasonal, timing and permanent ($2.8bn) Quarterly Medium to high; the permanent part is explained
Structural cost reductions Cumulative gross figure against a 2023 baseline ($4.0bn at Q3); offsets disclosed once ($3.0bn, CFO, 2 Nov 2026) Quarterly for the gross figure Low for the thesis. The gross figure is not an earnings input; absolute operating cost excluding perimeter is, and it must be computed
Reliability and safety Plant reliability and refining availability each quarter; Tier 1 and 2 events Quarterly Medium. Useful leading indicator of the cost programme's side effects
Trading Qualitative only ("exceptional", "strong", "weaker"); no profit figure; about 4 points of ROACE uplift on average Quarterly words Low. Morgan Stanley (12 Jan 2026): investors "struggle to assess normalisation risk"
Refining capture Not disclosed; brokers estimate (64-70%) None Low
Castrol retained 35% Preference on distributions stated; terms, rate and duration undisclosed; two-year lock-up None until the venture files accounts Low; the income is zero, so for the forecast it does not matter
Gulf of America Payable ($5.94bn), current portion ($1.13bn), deferred tax asset ($886m), guided pre-tax payments ($1.6bn 2026, $1.2bn 2027); settlement method and schedule undisclosed Quarterly balances; 15 December may add the schedule Medium
Buyback None; framework on 15 December 15 December 2026 The gap that matters most for the share count
Segments Three segments to Q4 2026; two from Q1 2027, with restated history "due in early 2027" Q1 2027 results, about May 2027 Negative for six months: the first two-segment quarter has no clean comparator, and gas and power trading moves into Upstream

The non-obvious points:

  • The disclosure is asymmetric in the direction of our risk. The lines that will show progress (debt, hybrids, obligations, capex) are the best disclosed. The lines where the thesis could fail (trading normalising faster, capture falling below 50%, cost savings leaking, Castrol and North Sea earnings leaving) are the least disclosed, and the segment change obscures them further. The Consumer should expect the first half of 2027 to look better in the disclosed metrics than in the undisclosed ones.
  • The mid-quarter trading statement moves the price reaction forward but also reduces the information in the result. For monitoring it is good; for anyone waiting to react on results day it means the surprise is already spent. BP's shares fell 5% on the day of its Q2 2026 beat.
  • Company-defined metrics multiply. Price-adjusted free cash flow, price-adjusted ROACE (17.7% trailing at Q3 2026), three gearing definitions and total financial obligations are each useful, but each requires the Consumer to rebuild the bridge to the brokers' basis. The 15 December event is likely to add new ones.
  • Peers set the bar higher on the lines BP hides. Shell discloses that about 80% of its LNG portfolio is long-term contracted and oil-indexed; BP publishes no indexation split. TotalEnergies gives a tax-rate range by oil price ($80-90 Brent: 45-50%); BP guides one year at a time.

For the next six months the monitoring list, in order, is: the Brent and product curves; the January trading statement (Q4 RIM, trading direction, working capital); the 15 December framework (buyback size, trigger, guardrail, 2027 opex and production guidance); Q4 results (absolute operating cost for FY2026 against about $20.8bn, net debt against about $6bn, impairments on the North Sea and Lightsource); the North Sea sale announcement (proceeds $2.0-2.5bn expected; retained decommissioning); and the Bumerangue flow test.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

–55

Layer 3 · Risks

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

Judgement: the external transmission is among the clearest in the sector: BP publishes the three sensitivities that explain about 90% of the variance in its pre-tax profit, and the AlphaSense evidence confirms that brokers reproduce them. The internal traceability splits. The balance-sheet commitments trace cleanly to identifiable lines (interest, hybrid coupons, share count) and to EPS; the operating commitments trace poorly, because BP states them in forms that do not map onto the profit and loss account: a gross cost figure against a 2023 baseline that includes sold businesses, a return target on a capital base that shrinks with impairments and disposals, and a 2030 production range that the North Sea sale has already made stale. The result is that the parts of the plan the Consumer can trace to EPS are the parts that add 10-15 points to it, and the parts that would add more cannot be traced at all.

The company's stated commitments and where each lands:

What BP says it will achieve Operational driver Line in the accounts Traceability to EPS Comment
Net debt below $14-18bn (met), total financial obligations $33-35bn by end-2026 Disposals, working-capital release, hybrid redemptions, operating cash Net finance cost; hybrid coupons (deducted before profit to shareholders) Clear: about $1.4bn lower interest and $0.25bn lower coupons by FY2028, about $0.08 a share The floor is also traceable: $0.70bn lease interest and about $0.95bn unwinding of discount do not fall
Hybrids to about $9bn by end-2027 Named calls (£1.25bn March 2027) Hybrid coupons Clear, about 1.5 cents a share S&P equity treatment preserved at $9bn
Structural cost reductions $6.5-7.5bn by end-2027 (gross, 2023 baseline) Supply chain, headcount, portfolio Production and manufacturing expense Poor. $2.5bn is Castrol and Gelsenkirchen, which take their revenue with them; $3.0bn of offsets have absorbed the rest to date The traceable milestone is absolute operating cost of about $18bn in 2027 (about $21.9bn in 2025); our base has $19.0bn and $18.3bn in 2028
ROACE above 16% in 2027 at $70, $4 Henry Hub, $10.30 RIM Profit and capital employed Both; capital employed falls with $4.0bn (Q3) and about $6bn (2025) of impairments and about $15bn of disposals Misleading if read as a profit target: our model gives 17-18% at that deck mainly from the smaller denominator A beat here is not EPS
Production 2.3-2.5 mboe/d by 2030; underlying flat 2026-27 Ten start-ups (about 250 kboe/d peak) through 2027; bpx; Kaskida 2029, Tiber 2030 Upstream volumes and depreciation Clear in principle, stale in fact: the North Sea (85-117 kboe/d) is for sale and is inside the 2.3-2.5 range; reported 2026 production is 2,210-2,250 against 2,312 in 2025 A re-based range is likely on 15 December
Capex $13-15bn a year; low-carbon under $0.8bn Project phasing, farm-downs Capex, then depreciation Clear Farm-down delays (Kaskida, Tiber) already moved 2026 capex up
Dividend growth at least 4% a year Cash Dividend Clear The first call on cash
$20bn of disposals by end-2027 North Sea, Archaea, Lightsource partner, retail Disposal proceeds; lost EBITDA Clear on cash, poor on earnings removed: BP does not state the EBITDA of what it sells Castrol about $1.0bn and Gelsenkirchen about $0.3bn of EBITDA are broker estimates
Buyback framework (15 December) Surplus cash Share count Will be clear once stated Today untraceable

The external transmission, which is clear:

  • Three sensitivities carry the load. $340m of pre-tax profit per $1 of Brent, $450m per $1 of RIM (from $550m after Gelsenkirchen), $40m per 10 cents of Henry Hub. On our deck these three explain the FY2026-27 EBITDA fall almost exactly (refining -$6.3bn, Brent -$4.6bn, trading -$1.7bn, perimeter -$0.8bn of -$13.8bn). The AlphaSense drivers report and every broker note use the same numbers. After tax at 36-37% and over about 15bn shares, $10 of Brent is about $0.14 of EPS and $5 of RIM about $0.10: the Consumer can convert any deck into EPS on one line.
  • The mechanics that shape timing are disclosed or inferable: one- to two-month upstream realisation lags (worth $1.8-2.1bn in Q2 2026, reversing in the quarter of the steepest fall), production-sharing entitlement that raises volumes at lower prices, a roughly nine-month lag on oil-indexed LNG (supporting H1 2027 gas realisations), and the inventory-holding effect, which BP strips out of underlying profit.
  • Two external links are opaque. Trading has no disclosed profit, so its roughly 2-4 points of ROACE uplift, and the question of whether 2026's "exceptional" result normalises to zero or stays positive, cannot be traced. Refining capture (the gap between realised margin and the indicator, $5/bbl in early 2026, 64-70% capture in 2026) is a broker construct, and its behaviour at a $12 indicator, after the sale of 17% of capacity, is unknown.

Two implications. First, the Consumer's model should be built from the outside in: deck, sensitivities, perimeter, then the balance-sheet lines, with the company's operating targets used only as checks. That is how the second set built it, and it is the right architecture. Second, the 15 December event should be judged on whether it converts the untraceable commitments into traceable ones: an absolute operating cost target by segment on the new basis, a re-based production range excluding the North Sea, a buyback expressed as a share of a defined cash flow with a stated guardrail, and a schedule for the Gulf of America payable. If it does, D-05 and D-06 improve; if it adds new company-defined metrics instead, they do not.

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

D338th of 13

Layer 1 · Risks

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

Judgement: sentiment risks are high: BP is a complex, levered, cyclical case near its 52-week high, in a market regime that the house reads as risk-off and fragility-averse; any peace headline, oil fall, windfall-tax headline or disappointment at the 15 December event could take 10-20% off the shares quickly.

  • Regime: Momentum Underweight and Fragility Double Underweight (23 October 2026); European defensives leading cyclicals on all five windows; energy positioning at the 69th percentile; the house's "record-margin trap" view that upstream returns are already paid for.
  • Peace or de-escalation: the single biggest sector-wide shock; the Fitness for Fighting note says that under $85 Brent the energy rows "lose their reason".
  • Events elsewhere: Shell's and TotalEnergies' third-quarter prints (due 29-30 October); a sector rotation out of energy; French and Italian sovereign stress hitting European risk premia.
  • BP-specific: the stock fell 5% on the day of its strong Q2 beat (the market sells good news it sees as peak); a buyback framework at the 15 December event below the market's hopes; Elliott selling once its agenda is met; governance headlines.
  • Mitigant: short interest only about 0.4% of free float, so there is no crowded short to fuel a squeeze either way; hedge-fund long-to-short ratio of 2 times (August).

C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

D528th of 13

Layer 1 · Guidance

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

Judgement: under the new team BP upgrades the targets it controls through portfolio and balance-sheet actions (the cost target twice, the debt timeline once) and quietly loosens the ones that cost money (capex up, divestment proceeds for 2026 down, tax rate walked back); under previous teams the pattern was resets and reversals. It is too early to call O'Neill a habitual "guide low, beat" chief executive, but the first signs point that way.

  • Upgrades: cost target from $4-5bn to $5.5-6.5bn to $6.5-7.5bn (the last step mechanically from Gelsenkirchen); net debt target pulled forward a year; total financial obligations given a hard year-end number.
  • Loosening: capex from $13.0-13.5bn to $13.5-14.0bn; 2026 divestment proceeds to $8-9bn; underlying tax rate to 35-40%.
  • History: the $49bn 2025 EBITDA target retired; the 2020 strategy reversed; the distribution framework of 30-40% of operating cash flow retired; buybacks suspended after earlier promises; earlier, under the previous chief executive as finance director, net debt fell from $40.4bn (Q3 2020) to $20.9bn (end-2023) ahead of target and $2.5bn of cost savings arrived six months early.
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

D5010th of 13

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

Judgement: BP beats when markets are volatile and margins high, and misses when markets are calm; the last year is a run of beats on exactly those conditions, the three-year record is poor, and on balance-sheet guidance BP has a good record of delivering early.

  • Last 12 months: Q1 2026 underlying profit of $3.20bn against $2.67bn consensus (+20%), led by trading in Customers and Products; Q2 2026 $5.73bn against $5.11bn (+12%), led by refining; Q3 2026 trading update "very strong". But Q1 2026 operating cash flow before working capital missed by 4% (Jefferies), and Gas and Low Carbon Energy and Oil Production and Operations missed in Q1.
  • Three years: consensus missed in eight quarters between 2023 and 2025 (Morgan Stanley; other sources say six consecutive); the 2023-25 performance share plan vested at 23.3% with zero vesting on return and shareholder-return metrics.
  • Five years and comparable conditions: in the 2022 price spike BP delivered record operating cash flow of $40.9bn; net debt fell from $40.4bn (Q3 2020) to $20.9bn (end-2023) ahead of target; FY2025 organic capex of $13.6bn under its original $14.5bn guide.
  • For the model: in a high-volatility 2026-27, a modest positive bias to consensus is reasonable; in a calm 2028, history argues for a negative bias.
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

D508th of 11

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

Judgement: weight the forecasts according to who controls each line, not by how far out the year is. BP's record splits cleanly. It delivers early on the lines management controls through portfolio and balance-sheet actions (net debt, disposals, hybrids, capex discipline). It beats in volatile, high-margin periods and misses in calm ones. It persistently under-delivers on operating lines that depend on execution (volumes, net cost reduction, reliability, avoiding impairments). The lean that follows: FY2026 up, H1 2027 slightly up, H2 2027-FY2028 operating profit down about 5% against a deck-consistent forecast, and the balance-sheet and per-share lines up. Overall weight: high for FY2026, medium for FY2027, low to medium for FY2028-30, where the deck alone moves EPS by ±35%.

The record, by category:

Category Record (dated evidence) Read for the forecast
Balance sheet and portfolio Net debt $40.4bn (Q3 2020) to $20.9bn (end-2023), ahead of target. 2026 net debt target hit five quarters early ($13.87bn at Q3). Castrol closed on time (2 Nov 2026). Hybrid redemptions done as guided ($2.9bn Q2, $1.0bn Q3) High weight. Lean favourable: net debt and total obligations will come in below guidance
Profit in volatile markets Q1 2026 +20% against consensus, Q2 +12%, Q3 about +15%. 2022: record operating cash flow of $40.9bn Lean up while volatility lasts (FY2026, H1 2027)
Profit in calm markets Consensus missed in eight quarters between mid-2023 and early 2025 (Morgan Stanley). The 2023-25 performance share plan vested at 23.3% Lean down when calm returns (H2 2027 onwards)
Operating cost (net) Gross savings ahead of plan. Net savings not reaching profit (CEO and CFO, Aug and Oct 2026). Ex-perimeter operating cost flat in Q3 2026 Lean down on any forecast that books gross savings
Volumes 2026 reported production cut to 2,210-2,250 kboe/d (2025: 2,312). Q4 guide lowered to 2,090-2,230. Redburn trimmed its 2026 production 2.5% for delays. On the other side, seven start-ups in 2025, five ahead of schedule Lean down by about 1-2% of volume a year

Guidance style Plans at $70-80 Brent while spot is $95-111. Cost target raised twice, partly mechanically. 2026 tax-rate guide cut to 31-34% Consensus is a floor in high-price years

How much weight, year by year:

  • FY2026 (high weight; lean up against the pre-Q3 consensus). Nine months are reported (underlying $17.4bn). Q4 is the only unknown: I estimate $5.5bn, with a range of $4.5-6.5bn depending on Q4 RIM and trading. FY2026 EPS is about $1.46, against $1.37 FactSet consensus (25-Oct-26, before Q3) and Redburn's $1.43 after Q3.
  • FY2027 (medium weight). H1 2027 carries lingering volatility, the oil-indexed LNG lag and restocking, so lean slightly up on H1. H2 2027 turns on the timing of the reopening. Lean down there on operating lines, up on per-share lines (buyback restart) and up on finance cost. My base EPS of $0.90 sits above the FactSet median of $0.856 and close to the mean of $0.898. After the Q3 beat, consensus will move up toward my number.
  • FY2028-30 (low to medium weight). The deck explains almost all of the broker range for FY2028 EPS ($0.21-0.93). Redburn at a flat $70 Brent has $0.60. Morgan Stanley at $75 has $0.77. On BP's record in calm years, lean my deck-consistent operating profit down by about 5% (about $1bn of EBIT, about $0.04 a share). Lean the share count down, meaning more buybacks than consensus, because the balance sheet allows it and BP has always delivered on balance-sheet actions. These two leans roughly offset at the EPS line. I keep base FY2028 EPS at $0.67, with a skewed cone of $0.45-0.92.
  • Project timing (Kaskida 2029, Tiber 2030). On industry precedent, supplier tightness (two-year lead times on equipment) and the Earthjustice lawsuit against Kaskida, lean each start-up about 6-12 months late. That costs about 15-25 kboe/d in FY2029-30, or about $0.3-0.5bn of EBITDA. It is not in my base case, but it is a downside lean.

On the first set: it said "weight the plan's financial lines at face value and haircut its operating lines". I agree, and I sharpen it. Lean the financial lines favourably, not just at face value, because BP is beating its own balance-sheet guidance by a wide margin.

Financial forecasts

D507th of 11

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

BP's financial forecast has two halves, and the Consumer should test them separately. The top half (revenue, EBITDA, EBIT) is a commodity forecast. A FY2026 peak (EBITDA $56.3bn, EPS $1.46) fades to a FY2028-30 plateau of EBITDA about $35-36bn, slightly below FY2024-25 because about $2.6bn of perimeter EBITDA has been sold. Refining normalisation is the largest swing, Brent second and trading third. Net cost efficiency contributes under $1bn, because the guided fall in operating cost is mostly perimeter. That half is unpredictable, skewed down over 18 months, and owned by geopolitics, not management. The bottom half (finance cost, hybrids, tax, Gulf of America, share count) is a balance-sheet forecast. It improves reliably and early: net debt about $6bn at end-2026, finance cost down about $1.4bn, hybrids down to $9bn, Gulf of America settled in 2027, the tax rate about 3 points lower, and buybacks of about $4.5-6bn a year cutting the share count about 16% by 2030. That half is predictable, management-controlled, and is where BP's record is good. The result is FY2028-30 EPS of about $0.66-0.67, 25-40% above FY2024-25 at a similar deck, and a dividend covered down to about $53-56 Brent. I disagree with three of the first set's conclusions. Normalised earnings are higher than its $7.5-9.5bn range. Consensus FY2028 is not 10-20% too high, except at a deck below $70. Distributable cash after the dividend at $70 is about $4-5bn, not $0-2bn. These conclusions changed because the first set was written before the Q3 results, the Castrol close and the Gulf of America acceleration, and because it did not bridge depreciation and tax. For the next sets: test predictability line by line. The deck lines are low-predictability and skewed down; the balance-sheet lines are high-predictability and skewed favourably. Expect guidance and consensus moves to be dominated by the deck in FY2027-28 and by the 15 December buyback framework on the per-share lines.

P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

A641st of 11

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement: below operating profit is where BP's forecast improves most reliably. In FY2025 only 41% of EBIT reached shareholders ($18.3bn of EBIT, $7.49bn of net income), squeezed by $4.0bn of finance cost, a tax rate of about 40% and hybrid coupons. By FY2028 I expect about 50% ($19.5bn of EBIT, $9.8bn of net income) at a similar EBIT. On FY2025's retention that is worth about $1.8bn of net income, about $0.12 a share. Buybacks then add about 7% to FY2028 EPS and about 16% to FY2030 EPS against a flat share count. For free cash flow, the largest change is the end of Gulf of America payments after a lump settlement in 2027. That is a cash outflow of about $4.9bn in 2027, then about $1.1-1.6bn a year more cash than in 2023-26.

Line by line (my base case):

  • Tax. The underlying rate was about 40% in FY2025. Guidance for 2026 is now 31-34%, after Q3's 28% on a downstream-heavy mix. I model 32.5% (FY2026), 36% (FY2027) and 37% (FY2028-30). The rate rises as the downstream share normalises, because upstream earnings are taxed more heavily (production-sharing and petroleum regimes). It falls about 2 points from the UK exit (78% marginal rate) and from a greater US weight. This disagrees with consensus: its implied leakage from pre-tax profit to net income is about 45% in FY2027-28, which suggests brokers carry about 40% or more. If I am right, that is a modest upside to consensus of about $0.5bn of net income. Each point of tax rate is worth about $0.17bn of net income in FY2028, about $0.012 a share.
  • Interest. $3.98bn (2025), then $3.6bn, $2.6bn, $2.55bn, $2.65bn and $2.8bn. It edges up in 2029-30 as buybacks lift net debt toward $8-10bn. The floor is about $1.65bn (P-02).
  • Hybrid coupons and other minorities. $1.16bn (2026), $0.95bn (2027), then $0.87bn a year: hybrids at $9bn at about 5.8% (about $0.52bn), plus about $0.35bn of other minority interests.
  • One-offs and adjusting items. These recur: about $6bn of impairments in 2025, $5.9bn of net adverse pre-tax adjusting items in Q3 2026 (including $4.0bn of post-tax impairments), and probable further items on the sales of the North Sea, Archaea and Lightsource. They do not affect the brokers'-basis EPS. They do affect reported EPS, which will stay well below underlying, and book equity. Cash restructuring costs sit outside underlying but are real cash. I include them in the "other operating cash" carrier (see the forecast-inputs bridge).
  • Minority investments. The retained 35% of Castrol pays no income in the short to medium term because of Stonepeak's preferred distributions. Its terms are undisclosed and are a research gap. The 5% Aker BP sale cuts equity income by about $0.1bn. Azule's Greater PAJ starts in 2028 (95 kboe/d gross). Equity-accounted income is larger than the dividends received, which is one reason BP's operating cash flow runs well below brokers' EBITDA less tax and interest.
  • Gulf of America. The payable was $5.9bn at Q3 2026, and BP accelerated the Louisiana payments, calling the remainder the first call on excess cash. I assume about $1.0bn in Q4 2026 and about $4.9bn in 2027, then zero. These payments sit in BP's working-capital line, so they are outside underlying profit but inside operating cash flow. The unwinding of discount on the payable also stops, which lowers finance costs by about $0.15-0.2bn.
  • Share count. Weighted basic was 15.56bn and diluted 15.71bn in Q3 2026. The 155m gap is employee plans and dilutes EPS by about 1%. Brokers' EPS is on basic shares. Non-buyback issuance is about +50m a year. Base buybacks: $6.0bn (2027; I assume a restart in February 2027 with the FY2026 results, at about $1.5bn a quarter), $5.5bn (2028), and $4.5bn a year (2029-30). At about $7.5-8.0 a share, year-end shares are 15.56bn, 14.86bn, 14.17bn, 13.62bn and 13.07bn. Morgan Stanley's $0.75bn a quarter from Q3 2027 is the low end of the plausible range. A Shell-type framework (about $3bn a quarter) is the high end. Why I am above Morgan Stanley: end-2026 net debt of about $6bn against the old $14-18bn range; total financial obligations below $35bn; the restart conditions management set in February 2026 have been met; and a buyback is worth two to three times as much to EPS as further deleveraging at the current price (P-02). Why I am below the high end: O'Neill's stated caution on liabilities, settling the Gulf of America payable first, capex of $14bn or more, and the hybrid redemptions still to come ($3.1bn in 2027).

The result against history:

Points of insight for the Consumer:

  • EPS in FY2028-30 holds near $0.66-0.67 at $70-72 Brent while net income drifts down. That is the buyback loop at work. Net income falls about 8% from FY2028 to FY2030 on a flat-to-lower deck, inflation and higher interest. The share count falls about 8% over the same period.
  • Free cash flow is lower than earnings suggest in 2027 because of the Gulf of America lump. It is then structurally better than in 2023-25 at a similar deck, because the claims ahead of equity have shrunk: Gulf of America, interest and hybrids together are about $3bn a year lower by 2028 than in 2025. This, not operating improvement, is what makes a buyback of about $4.5-5.5bn affordable at $70-72.
  • The FCF-to-net-income relationship flips. In 2023-25 free cash flow (after leases and hybrids) was below what EBITDA implied, because about $4bn a year of cash leaked between brokers' EBITDA and operating cash flow (2025: EBITDA $36.6bn, operating cash flow before working capital $22.7bn). I model a structural leak of about $2.5bn a year from 2027. If the true structural leak is the 2025 level, my free cash flow is about $1.5bn a year too high and the buyback about $1.5bn too large. I flag this as a research gap.

Judgement: my base case is a normalisation from a FY2026 peak (EPS $1.46) to a FY2028-30 plateau of about $0.66-0.67, on a deck of Brent $80, $72 and $70 and a RIM of $17, $12 and $11. My base is close to consensus for FY2027-28, slightly above it on FY2026 and FY2029, and differs from it in composition. The cone around FY2027 EPS ($0.52-1.36) and FY2028 EPS ($0.45-0.92) is as wide as the broker range and skewed down from my base. The deck is the single variable that matters; every other driver is second-order.

The skew over 18 months is downward from my base. The bear case needs only a political event, and it is the outcome the sector notes describe as "every path normalises margins from 2027" (record-margin trap, 16-Oct-26). The bull case needs a disruption to outlast restocking and demand loss. The cone is roughly symmetric in probability-weighted EPS around consensus, but asymmetric in the speed of moves: a peace headline re-prices the deck within weeks.

What is most and least predictable in my base case:

  • Most predictable (management-controlled, mechanical): net debt, hybrids, finance cost, the Gulf of America settlement, the dividend (at least +4% a year), depreciation and capex.
  • Moderately predictable: the tax rate (mix), perimeter timing (North Sea completion in 2027 is my assumption) and the size of the buyback (to be set on 15 December 2026).
  • Least predictable: Brent, RIM, refining capture, trading and the bottom-line effect of net cost savings. These are the five lines that make up about 90% of the forecast variance.

At 624p ($8.37), my base EPS puts the shares on about 5.7x FY2026, 9.3x FY2027 and 12.5x FY2028. That is consistent with the first set's observation that the 24-month-forward P/E sits near the top of its five-year range.

C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

D626th of 13

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

Judgement: there is meaningful scope, about $1.5bn to $2.5bn of post-tax annual improvement on a normalised base by 2028 plus a share-count benefit, and it comes from a few big, mechanical areas (financing costs, share count, perimeter quality, reliability) rather than many small ones; Shell's 2023-26 record shows it is achievable, and it is fully congruent with the five stated priorities.

The big areas:

  1. Financing: net debt to about $10-14bn and hybrids to $9bn save roughly $0.7-0.8bn pre-tax a year against 2025.
  2. Share count: buybacks restarted in 2027 at, say, $1-1.5bn a quarter would cut shares by 3-5% a year at today's price; Shell cut 19% in three years.
  3. Reliability: back to 96% from 92.4% recovers about 4% of upstream volume against the 2026 run-rate.
  4. Tax mix: exiting the 78% UK regime and shifting weight to the US.
  5. Projects: 10 start-ups (about 250 kboe/d peak) through 2027 and BPX growth hold volumes flat to up despite the North Sea sale.
  6. Capital efficiency: farm-downs of Kaskida and Tiber at full value recover capital; capex held flat in nominal terms.

Precedent: Shell delivered $5.8bn of savings by mid-2026 (three years early), 57% from non-portfolio activities, and nineteen quarters of $3bn-plus buybacks; Chevron delivered $3bn of structural cuts early; but both started from stronger balance sheets.

C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

D559th of 13

Layer 1 · Risks

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

Judgement: there has been no deceleration in the last twelve months; revenue is growing far above BP's normal (roughly zero) rate because the input force, oil and product prices, is accelerating; the risk is the reverse of deceleration: a growth rate so far above normal that it must revert. The self-help engine is running at a steady pace that needs to speed up to hit its target.

  • Revenue: Q2 2026 +48% year on year; FY2026 consensus +26%; underlying volumes flat (Q2 2026 2,201 kboe/d against 2,300 a year earlier, partly Middle East disruption).
  • Input force: Brent from about $69 (2025) to $81 (Q1 2026), $104 (Q2) and $111 (22 October); refining margin from $11.90 (Q2 2025) to $29.60 (Q2 2026).
  • Self-help pace: $2.8bn of structural savings by end-2025, $3.54bn by mid-2026 ($782m in the first half, about $1.6bn a year). Reaching $6.5-7.5bn by end-2027 needs about $2.0-2.6bn a year, of which about $1bn is the one-off Gelsenkirchen effect; excluding it, the required pace is close to the current pace.

Judgement: consensus has FY2026 at or near BP's 2022 peak, FY2027 at about 60% of FY2026 but still about 70% above FY2025, and FY2028 about 35% above FY2025. Granular credibility tests:

  • FY2026: first-half underlying profit of $8.93bn requires about $12.4bn in the second half; at $111 Brent and a "very strong" Q3, credible.
  • FY2027: $12.9bn needs roughly an $80-85 Brent deck and refining margins well above planning; plausible if the dislocation lasts into 2027, not if a peace arrives in early 2027.
  • FY2028: $10.1bn sits above my normalised range of $7.5-9.5bn and above the roughly $7-8bn implied by BP's own 16% ROACE target at $70; brokers' EBITDA of $38.2bn is above FY2024-25 ($36.6-37.8bn) despite Castrol, Gelsenkirchen and the North Sea leaving.
  • Finance costs: a fall from about $4bn to $2.1bn by FY2028 is large; net debt repayment of about $10-12bn saves about $0.5-0.6bn and hybrid redemptions do not reduce interest expense; part of BP's finance cost is the unwinding of discount on provisions. This is the line most likely to be too optimistic.
  • Below pre-tax profit: consensus leakage of about 45% (tax, minorities, hybrids) in FY2027-28 looks prudent against a 35-40% tax guide.
  • Share count implied: about 15.5bn (FY2026), 15.1bn (FY2027) and 15.0bn (FY2028), so modest buybacks; FY2029 (thin coverage) implies about 16.1bn, a basis inconsistency.
  • Dispersion: FY2028 EPS ranges $0.21-0.93, so "consensus" is a deck average.

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement: net, the forecast is a peak followed by a fade back to the FY2024-25 earnings base in EBITDA, with a better-quality, per-share-accretive structure underneath. FY2026 is near the FY2022 peak: revenue of $241bn against $241bn, EBIT of $39bn against $41bn. FY2027 is about halfway down. FY2028-30 is a normal-deck plateau. In that plateau, EBITDA (about $35-36bn) is slightly below FY2024-25 (about $37-38bn), because about $2.6bn of perimeter has left. The net drivers of the FY2028 plateau against FY2025, at a similar deck ($72 against $69 Brent; RIM $12 against $12.8):

EBITDA bridge, FY2025 to FY2028 $bn
FY2025 EBITDA 36.6
Deck (Brent +$3, RIM -$0.8, Henry Hub +$0.25) +0.7
Net cost efficiency +0.8
Upstream growth and mix, low-carbon losses reduced, trading back to normal +0.9
Castrol, Gelsenkirchen, North Sea, Aker BP equity income -2.7

FY2028 EBITDA 36.1

Lower depreciation (held for sale, impairments, disposals) +1.7

Pre-tax profit: FY2025 $14.3bn to FY2028 $16.95bn +2.65
Tax charge: higher profit at a lower rate (40% to 37%) -0.55

Net income: FY2025 $7.49bn to FY2028 $9.8bn +2.3

The lower tax rate alone is worth about +$0.5bn: 3 points on $16.95bn. Shares fall about 6% on average, so EPS goes from $0.48 to $0.67 (+40%).

Where this differs from the last four to six years in kind, not just in level:

  • Volatility of the line items stays high, but the balance sheet no longer amplifies it. In 2020, a deck collapse took EBIT to -$8.3bn and forced a dividend cut. In my bear case the dividend is covered and net debt stays below $10bn.
  • The earnings mix shifts toward the most cyclical parts. Castrol, the steadiest earner, has gone. Refining and trading are larger shares of normal-year EBIT. EPS predictability in calm years is therefore no better than before, and possibly worse.
  • Cash returns move from dividend-only (2026) to dividend plus about $4.5-6bn of buybacks a year. That is 3.5-4.5% of market value, against Shell's roughly 7-8% total cash yield. The gap to Shell narrows but does not close.

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

–45

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Our assessment of BP is largely reflected in current guidance, consensus and the narrative, with a gap that is favourable in the near term and unfavourable after it. Guidance is conservative where it is specific (prices, the balance sheet, capex, tax) and will be beaten at the December event and the Q4 results; it is ambitious only on absolute operating cost, where we expect a quiet miss of about $1bn in 2027, and silent on the buyback, where we now expect a measured framework ($4.5bn in 2027) rather than the second set's $6bn. Consensus has already moved to where we are on FY2026 and is slightly above us on FY2027, because it follows a forward curve that sits above our deck; we expect one more positive revision burst around the December event and the Q4 results, and then cuts of 10-20% to FY2027 and 5-10% to FY2028 through 2027 as the deck normalises, with the breadth of revisions turning negative from the second quarter of 2027. The narrative knows the deleveraging, the refining windfall and its reversal, and the December decision; it does not fully weigh the mechanical sources of FY2028-30 EPS or the finance-cost floor, which roughly offset. For the memo, the gap favours the case over the next two events and runs against it over the following four: the investment case's "scope for upgrades" (topic #7) is real but confined to the balance-sheet, per-share and tax lines and to the next three months, while the "momentum" (topic #8) in sell-side revisions is strong today and likely to reverse within six months. The honest summary for Richard is that the market has priced BP's repair and is now waiting to see whether the repaired company can hold its distributions through the normalisation that everyone, including us, expects.

Layer 4 · Guidance

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

Judgement: BP will meet or beat every balance-sheet and capital item it has guided for 2026, will beat its 2026 tax-rate and divestment guidance, and will meet the operating items that are set as ranges. The December event is likely to raise what BP controls (cost and balance-sheet targets, a distribution framework, a dividend commitment) and to re-base what it does not (the 2030 production range, to exclude the North Sea), and to set 2027 guidance at a planning deck of about $70-75 that will look conservative in the first half of 2027 and fair in the second. The item most at risk of a quiet miss is absolute operating cost of about $18bn in 2027, which our base puts at about $19.0bn. Over the 18 months the pattern should be: beats on balance-sheet lines at every event, operating beats in the first two or three events while the dislocation lasts, and then operating results that merely meet a deck-adjusted guide as margins normalise. Guidance is conservative where it is specific and ambitious where it is vague, and the gap to our assessment is favourable on the specific items and neutral on the vague ones.

Each guided item, with its likely outcome and driver:

Guided item (source, date) Our base Likely outcome What drives it
2026 underlying tax rate 31-34% (cut from 35-40%, 30 Oct 2026) 32.5% Met, low half: nine-month rate 31%, Q3 28% on a downstream-heavy mix; Q4 mix shifts back toward upstream as refining weakens, lifting the rate Mix of profit; no policy change
2026 capex $13.5-14.0bn $13.75bn Met Q3 $3.0bn; farm-down timing already in the guide
2026 depreciation $17.0-17.5bn $17.3bn Met Held-for-sale assets stop depreciating
Net debt below $14-18bn (met at Q3: $13.87bn) about $6bn at end-2026 Beaten by a wide margin: Castrol $6bn and Aker BP $1.06bn landed in Q4; Morgan Stanley sees net debt "close to zero" or net cash in H1 2027 Disposal proceeds; a Q4 working-capital release of about $0.5bn; strong Q4 cash flow
Total financial obligations $33-35bn by end-2026 (from $39-41bn) about $37bn on our inputs, which assume no further hybrid redemption in Q4 Met or beaten: Redburn has $34.6bn on a conservative deck. Hybrids to about $9bn by end-2027 $9.0bn Met: mechanical calls (£1.25bn March 2027); S&P equity treatment preserved None
2026 divestment proceeds about $9bn (raised from $8bn, 5 Nov 2026) $8.5bn Met or beaten Castrol and Aker BP already closed
Q4 2026 production 2,090-2,230 kboe/d about 2,150-2,200 Met around the midpoint Middle East disruption, Gulf weather (20 kboe/d), bpx phasing

Operating cost about $18bn in 2027 about $19.0bn At risk of a quiet miss, or of being met only through the North Sea's exit; Jefferies already raised 2027 non-shale unit cost to $16/boe (5 Nov 2026) Inflation of about 2.5% a year on about $19bn; bpx activity; timing of the North Sea sale
ROACE above 16% in 2027 at $70 Brent, $4 Henry Hub, $10.30 RIM 17-18% at that deck Met and probably beaten, on the smaller capital base; the headline (actual-price) figure will be far higher in H1 2027 Impairments and disposals shrink the denominator; the market (Visible Alpha 10.4% in April 2026) did not believe it, so a beat is an upgrade to credibility
Underlying production flat 2026-27; 2.3-2.5 mboe/d by 2030 about 2,190 (2027), 2,280 (2030) Flat in 2027 met in underlying terms; the 2030 range re-based lower on 15 December to exclude the North Sea The sale; Kaskida and Tiber timing
Dividend growth at least 4% 4% Met; a higher rate is possible if the framework ties growth to the share count Board choice
Buyback framework (15 December) $4.5bn in 2027 from February (changed from $6.0bn) Set as a share of cash flow with a guardrail; a restart in H1 2027 at a measured pace See below

The 15 December event, and what I expect it to contain. The evidence points to a framework that is credible rather than generous: (1) the chief executive called $40bn of total liabilities "too high for optimal resilience in a low-price environment" and put the Gulf of America settlement first; (2) the most constructive broker, Morgan Stanley, assumes $0.75bn a quarter from Q3 2027, and Redburn says BP should not resume yet; (3) the peer frameworks are all expressed as a share of cash flow (Shell 40-50% of cash flow from operations, TotalEnergies at least 40%, Eni 35-45% with commodity-linked triggers, Woodside 20-40% of adjusted operating cash flow set on 5 November 2026), and BP's own retired framework was 30-40%; (4) a board that suspended buybacks once in February 2026 will want a framework it can hold at $60-65 Brent. My expectation: distributions of 30-40% of operating cash flow (or an equivalent on BP's price-adjusted measure), a net debt or total-obligations guardrail, buybacks starting with the Q4 2026 results in February 2027 at about $0.75-1.25bn a quarter, the Gulf of America payable settled or scheduled, a cost target restated on the two-segment basis and possibly raised, a 2030 production range re-based for the North Sea, and new 2028-30 targets for ROACE and free cash flow at a $70-75 planning deck. On FY2027 operating cash flow of about $28-30bn, a 30-40% framework gives total distributions of $8.5-12bn and, after a dividend of about $5.4bn, buybacks of about $3-6.5bn. That is why I lower our FY2027 buyback to $4.5bn: it is the centre of the range the framework is likely to imply, above Morgan Stanley's $1.5bn and below the second set's $6bn. A Shell-type 40-50% framework with a catch-up, or a one-off use of the net debt headroom, is the upside; a framework that waits for the Gulf of America settlement to complete is the downside.

The events over 18 months and the probable pattern:

  • 15 December 2026 (strategy event): targets raised on costs and balance sheet; framework set; production re-based. Market reaction depends on the buyback size against hopes that Morgan Stanley puts at up to $3bn a year by late 2027 and others higher. Probability the framework disappoints the more bullish hopes: about 50%.
  • About 13 January 2027 (Q4 trading statement) and about 10 February 2027 (Q4 2026 results): a strong Q4 (our $5.5bn, in a range of $4.5-6.5bn) with weaker trading and maintenance already guided; FY2026 EPS of about $1.44-1.46; net debt about $3-6bn; impairments on the North Sea and Lightsource likely. Balance-sheet beats; the first buyback tranche if the framework allows. New 2027 guidance (capex, depreciation, tax rate, production) set at a planning deck well below the H1 2027 forward curve.
  • Q1 2027 (early May 2027), first two-segment quarter: H1 2027 still carries the oil-indexed LNG lag, restocking and the Q2 maintenance spike in product markets; operating results above a $70-75 planning deck. The segment re-basing makes beats hard to read.
  • Q2 2027 (early August) and Q3 2027 (early November): the quarters where our deck falls most (Brent $75, RIM $13 in H2); the operating lines merely meet a deck-adjusted guide; the FY2027 absolute operating cost question is answered at Q4 2027 results (February 2028): our base says about $19bn against about $18bn guided, with the North Sea's exit timing deciding whether the headline is met.
  • Q4 2027 (February 2028) and Q1 2028 (May 2028): cost target deadline (met in gross terms); the 2027 ROACE test (met at $70); the hybrid stack at $9bn; the first full year of the framework.

Secondarily, 18 months to three years out: the mid-term targets BP controls (cost, hybrids, obligations) are more likely to be raised than cut; the targets that depend on the deck (ROACE above 16%, free cash flow growth above 20% a year over 2024-27 on a price-adjusted basis) are likely to be met on the price-adjusted basis and replaced with new 2028-30 targets on 15 December; the 2030 production range is more likely to be re-based lower than met in its current form; and the Kaskida 2029 and Tiber 2030 start-ups carry a 6-12 month slippage risk on supply-chain and litigation grounds. Record: BP's recent habit is to raise what the portfolio delivers and to loosen quietly what costs money (capex up, divestment proceeds moved, tax rate walked both ways); the new chief executive's first framework is more likely to be set to be beaten than to stretch.

How this compares with our assessment: guidance is conservative on prices (an $80 planning Brent in 2026 against a $93.5 average; a 2027 plan likely at $70-75 against a curve near $88) and on the balance sheet (every target beaten early), which is favourable; ambitious on absolute operating cost, where we expect a miss of about $1bn; and silent on the lines that drive our per-share forecast (buyback and tax path), which the December event will fill. The gap is favourable but narrowing: the balance-sheet beats are now visible to everyone (I-03), so the next favourable surprise must come from the framework or from costs, and costs are the line we trust least.

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

Judgement: consensus for FY2026 rises once more (to about $1.43-1.46 from $1.37 after the Q3 beat and the post-results upgrades), FY2027 consensus is close to a peak and will be cut over the next twelve months by about 10-20% if our deck is right, and FY2028 consensus (about $0.65-0.67) is roughly right at our deck and about 5-10% too high at a flat $70 Brent and $12 RIM. The net breadth of revisions, now strongly positive (FY2027 EPS +14% over one month and +35% over three; EBITDA +4% and +13%; the buy share up 20 points in six months to 54%), has one more positive burst available, on the December framework and the Q4 results, and then turns negative from the second quarter of 2027 as the deck normalises. The operating lines (refining, trading, Brent) move consensus down; the per-share and below-EBIT lines (share count, tax rate) move it up but by less; finance costs move it down a little in FY2028, where consensus is too optimistic. Our base sits at or just below where consensus is heading on FY2027-28, so the gap is not favourable: the sell-side already reflects our view of the mechanics, and it is more optimistic than we are on the one line that matters most, the 2027 refining margin.

Where consensus sits and where it is going (EPS, brokers' basis):

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029
FactSet median, 25 Oct 2026 (before Q3) $1.37 $0.856 $0.670 $0.579 (10 brokers)
FactSet mean, 23 Oct 2026 $1.33 $0.898 $0.648
Post-Q3 marks: Redburn (30 Oct) $1.43 $0.78 $0.60
Post-Q3 marks: Morgan Stanley (5 Nov; Brent $86.20 in 2027, $75 in 2028; buybacks $0.75bn a quarter from Q3 2027) above consensus a 34% upgrade to its FY2027 figure
Consensus net income revision after Q3 (Morgan Stanley) +10.3% for FY2027 (Shell +7.3%, Equinor +5.4%, TotalEnergies +3.0%, Eni +2.8%)
Our base (model) $1.36 (second set $1.46) $0.87 $0.65 $0.63
Our base at a flat $70 Brent and $12 RIM from 2027 (my estimate) about $0.60-0.65 about $0.60 about $0.58
Dispersion of individual brokers (25 Oct) $0.80 to $1.64 $0.54 (Intesa) to $1.29 (Barclays) $0.21 (GERDES) to $0.93 (Barclays)

The drivers of each year's revisions, in order:

  • FY2026: one more upgrade, then done. Nine months are reported at $17.4bn of underlying profit; the pre-Q3 median of $1.37 implies a Q4 of only about $3.9bn, against our $5.5bn and Redburn's implied $5.1bn. Consensus moves to about $1.43-1.46 as stale estimates (several brokers last updated in July) are refreshed. Breadth stays positive into the January trading statement. The early-November fall in Brent trims Q4 by perhaps $0.3-0.4bn pre-tax; not enough to change the direction.
  • FY2027: near a peak; cut through 2027. The post-Q3 upgrades took consensus net income up about 10%, so the FY2027 median is probably now about $0.92-0.95, above our $0.87. The brokers' decks for 2027 range from $70 (Redburn) to $86 (Morgan Stanley), with the curve at $88-92; refining assumptions from a fast return to $12 (Redburn) to $25-28 (Morgan Stanley, the curve). Consensus is therefore an average of decks, and it follows the curve with a lag of one to two quarters. If the Strait reopens in stages through 2027 (our base), the curve falls first, then the H2 2027 estimates, then FY2027: cuts of 10-20% over the year, concentrated after the Q1 and Q2 2027 results. If the curve is right, consensus is cut little and ours is too low. Jefferies (19 October 2026) already judged that the sell-side had captured the near-term upside and that further upgrades need a strategic catalyst; Morgan Stanley (12 October) that record margins were "already priced into consensus" with cash going to debt, "leaving little room for payout surprises".
  • FY2028: about right at our deck; too high at a normal one. The median of $0.67 (mean $0.648) rests on decks of about $72-75 and margins of $12-16. It embeds a finance cost of about $2.1bn, which the second set shows is about $0.4bn too low (the floor of lease interest and unwinding of discount; a gross debt stack of about $58bn at 5% against about $35bn of cash), and a leakage below pre-tax profit of about 45%, which is about 8 points too high if the tax rate normalises at 36-37%. Those two errors roughly cancel. The share count implied by consensus (about 15.0bn) is above the model's 15.0bn only marginally, so the buyback is not a source of upgrades unless the framework is larger than Morgan Stanley's. At a flat $70 and $12 RIM, FY2028 consensus would be cut to about $0.60 (Redburn's figure).
  • FY2029: thin and inconsistent. Ten brokers; consensus implies about 16.1bn shares, a basis artefact. Ignore.

The net breadth of revisions, and its likely path:

  • Now: FactSet's revision momentum score is 15; FY2027 EPS revisions +14% (one month), +35% (three and six months), +53% (twelve); target price +2% and +7%; the buy share 54%, up 10 points in three months and 20 in six. The dashboard's sell-side momentum rating is B (peers: Shell B, TotalEnergies A, Eni C, Equinor A, Repsol A). BP's breadth is strong but not the sector's strongest, consistent with a consensus still catching up to the run-rate rather than extrapolating it.
  • Next two events (15 December, February 2027): a last positive burst is likely: FY2026 marks up, the framework adding buybacks to models that have none (Redburn and others modelled none until 2029 as recently as April 2026), the Gulf of America schedule removing an uncertainty. If the framework is at or above $1bn a quarter, FY2027-28 EPS estimates gain 1-3% from the share count. Breadth stays positive.
  • From Q2 2027: breadth turns negative as the H2 2027 deck falls and the first two-segment quarter removes the clean beat-or-miss signal. The precedent is BP's own 2023-25: a Q1 2023 beat followed by six quarters of misses and downgrades (Morgan Stanley, 13 January 2025), in a calm market with trading below expectations. The 2026-27 version has a far better balance sheet beneath it, so the downgrades will be to operating lines, not to the dividend or the share count.
  • Peers: the same pattern applies across the European majors (Morgan Stanley's 5 November title, "Longer disruption priced out"), but BP's revisions will be the most volatile in both directions because of its refining and trading weight (its post-Q3 upgrade was the sector's largest).

Secondarily, 18 months to three years out (FY2028-29): consensus is likely to be cut modestly (5-10%) as decks converge on $70-75 and refining on $12-14, offset partly by higher buyback assumptions once a framework exists; the dispersion ($0.21 to $0.93 for FY2028) will narrow as the deck debate resolves. The structural error I expect to persist in consensus is the finance-cost floor; the structural error in our favour is the tax rate.

The comparison with our assessment, which sets the score. Our base EPS is already at or slightly below where consensus is settling for FY2027-28; our EBITDA is 4-8% below consensus for FY2027-29; we agree with consensus on the mechanics and disagree on the speed of refining normalisation, where we are more cautious than the curve and most brokers. The direction of consensus over 18 months is therefore down toward us, and then, if the deck normalises fully, below where we stand today. That is not a favourable gap for an investment case: it means the earnings surprises ahead are more likely to be negative than positive once the December and February events are past. The favourable part is narrow: the FY2026 mark-up, the first buyback tranche, and the tax rate.

D-0745
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

–45

Layer 3 · Risks

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

Judgement: BP has become a high-beta proxy for its sector. Over the latest six-month window the peer basket explains 73% of BP's daily variance (correlation 0.85, beta 1.18), up from 42-45% in early 2025 and a peak of 79% in August 2026; the closest co-movers are Shell (R-squared 74%), TotalEnergies (70%) and Eni (67%). The idiosyncratic 27% has been negative: BP returned +2.4% over the six months against +12.2% for the basket, consistent with leadership turnover, the buyback suspension and the Gulf-of-America-first decision (Morgan Stanley, 5 November: BP "lagged the sector throughout 2026"). The multiple is therefore industry-driven in its level and company-driven in its discount: BP's 24-month-forward P/E of about 12.3 times sits at the 97th percentile of its own five-year range, but so do the peers' (P/E percentiles of 50 to 99 against their own ten-year histories), because the whole European sector has re-rated on the capital swing back to barrels. For predictability this cuts both ways: the share price will follow the oil and refining tide whatever BP delivers, and the company-specific levers (the buyback framework, delivery on costs) can only move the discount, which is perhaps one to two multiple points.

The evidence:

  • Co-movement is high and rising. R-squared against the equal-weighted basket of 19 dashboard peers: 58% (December 2024), 42% (March 2025), 69% (September 2025), 44% (December 2025, the leadership and buyback-suspension period, when BP decoupled), 79% (August 2026), 73% (6 November 2026). Beta has run between 0.92 and 1.25 and is 1.18 today: BP moves about a fifth more than the basket in both directions. The decoupling episodes of early 2025 and late 2025 were both company-specific and both negative (the strategy reset and Elliott; the chief executive change and the February 2026 kitchen-sink), which suggests that when BP trades on its own news the news has tended to be bad.
  • The relative-return gap is the idiosyncratic signal. Six-month return +2.4% against +12.2% for the basket; twelve-month +39.5% against Shell +40.1%, TotalEnergies +65.3%, Eni +77.6%, Equinor +87.2%, Repsol +94.8%. BP has participated in the sector's re-rating but at the bottom of the integrated majors, which fits a market that rewards distributions (Shell's 2027 total yield 10.1%, TotalEnergies 9.6%, Eni 9.1%, BP 5.9% on Morgan Stanley's estimates) and penalises complexity.
  • The multiple's level is the sector's. BP's current P/E of 10.8 times at the 64th percentile of its ten-year history compares with Shell 10.0 times at the 70th, Eni 10.7 at the 63rd, TotalEnergies 8.7 at the 50th, Equinor 12.3 at the 81st, Repsol 8.0 at the 78th. The dashboard's valuation rating is D for BP, Shell, Eni and Repsol alike, and F for most of the rest. The 24-month-forward P/E of 12.3 times at the 97th percentile of BP's five years is unusual for BP but typical of the sector today, and it is high partly because 24-month-forward EPS ($0.52 on the dashboard) already embeds normalisation. On trailing or FY2026 earnings the multiple is 5.7-6.1 times.
  • The company-driven residual is the discount, and it is narrowing for the right reasons. BP's discount to Shell and TotalEnergies has historically reflected leverage, strategic reversals and governance. Leverage is being removed; the other two are being tested. Morgan Stanley (5 November) expects the valuation discount to narrow as de-gearing and operational improvement proceed; it is also the broker that assumes the smallest 2027 buyback. The 15 December framework is the one company-specific event that could move the discount independently of the tide.

What this means for the forecasts' predictability and the fair multiple:

  • The share price over the next 18 months will be set mainly by the sector's response to the deck (R-squared of 73%, beta 1.18). If Brent and refining margins normalise, BP falls about 1.2 times as far as the basket, with the balance-sheet improvement limiting the damage to the discount, not to the level. The first set's trough arithmetic (400-430p in a normalisation, 310-330p in a downturn) is a sector call with a BP beta.
  • The multiple is a sector multiple. Viewforth's guideline fair multiple of 12.82 times (the model) and the ten-year bands (median 9.5 times, 75th percentile 12.1 times) should be read against the sector's current position near the top of its range: a reversion in the sector's multiple takes BP with it, whatever the framework says on 15 December.
  • The idiosyncratic 27% is where the memo's company analysis earns its keep, and it has recently been a drag. A credible framework, delivery on the North Sea sale and a clean FY2026 result could turn it from a negative to a modest positive; a cautious framework or a governance surprise would widen the discount again.
11h

Estimated TSR [to nearest 5% band]

–not yet ratedMemo topic 12, : the Rater's score
