Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

C-526th of 13

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement: in this sector outperformance comes from (1) a credible self-help turnaround plus a supportive sector tide, (2) capital discipline converted into covered, rising cash returns on a strong balance sheet, and (3) visible volume step-ups from advantaged projects; underperformance comes from high leverage into a price downturn, strategic flip-flops, capitalising a record margin as a run rate, and governance failures. BP fits the first outperformance pattern now, fails the second and third, and carries three of the four underperformance patterns, so its outperformance is conditional on the price tide holding while the self-help completes.

Patterns and BP's fit:

  • Self-help plus sector tailwind (fits). The best recent precedent is Shell from 2023 under Wael Sawan: cost cuts delivered early ($5.8bn of a $5-7bn target by mid-2026), buybacks of at least $3bn for nineteen quarters, the share count down 19% in three years, and a 30.2% dollar shareholder return from January 2023 to May 2025, ahead of BP and TotalEnergies. BP in late 2026 resembles Shell in mid-2023, but with a weaker balance sheet, a later point in the price cycle and no buyback yet.
  • Covered cash yield on record-low gearing (fails today). The sector digest's rule: "a yield the market believes is worth far more than one it doubts"; payouts set as a share of cash flow on low gearing "flex rather than break". BP's payout breaks first (dividend breakeven $60-65).
  • Volume step-ups (fails). "A step change in volume is the easiest thing for the market to extrapolate"; BP's volumes are flat; Kaskida and Tiber arrive in 2029-30.
  • Underperformance patterns BP carries: high leverage into a downturn (BP halved its dividend in 2020 and suspended it in 2010 after Macondo, general knowledge); strategic flip-flops (2020 pivot, 2025 reversal); governance failures (2023 chief executive resignation, 2026 chair removal). The fourth, "the record-margin trap" (the house's October 2026 energy memos all normalise margins from 2027), is the live risk for every upstream name, and BP is the most margin-geared.
  • Rules of thumb: whole cohorts move with the oil price; European majors trade at a discount to US majors (9.3 times against 14.2 times 2027 earnings); "when planning prices sit far below realised prices, consensus is a floor"; turnarounds take longer than expected.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

F2811th of 13

Layer 1 · Risks

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

Judgement: in a normalisation scenario (Brent $65-70, refining margins at BP's planning level, a modest buyback) the fair trough is about 400-430p, a fall of about 31-36% from 624p; in a genuine downturn with risk-off sentiment it is about 310-330p, a fall of about 47-50%; an extreme combination (a $60 oil war plus an operational or governance shock) could reach about 250p, a fall of about 60%.

Inputs (dashboard and FactSet):

  • 24-month-forward EPS 51.7p; 24-month-forward P/E 12.1-12.3 times, at the 97th percentile of its five-year range (4.83-13.33 times; median 6.89 times; 10th percentile 5.88 times) and the 91st of the last two years.
  • Ten-year bands for this stock: 15.0 times (90th, "mania"), 12.1 times (75th), 9.5 times (median), 8.3 times (35th, "conservative"), 6.0 times (10th, "crash").

Trough arithmetic:

  • Today's forward EPS at 8.3 times: 429p (-31%); at the five-year median 6.9 times: 357p (-43%); at the ten-year 10th percentile 6.0 times: 310p (-50%).
  • A downturn also lowers forward EPS: brokers' $60 Brent cases put EPS at $0.34-0.41 (about 25-31p); at the ten-year median 9.5 times that is about 240-290p; normalised EPS of about $0.55 (about 41p) at 6.0-8.3 times gives about 245-340p.
  • Real-world anchors: the 52-week low of 413p (with Brent in the $60s and buybacks just suspended); Jefferies' downside target of 320p ($60 oil and a failed Bumerangue appraisal); Morgan Stanley's bear case of $26.30 per ADS (about 327p); options in August priced about a 10% probability of falling below 327p; EV/DACF has a cyclical low near 3.8 times against 4.2-5.5 times today.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

C+604th of 13

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative staircase is strong and dated for the next six months: Q3 results, the end-2026 debt milestone, Castrol's close and, above all, the 15 December strategy event that should restart buybacks. That is why the shares are near their high despite a weak base. Beyond six months the steps depend on self-help reaching profit and on deepwater delivery, both less certain, and they will be climbed against a likely fall in prices and margins. For the Consumer: the 15 December framework (size, trigger and funding of buybacks) is the single most important new input for 2027-29 share count and EPS, and it should be modelled as a range until announced.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–60

Layer 3 · Risks

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

Judgement: the two sets agree on the architecture of the case and disagree on magnitudes in a way that facts, not reasoning, mostly resolved. The surviving incongruities are three, and they sit in one place: the second set is more generous than the earlier evidence allows about what management will do with the balance sheet (the buyback size), what it will deliver operationally (net cost savings, reliability) and how far below the market's refining path our base should sit. Congruence is high on direction (a 2026 peak, a fade to a plateau, a repaired balance sheet, a buyback restart) and medium on level. The case is coherent: a deleveraged, ex-growth, price-taking integrated whose per-share earnings are held up by below-EBIT mechanics while operating earnings fall by about half. That coherence is itself a finding: there is no second thesis in reserve if the deck disappoints.

Where the sets align and reinforce each other:

  • Peak and fade. C-06 (the cycle at its peak), C-10 (tailwinds cyclical, headwinds structural), C-49 (EPS growth not durable), P-01 (EBITDA $56.3bn to $42.5bn to $36.1bn) and P-10 (a cone skewed down) describe the same shape. Consensus and every AlphaSense report share it (Morgan Stanley: margins back to about $12 by late 2028).
  • Balance sheet first. C-15 and C-19 (disposals and deleveraging as the strongest internal force) lead to P-02 (net debt about $6bn at end-2026, the most predictable line). Q3 confirmed it: net debt $13.87bn, the target met five quarters early. This is the best-evidenced thread in the whole case.
  • Financial lines against operating lines. C-61 and C-69 (BP beats when markets are volatile, misses when calm, and delivers early on the balance sheet) lead to P-06 (weight each line by who controls it). The peer evidence in the plausibility report fits: Shell turned gross savings into lower absolute operating cost ($39.96bn in 2023 to $35.68bn in 2025); ExxonMobil's absolute cash operating cost rose over nine months of 2026 despite $16.9bn of cumulative gross savings.
  • Perimeter removes earnings. C-15 and C-54 lead to P-05 (each dollar of proceeds removes about four times its interest saving in EBITDA). The Castrol dividend prepayment confirms that the income was sold with the business.

Where the sets disagreed and how it was resolved:

  • Normalised net income, $7.5-9.5bn (first set) against $9.4bn at $70 and $9.8bn at $72 (second set). Resolved by Q3 facts (net debt, a 28% tax rate, held-for-sale depreciation) and by the first set's omission of the depreciation and tax bridges. I accept the second set, with the two caveats above: $1.7bn is non-cash depreciation and $0.8bn is undemonstrated net cost efficiency.
  • Finance costs, "consensus too optimistic" (first set) against "consensus right for FY2027, $0.4bn too low for FY2028" (second set). The tax-and-finance report adds Redburn at $3.77bn (2027) and $3.55bn (2028), which almost certainly includes hybrid coupons. Our all-in figure ($4.08bn in FY2027, including the pre-tax equivalent of hybrid coupons and minorities) sits between Redburn and consensus. The non-falling floor ($0.70bn of lease interest and $1.15bn of unwinding of discount in 2025, falling to about $0.95bn) is agreed by both sets and documented in BP's 2025 accounts.
  • Distributable cash at $70, $0-2bn against $4-5bn a year. The difference is the end of Gulf of America payments and lower finance costs; resolved for the second set, conditional on the payable being settled in 2027, whose mechanics are undisclosed.

The surviving incongruities, and what I do about them:

  1. Buyback size against management's own words. The second set quotes the chief executive ("too high for optimal resilience in a low-price environment"), the decision to settle the Gulf of America payable first, and Morgan Stanley's $0.75bn a quarter from Q3 2027, and then assumes $6bn from February 2027. The first set's C-85 expected "a measured, not aggressive, restart". I side with the first set and the brokers: FY2027 buybacks to $4.5bn (I-01 gives the reasoning).
  2. The refining path against the market. The second set's RIM of $17 for 2027 sits below Morgan Stanley ($25 to $27.7) and the curve ($28), while its own P-06 says to lean H1 2027 up. Its half-year split ($21.5, then $13) is defensible, but it is why our FY2027 EBITDA is 4% below consensus. The Consumer should read that gap as deliberate conservatism on the most reversible line, not as a disagreement about facts.
  3. Operational relaxation. The second set eased the first set's reliability concern after Q3. The safety data and the Rotterdam outage argue against easing. No model input changes (95% reliability is neutral), but the risk stays live in D-04.
  4. A smaller jar. The second set's 2030 production (about 2,280 kboe/d) is below BP's 2.3-2.5 mboe/d target, while its ROACE check says BP beats 16% at $70. Both are true only because ROACE is beaten on a shrunken capital base. The Consumer should not read a ROACE beat as operating outperformance (D-06).

The one structural tension across both sets is this: the first set says the market has not fully priced normalisation (C-82, C-83), yet our base EPS for FY2027-28 is at or just below consensus (P-10, the model). The two reconcile only through the deck: our base deck ($80 and $72) is itself above a $70 normal, so our forecasts are a slow-normalisation case, not a normalised case. When the Consumer reads the model's "in line with consensus", it is in line because both embed an elevated 2027. At a flat $70 and a $12 RIM from 2027, our FY2027 EPS would be about $0.60-0.65, roughly a third below consensus.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

–50

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

Judgement: the sell-side narrative on BP is dominated by three dynamics that are fully known and fully priced (the early deleveraging, the refining-margin windfall and its normalisation, and the December buyback decision), carries five more that one or two houses discuss but the market weights lightly (the finance-cost floor, cost savings not reaching profit, the perimeter removing steady earnings, the reserve-life gap, and the hybrid path), and leaves three almost undiscussed that matter for the per-share forecast (the depreciation and tax-rate effects on FY2028-30 EPS, the arithmetic by which a dollar of buyback is worth two to three times a dollar of debt repayment, and the step-up in free cash flow once the Gulf of America payments end). Investor commentary is thin in the evidence; what exists (Elliott's public demands, Jefferies' and Redburn's descriptions of what investors reward) shows a market that pays for headline deleveraging and dividend yield and has not yet re-based its view of normalised EPS onto the mechanical lines. For the next 18 months, the market knows the operating fade and the balance-sheet repair; it does not fully know how much of FY2028-30 EPS is mechanical rather than operating. That gap is modestly favourable. Beyond 18 months, the reserve-life and Bumerangue questions are known and unresolved, and the narrative will fill with them as the near-term story is exhausted.

Investor commentary. The AlphaSense report found no Twitter or investor-letter evidence and relied on broker descriptions of what the market does: Jefferies (19 October 2026) says the market rewards headline deleveraging and needs the strategy event to combine growth with returns; Morgan Stanley (3 September 2026) says the market prices about two thirds of the 16% ROACE target and had "prematurely stopped modelling buybacks"; Redburn (April 2026) says the absence of a leverage trigger had the market modelling buybacks only in 2029. Elliott's public agenda (about 5%; $20bn of annual free cash flow by 2027; deeper cuts; disposals) is well known and largely achieved. Short interest is about 0.4% of the free float, so there is no bearish crowd. The dashboard's interest level (B) and knowledge level ("Pre-IG") indicate a stock that is widely held and well covered but not deeply understood on the lines that will drive 2028-30 EPS. The reading is that investors hold BP for the deleveraging and the yield, judge it against Shell's distributions, and have not re-based their expectations onto the mechanical EPS lines.

What this means, against our assessment:

  • The things we expect to go right that the market does not fully weigh: the mechanical uplift to FY2028-30 EPS from depreciation, tax and the finance line (about $0.12 a share against FY2025 before buybacks), the free-cash-flow step-up after the Gulf payments end, and the tax-rate normalisation at 36-37% rather than 39-40%. These are quiet positives that will appear as consensus beats on the net income line from 2027 even as EBIT falls. They are modestly favourable for the case because the market will attribute them to "delivery".
  • The things we expect to go wrong that the market already knows: refining normalisation, trading normalisation, cost leakage. These are in the sell-side narrative and partly in the price (Morgan Stanley's "priced out"). Their arrival will still produce negative revisions, because consensus for FY2027 follows the curve with a lag, but it will not change the narrative.
  • The things the market weighs more heavily than we do: the headline deleveraging as a source of buyback capacity (we now assume a measured framework) and the gross cost target. These are modest negatives for the case at the December event.
  • Beyond 18 months: the reserve life and the Bumerangue result are known unknowns that the narrative will turn to once the balance-sheet story is finished; both are coin-flips for the multiple rather than for the forecasts, and the narrative has them priced as optionality rather than as a threat.

Net, the narrative is comprehensive on the headline dynamics and shallow on the per-share mechanics, in roughly equal measure. The gap between what we think and what the market articulates is slightly favourable on the mechanics and slightly unfavourable on the buyback size, so near neutral overall.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 30 October 2026, 11:32 to 11:53 UK, Opus, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
BP p.l.c. (BP-GB): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-10-29, the day before BP's third-quarter 2026 results (due 2026-10-30). Price 624.3p (2026-10-29), close to the 52-week high of 638.5p. All money figures are USD unless stated. "Underlying" means BP's underlying replacement cost profit, the basis the sell-side forecasts.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Overview for the Consumer: the ten judgements that should shape the operating and financial model

  1. BP runs on two clocks, and the model must keep them apart. The near-term clock (refining margin capture, trading, Brent, deleveraging) is the strongest it has been since 2022 and BP is the European major most leveraged to it. The medium-term clock (reserve life of 7.1 years, flat volumes, return on average capital employed of about 14% in 2025, the weakest balance sheet in the peer set) is the weakest in the peer set. Consensus already models the hand-over: earnings per share (EPS) on the brokers' basis of $1.37 for FY2026, $0.856 for FY2027 and $0.670 for FY2028, against $0.480 for FY2025.
  2. FY2026 is a price-and-margin windfall, not an operating step change. First-half 2026 underlying profit was $8.93bn ($3.20bn in Q1, $5.73bn in Q2). The FY2026 consensus of $21.3bn implies about $12.4bn in the second half, about $6.2bn a quarter, above Q2's $5.73bn, so consensus already needs Q3 and Q4 above Q2. On BP's own rules of thumb, Q2's deck (Brent $103.85, refining indicator margin $29.60) against BP's planning deck ($70 Brent, $10.80 margin) is worth roughly $20bn a year before tax, about $12bn after tax at a 40% tax rate, which is more than one and a half times BP's entire FY2025 underlying profit of $7.49bn.
  3. Normalised earnings power is the single most important number, and it is far below FY2026. My rough bridge from FY2025 ($7.49bn at $69 Brent) to FY2028 at BP's planning deck, adding net self-help, lower interest and project start-ups and subtracting Castrol, Gelsenkirchen and the North Sea, lands at about $7.5bn to $9.5bn of net income (centre about $8.5bn; about $0.49 to $0.62 a share on today's share count before any buyback; detail in C-49). BP's own target of return on average capital employed above 16% by 2027 at $70 Brent implies roughly $7-8bn. Consensus FY2028 net income of $10.1bn sits above both, so it embeds a deck above $70 or margins above planning. The model should be built as a price-deck model with an explicit normalisation path, never as an extrapolation of 2026.
  4. The windfall has largely completed the balance-sheet repair, which converts the equity story from deleveraging to capital return. Net debt excluding leases was $22.25bn at Q2 2026 against a $14-18bn target now due at end-2026; brokers model $6.3bn (Morgan Stanley) to about $10bn (Redburn, Jefferies) at end-2026. The buyback framework expected at the New York strategy event on 15 December 2026 is the largest single input to the share count and to EPS for 2027 to 2029.
  5. Self-help is real but leaky. Structural cost reductions reached $3.54bn by June 2026 against a $6.5-7.5bn target for end-2027, but about $3bn of gross savings has been absorbed by inflation and activity choices, and management admits the cuts have not yet reached the bottom line. The best evidence that some of it is reaching cash is price-adjusted free cash flow (at $70 Brent) of $10.36bn in the first half of 2026, up 49% on $6.97bn a year earlier.
  6. The perimeter is shrinking, and the model must remove the earnings that go with it. Castrol (65% sold; BP expects no income or dividends from its retained 35% in the short to medium term), Gelsenkirchen (refining margin sensitivity cut from $550m to $450m per $1/bbl), the UK North Sea (100 kboe/d for sale) and Archaea all leave. Part of the guided fall in absolute operating costs from $22bn (2025) to about $18bn (2027) is perimeter, not efficiency.
  7. The base deserves a low multiple until proven otherwise. Volumes are guided flat for 2026-27 and to 2.3-2.5 mboe/d by 2030, proved reserve life is the lowest among the majors, and the upside resource story (Bumerangue, 8 billion barrels in place) is a 2030s event.
  8. Three feedback loops matter for the model. Deleveraging is a reinforcing loop (lower interest and hybrid coupons, more free cash flow, buybacks, higher EPS). Working capital is a counter-cyclical buffer (it built by $7.0bn in the first half of 2026 as prices rose, and releases when prices fall). The commodity itself is a balancing loop (high prices invite windfall taxes, demand destruction, supply response and service-cost inflation).
  9. The data carry traps. Revenue, EBITDA, debt, EPS and even the "buyback" line come on different bases (listed at the end of C-54). Do not compute growth from FactSet history to brokers' consensus; use the brokers'-basis actuals ($36.6bn EBITDA and $0.480 EPS for FY2025).
  10. The shares already price much of this. At 624p the stock trades at about 6.1 times FY2026, 9.8 times FY2027 and 12.5 times FY2028 consensus EPS; the 24-month-forward P/E of 12.3 times sits at the 97th percentile of its five-year range; the consensus target price (654.6p) is about 5% above the price.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Closing steer to the Consumer: the model in eight lines

  1. Build on explicit decks: at least $60, $70-75 and $90 Brent, each with a matching refining margin ($8-10, $11-14, $18-22) and trading assumption; FY2026 actuals plus Q3-Q4 at current prices.
  2. Volumes: upstream about 2.2-2.35 mmboe/d through 2028 (start-ups and BPX offset decline; remove 100 kboe/d from the North Sea sale date; plant reliability recovery as an upside test).
  3. Costs: operating cost to about $18-19bn by 2027, but only 40-60% of the non-portfolio savings reaching profit; supplier inflation of 3-5% a year.
  4. Perimeter: remove Castrol's earnings (no income from the retained 35%), Gelsenkirchen's margin and the North Sea's profit; add proceeds to cash.
  5. Below EBIT: net finance costs falling by about $0.5-1bn by 2028, not $2bn; hybrids to $9bn by end-2027; tax rate 35-40% drifting lower; Gulf of America cash payments of about $1.1-1.6bn a year outside underlying profit.
  6. Cash: capex $13.5-14bn; working capital released when prices fall; leases about $2.2bn a year; dividend growth at least 4%.
  7. Distributions: a buyback restart in early 2027 sized from the elevated 2027 deck, net debt headroom up to the $14-18bn range and residual disposals; at a $70 deck organic surplus after the dividend is only about $0-2bn a year.
  8. Normalised EPS: about $0.49-0.62 by 2028 before buybacks at $70; consensus $0.67 assumes more.
Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: BP p.l.c. (BP-GB), the London-listed integrated energy major, reporting in 2026 through three segments (Gas & Low Carbon Energy, Oil Production & Operations, Customers & Products) and from 1 January 2027 through two: Upstream (oil and gas production including bpx energy in US onshore, LNG, gas and power trading, renewable natural gas and carbon capture) and Downstream (refining, oil and products trading, fuels marketing and convenience retail, aviation fuels, biofuels, hydrogen and the retained 35% of Castrol), with Supply, Trading & Shipping operating across both.

Request: T-P1

Peers: Shell (closest European integrated peer with a large trading arm; structural cost programme of USD 5-7bn by end-2028 against 2022, reported at USD 5.8bn by mid-2026); TotalEnergies (lowest supermajor upstream unit costs and a USD 12.5bn 2026-2030 savings programme); ExxonMobil (long-running structural cost-savings programme and the scale benchmark); Chevron (USD 3bn structural cost reduction delivered early, business units cut from 14 to five); Eni (Versalis restructuring with permanent cracker closures and loss-making European refining); Equinor (Norwegian shelf standardisation and subsea tie-back cost model); Repsol (refining-heavy European integrated peer); Vitol and Trafigura (private; the cost structure of independent trading houses as a benchmark for BP's Supply, Trading & Shipping); Klesch Group (private; buyer of BP's Gelsenkirchen refinery, as a reference for the cost economics of European refining assets). Inputs: What share of BP's operating cost base, which management described at the Q2 2026 results (4 August 2026) as about USD 22bn in 2025 and guided to about USD 18bn by 2027, is fixed and what share varies with production, refinery throughput, retail volumes and traded volumes, segment by segment? Of the USD 3.54bn of cumulative structural cost reductions since 2023 reported at 30 June 2026, how much came from divestments and how much from operating, maintenance, procurement and corporate-centre efficiency, and how much has been offset by inflation and activity (BP has cited about USD 3bn of offsets against its 2023 baseline)? How much of the raised end-2027 target of USD 6.5-7.5bn is mechanical, from the Gelsenkirchen sale (completed 31 July 2026, about USD 1bn of operating cost) and the Castrol transaction, rather than efficiency? Why have structural cost cuts not yet reached underlying earnings, as the CEO and CFO acknowledged in August 2026, and what evidence (for example price-adjusted free cash flow, adjusted operating cost excluding freight) shows how much is reaching profit? How do BP's upstream unit production costs (about USD 6/boe) and refining cash costs per barrel compare with peers, and how will the planned exit from the UK North Sea (launched 31 July 2026, about 100 kboe/d) and the Gelsenkirchen sale change group unit costs? How did BP's and peers' segment margins move with the oil price and the refining indicator margin in the 2014-2016 and 2020 down-cycles and the 2021-2022 and 2026 up-cycles, and how did realised moves compare with published rules of thumb (BP: USD 340m pre-tax per USD 1/bbl Brent; USD 450m per USD 1/bbl refining indicator margin after Gelsenkirchen)? What did BP's past cost programmes (including the Reinvent programme's USD 2.5bn delivered by mid-2021) and peers' programmes deliver against promises, over what time, and which cost lines proved cuttable and which did not? Is there evidence that cost and headcount cuts (BP's workforce reduction of over 5%, more than 4,700 employees and 3,000 contractors) are damaging capability: upstream plant reliability fell from 96.1% in 2025 to 92.4% in Q2 2026 and refining availability to 94.7%; what dated precedents exist at oil majors where deep cuts were followed by reliability, safety or project failures? What are oilfield-service, rig, subsea and offshore-vessel cost trends for 2026-2028, and what do they imply for the physical activity bought by BP's 2026 capital expenditure guidance of USD 13.5-14.0bn and its USD 13-15bn frame? How does the cost base of Supply, Trading & Shipping behave when traded volumes or volatility fall? Focus: How much of BP's guided reduction in absolute operating costs will reach underlying replacement cost profit and free cash flow by 2028, net of inflation and of the costs that leave with divested businesses, and how much further reduction is feasible without eroding plant reliability, safety and project delivery.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Shell (integrated trading and high refining utilisation; the closest comparison for capture and trading earnings); TotalEnergies (largest European refining capacity, lower refining capture, and a gas trading loss in Q2 2026 that gives a contrast); ExxonMobil and Chevron (US majors with larger refining systems and Gulf Coast exposure); Eni (gas and LNG portfolio optimisation and the Mercuria trading joint venture); Equinor (unhedged European spot gas seller); Repsol (complex refining and heavy-crude processing premium); Valero and Marathon Petroleum (US independent refiners, benchmark for US refining margin capture); Vitol and Trafigura (private; trading houses competing for products and LNG optimisation margins); Cheniere and Venture Global (US LNG developers whose capacity shapes the LNG market from 2027). Operating conditions: a geopolitical supply shock with the Strait of Hormuz disrupted and Middle East crude and product exports curtailed (2026); its rapid reversal on de-escalation, with stored crude and products released; exceptionally high refining margins and their normalisation, including new refining capacity in Asia and the Middle East (2022-2023 and 2026 as precedents); an oil price collapse or OPEC+ market-share war (2014-2016, 2020); an LNG buyer's market as new US and Qatari liquefaction arrives (2027-2030); calm, low-volatility markets for trading (2023-2024); high inflation with high interest rates and weak consumer demand in the UK and Europe (2026-2027); windfall taxes on producers (UK Energy Profits Levy 2022-2030; the EU solidarity contribution 2022-2023). Inputs: Refining margin capture: Jefferies reported that BP captured 64% of global refining margin markers in the first half of 2026 against Shell's 55% and TotalEnergies' 35% (Jefferies, September 2026); how has BP's capture behaved when indicator margins normalised in the past, and is the gap between realised and indicator margins that BP cited in 2026 (about USD 5/bbl, on feedstock availability and freight) structural or cyclical? Which BP refineries (Whiting, Castellón, Rotterdam, Lingen, Kwinana and others) drive the capture, and how does complexity translate into margin across conditions? Trading: BP states that trading added on average about four percentage points to group return on average capital employed over 2020-2025 (Q2 2026 results presentation, 4 August 2026); how did BP's and peers' trading earnings behave in volatile years (2022, 2026) against calm years (2023, 2024), and is any volatility premium structural or cyclical as markets become more liquid and new trading entrants arrive? Upstream realisations: realisation lags (about one month in the Gulf of America and two months in the UAE), production-sharing contract entitlement effects in Angola, Azerbaijan, Egypt, Oman and Iraq (Rumaila), and how realised liquids prices tracked Brent in 2026 (BP realised USD 85.14/bbl in Q2 2026 against Brent of USD 103.85). LNG and gas: BP's contract book (about 23 mtpa of long-term supply plus about 10 mtpa of short-term and spot volumes), oil-indexed slopes and lags (gas contract prices often follow oil with about a nine-month lag), price-review and reopener clauses, and evidence of buyers renegotiating in 2015-2016 and 2020; bpx energy's gas realisations against Henry Hub. Retail fuels and convenience: margin behaviour and volume elasticity at high pump prices (2022, 2026) in the US and Europe, the role of loyalty programmes (earnify) and of TravelCenters of America, and the effect of exiting about 10% of company-owned sites by 2027. Windfall taxes as a cap on price capture: the UK Energy Profits Levy at a 78% headline marginal rate to March 2030 and its proposed successor, EU proposals, and their likelihood at Brent above USD 100. What the company, customers, competitors and the sell-side say now about how much of BP's 2026 downstream and trading earnings is repeatable. Focus: Whether BP's ability to earn above benchmark prices, through refining capture, trading and integration, persists when the 2026 dislocation reverses and markets calm, and how much of its 2026 downstream and trading outperformance the company and analysts regard as repeatable.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: BP's earnings, cash flow and distributions over 2027-2030 depend on several interacting loops (deleveraging feeding buybacks, working capital releasing as prices fall, high prices inviting taxes and supply, disposals shrinking the earnings base), so the second set needs evidence on which loops will dominate the path out of the 2026 windfall. Peers: Shell (buyback compounding: about a 19% share-count reduction over three years with at least USD 3bn of buybacks for nineteen consecutive quarters); TotalEnergies (organic growth with buybacks under a payout above 40% of cash flow); Eni (asset-sale-funded deleveraging through satellites and a rule-based payout); Equinor (Norwegian tax-lag cash dynamics that reverse as prices fall); ExxonMobil (programmatic buybacks across cycles); Chevron (buybacks scaled to a Brent range); Vitol (private; trading earnings across volatility regimes). Inputs: The deleveraging-to-buyback loop: BP moved its USD 14-18bn net debt target to end-2026 (Q2 2026 results, 4 August 2026), plans to cut its hybrid stack to about USD 9bn by end-2027, suspended buybacks in February 2026 and is expected to discuss distributions at a strategy event on 15 December 2026; how far does lower debt cut interest and hybrid costs and free cash for buybacks, how did the loop run at BP over 2020-2023 (net debt from USD 40.4bn in Q3 2020 to USD 20.9bn at end-2023) and at Shell over 2023-2026, and what stopped it? Working capital as a counter-cyclical buffer: BP's USD 6.0bn build in Q1 2026 (about USD 4.1bn seasonal inventory and transit volumes) and its guided USD 2-3bn release in the second half of 2026; dated releases in past price falls (2014-2015, 2020) at BP and peers. High prices leading to windfall taxes, demand destruction and supply response (OPEC+ decisions, non-OPEC growth), which then lower prices and earnings. Capital discipline across the majors leading to scarce oilfield-service capacity and cost inflation that absorbs savings. Disposals and depletion: BP's USD 20bn divestment programme (about USD 11bn done or signed by mid-2026), sales of steady earners (65% of Castrol to Stonepeak, with no income expected from the retained stake in the short to medium term according to BP), a proved reserve life reported at 7.1 years (Jefferies), and whether this forms a shrink-to-deleverage loop that requires further disposals or acquisitions. Credit-rating feedback (Fitch A+, stable, May 2026) in a downturn. Organisational feedback: repeated strategy changes and workforce reductions, staff engagement falling (64% in the latest pulse survey), talent attrition and plant reliability. Activist feedback: Elliott Management's stake of about 5% and its influence on disposals, costs and distributions. Distribution policies set as a share of cash flow across cycles at peers. Focus: Which loops will dominate BP's financial line items over 2027-2030, in particular whether the deleveraging-to-buyback loop and working-capital release can cushion a normalisation of oil prices and refining margins, and whether disposals and depletion form a balancing loop that erodes the earnings base.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Individual broker forecasts of BP's FY2027 and FY2028 earnings per share are very widely dispersed, and several inputs that drive them (finance costs, the retained Castrol stake, tax, perimeter changes, share count) are not settled by company guidance. Peers: Shell, TotalEnergies, Eni and Equinor (European integrated and upstream peers whose consensus bridges face the same price, refining, trading, tax and buyback inputs); ExxonMobil and Chevron (US majors with programmatic buybacks and published price sensitivities); Repsol (refining-heavy European peer); Stonepeak (private; buyer of 65% of Castrol, as the counterparty whose preferred distribution terms determine BP's income from its retained 35%). Inputs: The price deck: what Brent, refining margin and Henry Hub assumptions brokers use for 2027-2029, against BP's own planning assumptions in its Q2 2026 interim report (a short-term Brent assumption raised to USD 80 for 2026; a refining indicator margin baseline of USD 10.80/bbl after Gelsenkirchen; return targets set at USD 70 Brent, USD 4 Henry Hub and USD 10.30/bbl refining margin), and how much of the FactSet range of individual broker FY2028 EPS estimates (USD 0.21 to USD 0.93 as at 25 October 2026) the deck explains. Volumes for 2026-2029: underlying production guided broadly flat; ten major project start-ups with about 250 kboe/d of peak production through 2027 (Q2 2026 presentation); bpx energy at 545 kboe/d in Q2 2026; the UK North Sea sale (about 100 kboe/d); Middle East disruption of about 100 kboe/d in Q2 2026; plant reliability; Kaskida and Tiber timing. Refining capture and the trading contribution in normal against exceptional years. Perimeter: earnings lost with Castrol (enterprise value about USD 10.1bn for the 65% sale; BP expects no income or dividends from its 35% in the short to medium term), Gelsenkirchen (refining sensitivity cut from USD 550m to USD 450m per USD 1/bbl; Morgan Stanley trimmed FY2027-2030 EPS by about 3%), the North Sea and Archaea Energy. Finance costs: how net interest expense (USD 3.98bn in FY2025 on FactSet) will move as net debt falls toward BP's target, including lease interest (lease liabilities of USD 14.57bn at end-2025), the unwinding of discount on provisions and a weighted average cost of finance debt of about 5% with 29% floating (annual report 2025). Hybrids: the path to about USD 9bn by end-2027 and how hybrid coupons enter earnings per share. Tax: the underlying effective tax rate guided at 35-40% for 2026 and the effect of leaving the UK Energy Profits Levy regime. Gulf of America payments (about USD 1.1-1.6bn a year pre-tax), depreciation guided at USD 17.0-17.5bn, and the frequency of adjusting items and impairments. Share count: the timing and size of a buyback restart and the framework expected on 15 December 2026, the dividend growth commitment of at least 4% a year, and the difference between basic and diluted share counts. The flow-through of structural cost savings into underlying profit. Focus: Reconcile the wide range of broker FY2027-2028 earnings-per-share estimates for BP input by input, and identify which inputs (price deck and refining margins, trading, finance costs, tax, perimeter changes and share count) explain most of the dispersion and which are most likely to move materially either way.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 8 November 2026, 06:09 to 06:31 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
BP p.l.c. (BP-GB): second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Written 2026-11-08. Price 624.3p (2026-10-29), about $8.37 a share at the exchange rate implied by FactSet's capital-structure snapshot (638p = $8.55). All money in USD; "bn" is billions. "Underlying" means BP's underlying replacement cost (RC) profit attributable to BP shareholders. That is the brokers' adjusted basis: it is after hybrid coupons and minorities, and it excludes adjusting items and inventory holding effects. "EBITDA" and "EBIT" are on the brokers' adjusted basis unless I say otherwise. "RIM" is BP's refining indicator margin.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

What changed since the first set (read this first)

The first set was written on 2026-10-29, the day before BP's Q3 2026 results. Seven facts published since then change the model materially. All are from the AlphaSense reports of this set:

  1. Q3 2026 underlying profit was $8.46bn, against $5.73bn in Q2. It beat consensus by about 15% (Rothschild & Co Redburn, Morgan Stanley, 30-Oct-26). The drivers were a RIM of $46.70/bbl (Q2 $29.60), Brent of $96.99 (Q2 $103.85) and an underlying tax rate of 28% (Q2 34%). Nine-month underlying profit is therefore about $17.4bn.
  2. Net debt was $13.87bn at 30 September, against $22.25bn at 30 June. That is already below the $14-18bn end-2026 target, five quarters early.
  3. The Castrol sale closed on 2 November for $6bn of net proceeds, and BP sold 5% of Aker BP for $1.06bn on 5 November. Neither is in the Q3 net debt. On my estimate, end-2026 net debt will be about $6bn, not the $10bn the brokers expected before Q3.
  4. New targets: total financial obligations of $33-35bn by end-2026 (earlier $39-41bn), and hybrids cut from $12.1bn (Q3) to about $9bn by end-2027.
  5. Guidance changes: the 2026 underlying tax rate is cut to 31-34% (from 35-40%). Q4 2026 production is guided at 2,090-2,230 kboe/d, with weaker Q4 trading, refinery maintenance cutting throughput by about 200 kb/d, and only about $0.5bn of working capital left to release in 2026. About $2.8bn of the $6.5bn nine-month working capital build is "permanent" (Gulf of America payments $1.5bn, assets held for sale $0.8bn, decommissioning $0.5bn).
  6. Gulf of America: BP agreed with Louisiana to accelerate its Deepwater Horizon payments. It now names settling the remaining $5.9bn payable as the first call on excess cash, ahead of buybacks (Jefferies, Morgan Stanley, 30-Oct-26).
  7. Operations: upstream plant reliability recovered to 95.3% and refining availability to 96.5% in Q3, from 92.4% and 94.7% in Q2. Structural cost reductions reached $4.0bn, but upstream unit production cost rose to $6.54/boe (Q3 2025: $6.19). Q3 underlying operating expenditure was $5.26bn, against $5.49bn a year earlier. Post-tax impairments were a further $4.0bn, mainly Lightsource bp and offshore wind.

Where I disagree with the first set, in summary. (a) Its normalised net income of $7.5-9.5bn at BP's planning deck (centre $8.5bn) is too low. Bridged properly, I get about $9.4bn at $70 Brent and a $10.80 RIM. The first set left out three things: the roughly $1.5bn fall in depreciation (assets held for sale stop depreciating, and impairments shrink the asset base); the lower tax rate as the mix changes; and a fall in finance costs larger than it allowed, now that net debt is near $6bn. (b) Its view that consensus FY2028 is "10-20% too high at $70" is therefore overstated. At $70 consensus is about 7% high. At my base deck ($72 Brent, $12 RIM) consensus FY2028 net income of $10.1bn is about right. The disagreement with consensus is the price deck, not the arithmetic. (c) Its view that distributable cash after the dividend is only "about $0-2bn a year" at $70 is too low for 2028 onwards. By then finance costs are about $1.4bn lower, the Gulf of America payments ($1.1-1.6bn a year) have ended, and hybrid coupons are lower. I estimate about $4-5bn a year at $70, which funds a buyback of that size from a near-zero net debt base. (d) I agree with the first set on the central point: FY2026 is a price-and-margin peak, and operating profit fades by about half to FY2028.

A data trap to add to the first set's list. FactSet's historical "free cash flow" line deducts dividends paid. Check FY2025: operating cash flow $24,493m less capex $13,221m less dividends $5,059m gives $6,213m, the figure shown. The same arithmetic holds for every year from FY2022 to FY2025. FactSet's consensus free cash flow does not deduct dividends: FY2026 consensus operating cash flow of $38.8bn less capex of $13.7bn is about $24.7bn. The first set's table in C-67 ("FCF 6.2 in FY2025, 24.7 in FY2026") therefore compares two different definitions. On the like-for-like basis (operating cash flow less capex), FY2025 free cash flow was $11.3bn.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Four conventions the model's user must know. Without them, three of the input lines will look wrong.

  1. Net financial items combine two things: BP's true net finance cost ($3.6bn, $2.6bn, $2.55bn, $2.65bn and $2.8bn for FY2026-30), and the pre-tax equivalent of hybrid coupons and other minority interests (post-tax $1.16bn, $0.95bn, $0.87bn, $0.87bn and $0.87bn, divided by one minus the tax rate). The model has no minorities line. This convention makes the model's net income equal underlying RC profit attributable to BP shareholders (the brokers' basis), and it makes the model's free cash flow fall after hybrid coupons. True net finance cost is the figure to compare with consensus EBIT less pre-tax profit ($4.1bn, $2.7bn, $2.1bn).
  2. Working capital (% of revenue) carries two things against the FY2025 year-end balance (FactSet net working capital -$13,237m, -7.0% of revenue). (a) BP's own working-capital movement, which includes Gulf of America settlement payments and decommissioning: a build of about $6.0bn in FY2026, a net build of about $1.9bn in FY2027 (a price-driven release of about $3.0bn less a Gulf of America settlement of about $4.9bn), releases of about $1.2bn in FY2028 and $0.3bn in FY2029, then flat. (b) About $1.5bn (FY2026) and $2.5bn a year (FY2027-30) of other operating cash items that sit between brokers' EBITDA and operating cash flow: equity-accounted income above dividends received, provisions, restructuring cash and cash-tax timing, net of the non-cash unwinding of discount included in net financial items. On BP's own basis alone, year-end working capital would be about -3.0%, -2.6%, -3.6%, -3.8% and -3.8% of revenue. The rising percentage in my inputs is therefore not a forecast of a working-capital build.
  3. Lease payments are the principal portion only. Lease interest (about $0.7bn) is in net financial items. Total lease cash is about $2.2bn a year.
  4. Acquisitions less disposals include hybrid redemptions, which are equity outflows that are not share buybacks. Gross figures: FY2026 disposals +$8.5bn, hybrids -$3.9bn, acquisitions -$0.3bn. FY2027 disposals +$4.0bn, hybrids -$3.1bn, acquisitions -$0.5bn. FY2028 +$1.5bn and -$0.5bn. FY2029-30 +$1.0bn and -$0.5bn a year.

The change in share count for FY2026 (+180m) reconciles FactSet's FY2025 count (15,376.7m) to BP's Q3 2026 weighted basic count (15.56bn), the basis brokers' EPS uses. There were no buybacks in 2026. From 2027, +50m a year is issuance to employee share plans.

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

BP reports three segments in 2026 (Gas & Low Carbon Energy; Oil Production & Operations; Customers & Products). From 1 January 2027 it reports two: Upstream, which includes gas and power trading, and Downstream, which includes oil trading and the retained 35% of Castrol. Restated history is due in early 2027. My estimates below are on the new basis. EBIT is underlying RC profit before interest and tax. Revenue includes some inter-segment sales, so these are approximations.

Upstream EBIT margin about 48% about 54% about 50%
Downstream revenue ($bn) about 199 about 166 about 152
Downstream EBIT ($bn) about 20.5 about 7.2 about 4.5
Downstream EBIT margin about 10% about 4% about 3%

Reported 2026 segment EBIT (old basis, $bn):

  • Q1: Customers & Products 3.20, Gas & Low Carbon 1.34, Oil Production & Operations 1.98.
  • Q2: 4.95, 2.12 and 3.58.
  • Q3: not split in my sources. Group underlying profit was $8.46bn, led by Customers & Products on a RIM of $46.70.

Downstream carries the FY2026-28 fade: refining normalisation, trading back to normal, Castrol and Gelsenkirchen gone. Upstream fades with Brent and the North Sea exit (Q4 2027 assumed), partly offset by lower depreciation, start-ups and the oil-indexed LNG lag in H1 2027. FactSet's consensus segment revenue (Upstream $44.3bn, $40.9bn, $34.3bn; Downstream $203.2bn, $155.1bn, $130.4bn) has the same shape.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Price deck:
  • Brent: $93.5 (FY2026; Q1 $81, Q2 $104, Q3 $97, Q4 about $95), $80 (FY2027; H1 $85, H2 $75), $72 (FY2028), $70 (FY2029-30, nominal).
  • Refining indicator margin: about $31 (FY2026; Q3 $46.70, Q4 about $30-35), $17, $12, $11 and $11.
  • Henry Hub: about $3.3, $3.5, $3.75, $3.75 and $3.75.
  • The rationale: the Hormuz disruption eases through 2027, with restocking of the 507m barrel inventory draw slowing the fall, and refining capacity and Gulf restarts bring the RIM back to about $12 by late 2028 (Morgan Stanley's view of peer indicators).
  • Sensitivities applied (BP rules of thumb, after Gelsenkirchen): $340m of pre-tax profit per $1/bbl Brent, $450m per $1/bbl RIM, $40m per $0.10 Henry Hub.
  • Volumes: reported production of about 2,220 kboe/d (FY2026), 2,190, 2,150, 2,200 and 2,280. North Sea (about 110 kboe/d) out from Q4 2027. Iraq disruption easing in 2027. Kaskida from mid-2029, Tiber in 2030. Plant reliability 95%. Refining throughput about 1.29-1.30 mb/d after Gelsenkirchen, with capture falling from about 60% to about 50% of the indicator by 2028.
  • Trading: about $1.7bn above a normal year in FY2026, $0.8bn above in FY2027, normal from FY2028.
  • Perimeter EBITDA removed: Castrol about $1.0bn a year (from November 2026; no income from the retained 35%), Gelsenkirchen about $0.3bn (from August 2026), North Sea about $1.3bn (from Q4 2027), Aker BP equity income about $0.1bn (from November 2026).
  • Costs: net efficiency reaching EBITDA, against FY2025, of 0 (FY2026), +$0.4bn, +$0.8bn, +$0.9bn and +$0.9bn. That is the gross programme less 2.5% a year inflation and activity choices. Underlying operating cost about $20.8bn, $19.0bn and $18.3bn (FY2026-28).
  • Capex: $13.75bn (FY2026), then $14.0-14.5bn, at the top of the $13-15bn frame because of service-cost inflation.
  • Below EBITDA: depreciation falls to $16.6bn by FY2028 (held-for-sale assets, impairments, disposals), then rises with Kaskida and Tiber. Net finance cost falls with net debt (about $6bn at end-2026). Tax rate 32.5% in FY2026, rising to 37% as the downstream share normalises, about 2 points lower than history after the UK exit. Hybrids $12.1bn to $9bn during 2027.
  • Distributions: the dividend rises 4% a year (BP's minimum commitment). Buybacks restart in February 2027 at about $1.5bn a quarter ($6.0bn in FY2027), then $5.5bn and $4.5bn a year, sized so that net debt stays below about $10bn. The 15 December 2026 strategy event will set the actual framework.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Profit.
  • Start from IFRS profit attributable to BP shareholders. Remove inventory holding gains and losses to get the RC basis, then add back adjusting items to get underlying RC profit attributable to BP shareholders. Adjusting items are impairments (Q3 2026: $4.0bn post-tax), disposal gains and losses, restructuring, environmental and legal items, and fair-value effects on LNG hedges (BP marks the hedges to market but not the underlying contracts).
  • Underlying RC profit is after hybrid coupons and minorities. That is the brokers' adjusted net income.
  • Gap examples: FY2025 IFRS $55m against underlying $7.49bn; Q2 2026 underlying above IFRS by $1.8bn.
  • EPS. Underlying RC profit divided by the weighted basic share count (15.56bn in Q3 2026; diluted 15.71bn). FactSet's "EPS, brokers' basis" (FY2025: $0.480) is on this basis. FactSet's reported EPS ($0.004) and adjusted diluted EPS ($0.166) are not.
  • EBITDA. Brokers' EBITDA is about underlying RC profit before interest and tax plus depreciation (FY2025 $36.6bn). FactSet's operating EBITDA ($31.3bn) is 17% lower and is not comparable. My margins are on the brokers' basis.
  • Revenue. FactSet revenue equals BP's sales and other operating revenues ($189.3bn in FY2025). Some reports quote $148.8bn, which is Customers & Products third-party sales only.
  • Net debt. BP's figure excludes leases ($22.18bn end-2025; $13.87bn Q3 2026). FactSet's includes leases (about $14.6bn more) and was $35.5bn at Q2 2026. Hybrids are equity in both.
  • Buybacks. FactSet's 2026 year-to-date "share buybacks" line (-$3.4bn) is mainly the €2.5bn hybrid redemption. BP bought back no shares in 2026.
  • Free cash flow. FactSet's history line deducts dividends: FY2025 $6.2bn = operating cash flow $24.5bn less capex $13.2bn less dividends $5.1bn. FactSet's consensus free cash flow does not deduct dividends (FY2026 $24.7bn is about OCF $38.8bn less capex $13.7bn). On the like-for-like basis, FY2025 was $11.3bn. BP's "adjusted free cash flow" and "price-adjusted free cash flow" are company definitions; the latter is restated to $70 Brent.

Your cross-check: your own earnings per share and free cash flow for each of the five fiscal years

EPS is underlying RC profit attributable to BP shareholders divided by weighted basic shares (15.56bn, 15.27bn, 14.54bn, 13.92bn, 13.37bn), on the brokers' basis. Free cash flow is operating cash flow less capex, less lease principal, after hybrid coupons and minorities, and before dividends, disposals, hybrid redemptions and buybacks. It is built the same way the model builds it from the inputs above. On the simpler operating cash flow less capex basis it is $19.9bn, $13.3bn, $12.0bn, $10.3bn and $10.1bn.

Cross-check FY2026 (year to 31-Dec-26) FY2027 (year to 31-Dec-27) FY2028 (year to 31-Dec-28) FY2029 (year to 31-Dec-29) FY2030 (year to 31-Dec-30)
Earnings per share 1.46 0.90 0.67 0.66 0.67

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: BP p.l.c. (BP-GB), the London-listed integrated energy major. In 2026 it reports Gas & Low Carbon Energy, Oil Production & Operations and Customers & Products. From 1 January 2027 it reports two segments. Upstream covers oil and gas production including bpx energy in US onshore, LNG, and gas and power trading. Downstream covers refining, oil and products trading, fuels marketing and convenience retail, aviation fuels, biofuels and the retained 35% of Castrol.

Request: T-D1

Peers: Shell (the closest integrated peer with a large trading arm; its 2023-26 turnaround under Wael Sawan is the precedent for cost cuts plus compounding buybacks). TotalEnergies (lowest upstream unit cost and organic growth; the benchmark for operational delivery). Eni (asset-sale-funded deleveraging through satellites and a rule-based payout). Equinor (flat-volume European peer). ExxonMobil and Chevron (structural cost programmes delivered early; Chevron cut its business units from 14 to 5). Repsol (refining-heavy integrated peer). Woodside Energy (Meg O'Neill's previous company, for her record on cost, projects and integration). Vitol and Trafigura (private; trading earnings across volatility regimes). Operating conditions: the 2026 Strait of Hormuz disruption and its reversal on reopening. Exceptional refining margins and their normalisation as Gulf refineries restart and new Asian and Middle East capacity arrives. An OPEC+ market-share phase (2014-16 and 2020 precedents). The 2027-2030 LNG supply wave. Calm, low-volatility trading markets (2023-24). Persistent oilfield-service, rig and subsea cost inflation through 2028. UK and EU windfall taxes. Inputs:

  • Costs and cash returns:
  • Can BP turn its gross structural cost reductions into lower absolute operating cost and higher underlying profit? It reported $4.0bn of reductions at Q3 2026 against a 2023 baseline of $22.6bn, with a target of $6.5-7.5bn by end-2027 that includes about $2.5bn from the Castrol and Gelsenkirchen disposals (Morgan Stanley, 22-Sep-26). Q3 2026 underlying operating expenditure was $5.26bn against $5.49bn a year earlier, and upstream unit cost was $6.54/boe against $6.19/boe. The CFO cited $3.0bn of offsets from inflation, acquired assets and activity choices (2-Nov-26).
  • Has any oil major converted a mostly gross, baseline-relative cost target into a net reduction in absolute cost? How long did it take?
  • How plausible is a sustained buyback programme from 2027? BP's 2020-23 deleveraging ($40.4bn to $20.9bn of net debt) was followed by a buyback suspension in February 2026. Net debt was $13.87bn at Q3 2026, and Castrol closed on 2 November 2026 for $6bn. What distinguished Shell's 19-quarter buyback run from BP's earlier one?
  • Operations:
  • Can BP hold upstream plant reliability at about 95% while cutting its workforce by over 5%? Reliability was 96.1% in 2025, 92.4% in Q2 2026 and 95.3% in Q3 2026. Tier 1 and 2 process safety events were 18 in Q2 2026 against 5 a year earlier.
  • How often have majors delivered deepwater Gulf of America projects on schedule in today's supply chain? This bears on Kaskida (35% complete, 2029 start-up) and Tiber (10% complete, 2030).
  • Do BP's refining capture (64% of indicator margins in H1 2026, against Shell 55% and TotalEnergies 35%, per Jefferies) and its trading uplift (about 4 points of ROACE on average over 2020-25, per BP) persist in calm markets?
  • Targets and execution capacity:
  • Is BP's target of ROACE above 16% by 2027 at $70 Brent plausible on a smaller post-disposal capital base?
  • How has a new external chief executive with eight concurrent change programmes performed elsewhere? The programmes here are the segment restructure, disposals, costs, deleveraging, hybrids, the buyback restart, the Bumerangue appraisal and the North Sea exit. Focus: For each change now expected at BP, does it rest on strengths the company has demonstrated, or on strengths it would need to acquire? The changes are net cost reduction reaching profit, reliability, deepwater start-ups, refining capture and trading in calm markets, and a buyback sustained through a price downturn. What does the precedent record say about each?

Request: T-D2

Peers: Shell (integrated trading and refining; the closest comparison for earnings volatility). TotalEnergies (largest European refining capacity; a gas trading loss in Q2 2026 as a contrast). Eni, Equinor and Repsol (European peers with different mixes of refining, gas and upstream). ExxonMobil and Chevron (larger, lower-gearing US majors with published sensitivities). Valero and Marathon Petroleum (US independent refiners, for how fast refining margins normalise). Vitol (private; trading earnings across regimes). Operating conditions: the Strait of Hormuz disruption (2026) and a rapid reopening with stranded crude and products released. Refining margin spikes and their unwinding (2022-23 and 2026). Oil price collapses (2014-16, 2020). An OPEC+ market-share decision. The LNG buyer's market of 2027-2030. Calm trading markets. A new European windfall levy at Brent above $100. Inputs:

  • What drives BP's earnings:
  • How much of the change in BP's earnings from 2025 to 2026, and the expected change to 2028, comes from each driver? The drivers are the refining indicator margin ($11.90/bbl in Q2 2025, $29.60 in Q2 2026, $46.70 in Q3 2026, against a planning baseline of $10.80), Brent ($69 average in 2025; $103.85 in Q2 2026; $96.99 in Q3 2026), trading, perimeter changes (Castrol, Gelsenkirchen, the UK North Sea), finance costs, tax and share count?
  • BP's published sensitivities are $340m of pre-tax profit per $1/bbl Brent and $450m per $1/bbl of refining margin after Gelsenkirchen. Is the earnings change concentrated in one or two of these drivers?
  • How fast margins normalise:
  • After past supply disruptions ended (for example 1990-91, 2022) and after past refining margin spikes, how fast did refining margins and diesel cracks normalise, and how did BP's and peers' earnings follow?
  • How fast would stranded Gulf crude and product cargoes, Middle East refinery restarts, and the rebuilding of about 507m barrels of inventories drawn since February 2026 move prices in either direction?
  • The width of the range:
  • What explains the range of individual broker FY2028 EPS estimates for BP of $0.21 to $0.93 (FactSet, 25-Oct-26) and FY2027 of $0.54 to $1.29? How much is price deck and how much is other assumptions?
  • How often have forecasts resting on a refining margin far above its average been met, at BP and at peers?
  • Tail events and cushions:
  • What are the size and frequency of BP's tail events: refinery outages (Whiting 2024 and 2026, Rotterdam October 2026), large trading losses, and windfall levies (EU solidarity contribution of $505m in 2022)?
  • How did working-capital releases cushion BP's and peers' cash in past price falls (BP 2014, 2020; Shell 2014, 2020)? Focus: How wide and how skewed the range of BP's earnings and cash flow is over the next 18 months. How concentrated the difference from the last four to six years is in refining margins and Brent rather than in company-controlled lines. How quickly earnings fell back after comparable disruptions ended.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Shell, TotalEnergies, Eni, Equinor and Repsol (European integrated peers reporting Q3 2026 results and setting 2027 distributions). ExxonMobil and Chevron (programmatic buybacks; ExxonMobil announced a $20bn repurchase pace for 2027). Woodside Energy (the guidance style of BP's chief executive at her previous company). Operating conditions: the Hormuz disruption and its reopening. Refining margin normalisation. A Brent path from about $97-111 (Q3 to October 2026) toward company planning prices of $70-80. The 2027 LNG supply wave. Higher-for-longer interest rates. A new UK or EU windfall levy. Inputs:

  • Current guidance and the record:
  • How likely is BP to beat, meet or miss each guided item? They are: 2026 underlying tax rate of 31-34% (cut from 35-40% on 30-Oct-26); 2026 capex of $13.5-14.0bn; depreciation of $17.0-17.5bn; net debt below the $14-18bn target ($13.87bn at Q3); total financial obligations of $33-35bn by end-2026; hybrids of about $9bn by end-2027; operating cost of about $18bn in 2027; structural cost reductions of $6.5-7.5bn by end-2027; ROACE above 16% in 2027 at $70 Brent; Q4 2026 production of 2,090-2,230 kboe/d with weaker trading and refinery maintenance.

  • Where consensus sits:
  • Where does consensus sit against post-Q3 broker revisions? FactSet median EPS at 25-Oct-26 was $1.37, $0.856 and $0.670 for FY2026-28. Rothschild & Co Redburn has $1.43, $0.78 and $0.60 (30-Oct-26). Morgan Stanley assumes buybacks of $0.75bn a quarter from Q3 2027 and Brent of $86.20 in 2027 and $75 in 2028 (5-Nov-26). Net FY2027 EPS revisions were +14% over one month and +35% over three (FactSet, 29-Oct-26).

  • Read-across from peers:

Focus: Whether the 15 December 2026 framework and the Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 results are more likely to lift or cut consensus FY2027-28 EPS and the net breadth of revisions. Which way the per-share lines (buybacks, share count) and the operating lines (refining margins, trading, costs) are each likely to move.

Request: T-I2

Peers: Shell, TotalEnergies, Eni, Equinor, Repsol, ExxonMobil and Chevron (the peer set investors compare BP against). Elliott Management (activist shareholder of about 5%; its public demands are part of the narrative). Stonepeak (private; owner of 65% of Castrol since 2 November 2026). Operating conditions: the Hormuz disruption and its reopening. Refining margin normalisation. The 2027-2030 LNG supply wave. A risk-off equity regime that penalises complex, cyclical cases. Possible sector consolidation. Inputs: how widely discussed is each of these dynamics?

  • Balance sheet and distributions:
  • BP's net debt fell below target five quarters early ($13.87bn at Q3 2026, before the $6bn Castrol proceeds and $1.06bn from the Aker BP stake sale), which creates capacity for buybacks.
  • The finance-cost floor: lease interest and the unwinding of discount on provisions do not fall as net debt falls.

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 8 November 2026, 22:54 to 23:10 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
BP p.l.c. (BP-GB): third and fourth sets of questions. The predictability of the forecasts, and how far guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect our assessment

Written 2026-11-08. Price 624.3p (29 October 2026, the dashboard); the model's price is 638p (25 October). All money is in USD; "bn" is billions. "Underlying" means BP's underlying replacement cost profit attributable to BP shareholders, the brokers' basis. "RIM" is BP's refining indicator margin. "The first set" and "the second set" are the earlier answers; "the model" is the Financial Analyst's model after update 1; "our base" is the second set's base case as the model calculates it.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Read this first: what the new evidence adds, where I disagree with the earlier sets, and the model's flagged gaps

Five facts in the AlphaSense reports that the second set did not have, or did not weigh enough.

  1. The deck has already started to move. Front-month Brent fell about 16% in the three trading days to 4 November 2026 (Morgan Stanley, 5 November; the tenth-largest three-day fall in the history of the Brent contract) on hopes of a US-Iran agreement, while the Strait of Hormuz stayed closed (Iranian statements, 8 November) and tanker fires were reported in the Strait in early November. The 2027 curve stayed high: the December 2027 Brent future was about $88 (Morgan Stanley, 27 October) and the 2027 Brent swap $92.41 (Rabobank, 4 November). Morgan Stanley's 5 November note is titled "Longer disruption priced out" and brings its $75 Brent forward to Q4 2027. The lesson of D-04 arrived before I wrote it: the deck re-prices in days, and earnings follow it over quarters.
  2. The refining curve sits well above our path. Forward refining margins trade at about three times their historical average for December 2027 and twice for December 2028 (Morgan Stanley, 27 October). Morgan Stanley models European margins of $25 (2027) and $14 (2028), and $27.7 and $16.8 for BP's indicator; Redburn models a faster return to $12. Supporting the curve: planned refinery maintenance of 3.4 to 6.4 mb/d by mid-2027, Saudi and Kuwaiti refineries with no restart timetable, European diesel stocks near five-year lows and a record jet yield. Against it: Singapore gross margins already fell from above $30 (April) to $9 (October 2026). The second set's RIM of $17 (2027) and $12 (2028) is below the curve and below Morgan Stanley.
  3. Castrol's income is gone, not deferred. The $6bn of net proceeds includes about $0.8bn of prepaid future dividends (Edison, 2 November), the retained 35% is locked up for two years, and Stonepeak holds a preference on distributions. The second set's "no Castrol income in the short to medium term" is structural.
  4. The Gulf of America acceleration is smaller and vaguer than the second set's $4.9bn settlement in 2027 implies. The Louisiana agreement is a $350m lump sum replacing $373m due over seven years; BP's guided pre-tax payments are about $1.6bn (2026) and $1.2bn (2027); management "gave limited detail" on how the remaining $5.9bn payable will be settled in full, whether state by state or through a vehicle (Redburn, 30 October); a February 2026 statement had the liabilities "largely settled by end-2032". The cash leaves either way, to the payable or to buybacks, so EPS is barely affected by the timing, but the second set's 2027 net debt path depends on it.
  5. The December framework is likely to be more measured than the second set assumes. The most constructive covering broker, Morgan Stanley, assumes buybacks of $0.75bn a quarter from Q3 2027 (about $1.5bn in 2027; a 2027 buyback yield of 1.3% against Shell's 6.7%); Redburn (13 October) says BP "should not resume buybacks yet"; Woodside, the chief executive's previous company, set a 20-40% of adjusted operating cash flow framework on 5 November 2026; BP's retired framework was 30-40%; Shell's is 40-50%. The second set's $6bn from February 2027 is above every broker and at the top of any share-of-cash-flow framework.

Where I disagree with the second set. (a) Its FY2027 buyback of $6bn is too high for the evidence on management's intent; I lower it to $4.5bn (the change and its reasons are at the end of the fourth set and in inputs-changed.md). (b) Its RIM path for 2027 is below the forward curve and below Morgan Stanley. I keep it, because the skew argument in D-04 says our base should sit below the curve on the most reversible line, but the Consumer should know that this is why our FY2027 EBITDA is 4% below consensus, and that it is a judgement, not a factual disagreement. (c) It is "less concerned than the first set" about reliability after Q3's recovery to 95.3%; the process-safety data (Tier 1 and 2 events of 18 in Q2 2026 against 5 a year earlier) and the Rotterdam outage in October 2026 mean the first set's caution should stand. The model's 95% reliability is neutral, so no input changes. (d) Its $0.8bn of net cost efficiency reaching FY2028 EBITDA is a strength BP has not demonstrated (D-02); I keep it because it is small and offset by the second set's 5% downward lean, but it is the least-founded operating input.

Where I disagree with the first set. Its normalised earnings range ($7.5-9.5bn at $70) was superseded by the second set's bridge, which I accept, with one caveat the second set did not make: about $1.7bn of the second set's uplift is lower depreciation from impairments and held-for-sale accounting. That raises EPS without raising cash. On a cash basis the two sets are closer than their EPS figures suggest.

The model's flagged gaps, explained (FY2026 EPS 1.46 against 1.36; FY2029 0.66 against 0.63; FY2030 0.67 against 0.64).

  • FY2026. The model's pre-tax profit ($33,627m) is the second set's. Its net income is $21,439m against the second set's $22.7bn, which implies a tax charge of 36.2% of pre-tax profit against the 32.5% input. The most likely cause is that the two reported quarters of 2026 (Q1 and Q2) carry FactSet's reported tax charge, which includes tax effects of adjusting items (FactSet's reported FY2025 tax rate is 90.5%), while the input applies only to the forecast quarters. The remaining gap is the share count: $21,439m divided by $1.36 implies about 15.76bn shares against BP's Q3 2026 weighted basic count of 15.56bn, which the second set used. Neither shows an input error: 32.5% matches the guided 31-34% and the nine-month actual of 31%, and 15.56bn is BP's own count. I change nothing. The Financial Analyst should check whether the reported H1 2026 quarters carry FactSet's reported tax charge rather than the underlying charge, and which share count the model uses for FY2026 and why it is about 200m above BP's Q3 weighted basic count. For the memo, FY2026 underlying EPS of about $1.44-1.46 (nine-month actual of $17.4bn plus a Q4 of about $5.5bn, over 15.56bn shares) is the better figure.
  • FY2029 and FY2030. Net income is identical in both ($9,127m against about $9.1bn; $9,003m against about $9.0bn). The whole gap is the share count: the model has 14.49bn (2029) and 14.07bn (2030) against the second set's 13.92bn and 13.37bn. The model retires fewer shares per dollar of buyback than the second set, which assumed $7.5-8.0 a share; the model evidently buys at a higher price (today's $8.4-8.5, or its own rising value per share). If the shares hold or re-rate, the model is right and the second set's count is too low. The dollar amount of buybacks is the decision BP takes and the count is its result, so I keep the dollar inputs and the Consumer should treat the model's EPS of $0.63-0.65 for FY2028-30 as the forecasts of record. That puts our FY2028 about 3% below consensus ($0.67) on a deck ($72) close to consensus's. The Financial Analyst should state the share price at which the model converts buybacks into shares retired.

The third set: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's heading (Layer 4) The fourth set: Assessing the extent to which our assessment is reflected in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative
The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Changes to the forecast inputs

One input changes. The FY2027 buyback falls from $6,000m to $4,500m, for the reasons in I-01: management's stated priorities (resilience, the Gulf of America settlement first), the brokers' assumptions ($1.5bn at Morgan Stanley; none yet at Redburn), the peer and Woodside frameworks expressed as 30-45% of cash flow, which on our FY2027 cash flow imply buybacks of about $3-6.5bn, and the £1.25bn hybrid call and the Gulf payable competing for the same cash in 2027. The effect is small: FY2027 average shares about 80m higher, FY2027 EPS about 0.3% lower, end-2027 net debt about $1.5bn lower, and about 1% fewer shares retired by end-2030 unless later years are raised. I leave FY2028-30 unchanged because a framework at full pace from 2028, with the Gulf payable settled and the hybrid calls done, supports them, and because the model's slower share-count decline already makes our EPS more conservative than the second set's. The change is written in full in inputs-changed.md.

Appendix The share-price co-movement table the Investment Analyst was given

Co-movement of BP-GB with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-11-06 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: BP p.l.c. (BP-GB), the London-listed integrated energy major; in 2026 it reports Gas & Low Carbon Energy, Oil Production & Operations and Customers & Products, and from 1 January 2027 it reports two segments, Upstream (oil and gas production including bpx energy, LNG, and gas and power trading) and Downstream (refining, oil and products trading, fuels marketing and convenience retail, aviation fuels, biofuels and the retained 35% of Castrol).

Request: OWN-1

Question: What have BP p.l.c.'s supply, trading and shipping activities and its refining margin capture earned in calm, low-volatility markets, as against dislocated ones? BP discloses no trading profit, but has said trading added about four percentage points to its return on average capital employed on average over 2020-25, and Jefferies estimated its refining capture at 64% of indicator margins in the first half of 2026 against Shell's 55% and TotalEnergies' 35%. For 2015-2019 and 2023-2024, what do broker estimates, expert calls with former BP and Shell trading staff, and BP's own segment disclosures indicate about the trading contribution to Customers & Products and Gas & Low Carbon Energy profit, and about realised refining margins against BP's refining indicator margin by quarter, including the Whiting (2024, 2026) and Rotterdam (October 2026) outages? After the Gelsenkirchen sale (completed 31 July 2026) and with the refining indicator margin at $46.70/bbl in Q3 2026 against BP's planning baseline of $10.80/bbl, what capture rate and trading contribution have Shell, TotalEnergies, Repsol, Valero and Marathon Petroleum shown at indicator margins of $10-14/bbl, and what does that say about BP's downstream earnings at mid-cycle margins?

Request: OWN-2

Question: How do investors, as distinct from sell-side analysts, currently describe and position in BP p.l.c.: what do hedge-fund and long-only commentary, investor letters, Twitter and other social media, 13F and UK shareholder-register changes, short interest and options positioning say about why they own or avoid BP, what they expect from the 15 December 2026 strategy event in New York (the buyback size and trigger, a payout as a share of cash flow, net debt guardrails, new medium-term targets), and how they compare BP with Shell, TotalEnergies, Eni, Equinor and Repsol? Include Elliott Management (which disclosed a stake of about 5% in 2025 and has called publicly for cost cuts, disposals and $20bn of annual free cash flow by 2027) and any evidence on whether its stake has changed. If the 15 December 2026 event has taken place by the time of this research, cover what BP announced and how the share price, the sell-side and investors reacted in the following days.

Request: OWN-3

Question: How have the share prices and forward price-to-earnings multiples of BP p.l.c. and of Shell, TotalEnergies, Eni, Equinor, Repsol, ExxonMobil and Chevron behaved in the six to eighteen months after past peaks in oil prices and refining margins, in 2008-09, 2011-14, 2014-16 and 2022-23: did forward multiples compress or expand as consensus earnings fell, how far did the shares fall from peak to trough and over what period, how did companies that were raising buybacks at the peak fare against those that were deleveraging, and how did BP's multiple relative to its peers behave through each unwind? What do those precedents indicate for the position of BP's shares in the current cycle, with Brent at about $97 in Q3 2026 and BP's refining indicator margin at $46.70/bbl in that quarter, BP's 24-month-forward price-to-earnings multiple near the top of its five-year range in October 2026, and most European integrated majors trading near the top of their own ten-year ranges of price-to-earnings multiples?
